\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{X}{\rho})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}}

commutes.

Remark 2.3.5
.

It may seem strange at first glance to use the transition isomorphisms
\tau_{f}^{A}
in place of the
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
in our explict description of
\PC(F)
. This is done primarily so that we can leverage our geometric intuition in more straightforward ways as well as make more direct comparisons with
[
6
]
,
[
38
]
, and other works which use the equivariant derived category; cf. also Propositions
2.3.10
and
2.3.11
below. The main reasoning behind this discrepancy between the transition isomorphism/pseudocone direction (namely in defining
\tau_{f}^{A}=\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}
) is because the equivariant derived categories were developed with morphisms going in the geometric direction while the definition of pseudonatural transformations go in the algebraic direction. We have made the decision to stick with the geometric direction of morphisms so as to make the comparisons and applications to geometric and topological situations that motivate us more straightforward and immediate.

Remark 2.3.6
.

It is of general categorical interest in to drop the “pseudo” assumption in our pseudocone definitions and instead work with lax cones. If we do this we cannot use the trick where we chose
\tau_{f}=\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}
as, of course, the witness morphisms in lax cones need not be invertible. Instead we simply must choose a convention when working with a category
\LC(F)
of lax cones of
F
. In this monograph we choose to work with our witness morphisms working in the same direction as the pseudocone category (and so move in the geometric direction and not in the algebraic direction).

In light of the remark above, we give an explicit definition of the category of lax cones of a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. While we will not work seriously with the category of lax cones in this monograph, it is relevant in locally to this section: the description of lax limits of
F
and sections of the elements fibration rely on the category of lax cones.

Definition 2.3.7
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. The category of lax cones with apex
\One
is defined to be

\LC(F):=\Bicat_{\operatorname{lax}}\left(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat\right)(\cnst(\One),F)

the category of lax natural transformations from
\cnst(\One)
to
F
.

Remark 2.3.8
.

As with
\PC(F)
, we can give a more explicit description of
\LC(F)
. It is given as follows:

•

Objects: Pairs
(A,T_{A})
where

A=\{\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{A}\in F(X)_{0}\}

is a collection of objects and transition morphisms

T_{A}=\{\tau_{f}^{A}:F(F)(\,_{Y}{A})\xrightarrow{}\,_{X}{A}\;|\;f:X\to Y,f\in\Cscr_{1}\}

which satisfy the cocycle condition

\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\left(\tau_{g}^{A}\right)

for any composable morphisms
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
.

•

Morphisms: A morphism
P:(A,T_{A})\to(B,T_{B})
is a collection of morphisms

P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{B}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

such that for any morphisms
f:X\to Y
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(F)(\,_{X}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{X}{\rho})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}}

commutes.

For those familiar with the Grothendieck construction of a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
, the categories
\LC(F)
and
\PC(F)
should look familiar by virtue of a comparison with the categories of elements of
F
; in Propositions
2.3.10
and
2.3.11
we make this comparison precise. Let us recall this construction; for an alternative presentation, see
[
62
]
. Fix a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. The Grothendieck construction gives an equivalence (and in fact a
2
-equivalence of
2
-categories) between pseudofunctors
\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and cloven fibrations
\Escr\to\Cscr
. This allows us to view the pseudofunctor
F
as a fibration
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
where
\El(F)
is the category of elements of
F
. The category
\El(F)
of elements of
F
is defined as follows:

•

Objects: Pairs
(X,A)
where
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and
A\in F(X)_{0}
.

•

Morphisms: A map
f:(X,A)\to(Y,B)
is a pair
(f_{0},f_{1})
where
f_{0}:X\to Y
is a morphism in
\Cscr
and
f_{1}:F(f_{0})(B)\to A
is a morphism in
F(X)
.

•

Composition: Given morphisms
(X,A)\xrightarrow{(f_{0},f_{1})}(Y,B)\xrightarrow{(g_{0},g_{1})}(Z,C)
the composite
(g_{0},g_{1})\circ(f_{0},f_{1})
is defined by setting

(g_{0},g_{1})\circ(f_{0},f_{1}):=(g_{0}\circ f_{0},f_{1}\circ F(f_{0})(g_{1})\circ\phi_{f_{0},g_{0}}^{-1});

note that the second morphism in the pair types correctly because it takes the form

F(g_{0}\circ f_{0})(C)\xrightarrow{\phi_{f_{0},g_{0}}^{-1}}F(f_{0})\left(F(g_{0})(C)\right)\xrightarrow{F(f_{0})(g_{1})}F(f_{0})(B)\xrightarrow{f_{1}}A.

•

Identities: The identity on
(X,A)
is given by
(\id_{X},\id_{A})
.

Note that for the identities to have the form expressed above we used that our psuedofunctors have been assumed to be normalized. If we instead have that
F(\id_{X})\neq\id_{FX}
we need to redefine
\id_{A}
and use the unitor isomorphism
\phi_{A}:F(\id_{X})(A)\to A
in its place.

The category
\El(F)
comes naturally equipped with a projection
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
given by, simply, first projection. This functor is a fibration and gives one half of the equivalence between cloven fibrations and psuedofunctors. The other equivalence, which we will not use here, determines a pseudofunctor
F
from a fibration
p:\Escr\to\Cscr
by taking
F(X):=p^{-1}(\id_{X})
and then defining the translation functors
F(f)
by taking Cartesian lifts which lie within a given cleavage; cf.
[
62
]
for details.

What is of interest to us lies in giving sections to the fibration
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
. To give such a section
s:\Cscr\to\El(F)
we must declare the following:

•

For each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, an object
sX\in F(X)_{0}
. Such an object gives our object pair
s(X):=(X,sX)
in
\El(F)
.

•

For each morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
we must determine a morphism
\tau_{f}:F(f)(sY)\to sX
. This gives our morphism
s(f):=(f,\tau_{f})
.

•

Asking for
s
to be a functor asks that for all composable pairs of morphisms
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(F(g)(sZ)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(sY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)(sZ)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces sX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\tau_{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{g\circ f}}

must commute. Similarly, we must also have that
\tau_{\id_{X}}=\id_{sX}
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
.

However, such information is exactly that of a lax cone of shape
F
with apex
\One
by an earlier discussion of ours.

Describing morphisms between sections is more difficult. We have to decide whether we work with
all
possible natural transformations of sections or only with those which behave trivially over the base category
\Cscr
. In keeping with the fibrational theme, we elect to work with those sections which are invariant over the base in the following sense.

Definition 2.3.9
.

Let
p:\Escr\to\Bscr
be a fibration and let
s,t:\Bscr\to\Escr
be sections of
p
. If
\alpha:s\Rightarrow t
is a natural transformation such that the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

is equal to the identity
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\id}}

then we say that
\alpha
is a based natural transformation between sections. We write
\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)
for the category of sections of
p
and based natural transformations.

Let us return to the case where we have a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and two sections
s,t:\Cscr\to\El(F)
of the fibration
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
. When we have a based natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\El(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

we find from the condition
p\ast\alpha=\iota_{\id_{\Cscr}}
that when we write each map

\alpha_{X}:(X,sX)\to(X,tX)

as a pair
\alpha_{X}=(f,\rho_{X})
then
f=\id_{X}
and
\rho_{X}
is a morphism of the form

\rho_{X}:tX\to sX;

note that this follows from the fact that our pseudofunctors are normalized in the sense that
F(\id_{X})=\id_{FX}
so
F(\id_{X})(tX)=\id_{FX}(tX)=tX
. Consequently, for any morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
the commutativity of the naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,sX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,tX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,sY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,tY)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\sigma_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\id_{X},\rho_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\tau_{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\id_{Y},\rho_{Y})}

tells us that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f\circ\id_{X})(tY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(tY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces tX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(sY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces sX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\rho_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{f}}

commutes. However, this is exactly the condition that the collection of maps
P=\{\rho_{X}:tX\to sX\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
is a morphism

P:(\{tX\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\},\{\tau_{f}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\})\to(\{sX\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\},\{\sigma_{f}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\}).

In this way we see that every based transformation of sections of
p
is an
\op
-morphisms of lax cones. Perhaps unsurprisingly this construction works both ways and allows us to derive the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3.10
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and let
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
be the associated elements fibration. Then
\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)^{\op}=\LC(F)
.

Proof.

The discussion prior to the statement of the proposition gives the subobject inclusions
\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)_{0}\subseteq\LC(F)_{0}
and
\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)_{1}^{\op}\subseteq\LC(F)_{1}
. However, the reverse inclusion
\LC(F)_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)_{0}
is simply an unwinding of the definition using the objects
\,{}_{X}{A}:=sX
and morphisms
\tau_{f}^{A}:=sf
. The inclusion
\LC(F)_{1}\subseteq\mathbf{Sect}_{B}(p)^{\op}_{1}
is shown similarly.
∎

We can capture the category
\PC(F)
by using what we call Cartesian sections, i.e., sections
s:\Cscr\to\El(F)
of the projection
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
for which each morphism
s(f)=(f,\tau_{f})
is a Cartesian arrow over
f
. In order classify these sections we need to know the Cartesian arrows over
f
. However, by construction each Cartesian arrow in
\El(F)
over
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
is isomorphic to an arrow of the form
(f,\id_{F(f)A}):(X,F(f)A)\to(Y,A)
for some
A\in F(Y)_{0}
. Consequently the Cartesian arrows are of the form
(f,\tau_{f}):(X,B)\to(Y,A)
where
\tau_{f}:F(f)A\to B
is an isomorphism. This gives the characterization of
\PC(F)
below.

Proposition 2.3.11
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and let
p:\El(F)\to\Cscr
be the associated elements fibration. Then if
\mathbf{CSect}_{B}(p)
is the category of Cartesian sections of
p
and based natural transformations then
\PC(F)
is equal to the category
\mathbf{CSect}_{B}(p)^{\op}
.

Proof.

This follows mutatis mutandis to Proposition
2.3.10
and the observation categorizing the Cartesian arrows above any given arrow in
\El(F)
.
∎

For various examples of the categories of pseudocones in algebraic geometry, see
[
63
, Example 3.2.7]
; we will recall some here, however. Various topological analogues of these can be given, and we present some below (in addition to other examples).

Example 2.3.12
.

Let us consider the following various examples of
\PC(F)
for various pseudofunctors
F
.

(1)

Let
K
be a field, let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over
K
, and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then the category of (
\ell
-adic) equivariant perverse sheaves has a formulation in terms of a category of the form
\Per_{G}(X)=\PC(F)
by taking
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
to be a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto{}^{p}\of^{\ast}:\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),

respectively. That this is equivalent to the usual incarnation of the category of equivariant perverse sheaves is shown in
[
1
]
,
[
63
]
, and also in Corollary
5.1.28
.

(2)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over
K
and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then the category of equivariant local systems on
X
is given by
\PC(L)
where
L
is a pseudofunctor
L:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
with object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto\of^{\ast}:\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X))\to\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)),

respectively.

(3)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over
K
and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then the equivariant derived category of perverse sheaves on
X
,
D_{G}^{b}(\Per(X))
is determined by
\PC(F)
for the pseudofunctor

F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat

where
F
has object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)))

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto L\left({}^{p}\of^{\ast}\right):D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)))\to D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))),

respectively.

(4)

Let
G
be a topological group and let
X
be a left
G
-space. The category of equivariant perverse sheaves on
X
is determined by
\PC(F)
for a pseudofunctor
F:\FResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
where
F
has object and morphism assignments

P\mapsto\Per(G\backslash P)

and

f\mapsto{}^{p}\of^{\ast}:\Per(G\backslash Q)\to\Per(G\backslash P)

respectively.

(5)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. We can then describe the category
D
-
\mathbf{Mod}_{G}(X)
of
G
-equivariant
D
-modules as
\PC(D)
where
D:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

D(\Gamma\times X):=\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))

and

D(f\times\id_{X}):=\of^{\ast}:\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X))\to\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))

respectively. Note that in this case the functor
\of^{\ast}
exists by
[
5
, Section 4.1]
because each morphism
\of
is smooth by Proposition
6.1.4
.

(6)

Let
G
be a Lie group, regarded as a topological group, and let
X
be a smooth manifold (regarded as a topological space) with a smooth
G
-action. Then the category of
G
-equivariant sheaves on
X
is given by
\PC(F)
where
F:\FResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

P\mapsto\Shv(G\backslash P)

and

f\mapsto\of^{\ast}:\Shv(G\backslash Q)\to\Shv(G\backslash P),

respectively.

(7)

Let
(\Cscr,J)
be a site and consider a pseudofunctor
S:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
defined on objects and morphisms by

S(X):=\Shv(\Cscr_{/X},J_{/X})

and

f\mapsto f^{\ast}:\Shv(\Cscr_{/Y},J_{/Y})\to\Shv(\Cscr_{/X},J_{/X}),

respectively. Note that
f^{\ast}
is the functor where, if
f:X\to Y
is the morphism then for any sheaf
\Fscr\in\Shv(\Cscr_{/X},J_{/Y})
the sheaf
f^{\ast}\Fscr
acts on objects
u:U\to X
by

\Fscr(U\xrightarrow{u}X\xrightarrow{f}Y)

and on
X
-morphisms
g:U\to V
by evaluating
\Fscr
on the morphism
g
regarded as a commuting triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ v}

Then
\PC(S)
gives a category anti-equivalent to the global sections of the
J
-stack
\El(S)\to\Cscr
which are effective on descent.

We close this section introducing the pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
by showing that when
\Cscr
has a terminal object there is an equivalence of categories
\PC(F)\simeq F(\top_{\Cscr})
and use this to give a quick proof of the fact that for principal
G
-fibrations
X
there is an equivalence of categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D^{b}(G\backslash X)
implied by the pseudocone formalism.

Proposition 2.3.13
.

Let
\Cscr
be a category with a terminal object and let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. Then there is an equivalence of categories

F(\top_{\Cscr})\simeq\PC(F).

Proof.

We show this by providing two functors
L:F(\top_{\Cscr})\to\PC(F)
and
R:\PC(F)\to F(\top_{\Cscr})
and then showing that
R\circ L\cong\id_{F(\top)}
and
L\circ R\cong\id_{\PC(F)}
. Following alphabetic order, we first define the functor
L:F(\top_{\Cscr})\to\PC(F)
as follows. Given an object
A
of
F(\top)
we define the collection

LA:=\left\{F(!_{X})(A)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

where
!_{X}:X\to\top
is the unique map from
X
to the terminal object in
\Cscr
. Then for any morphism
f:X\to Y
we have the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{Y}}

in
\Cscr
. Consequently we define our transition isomorphisms by setting

\tau_{f}^{LA}:=\phi_{f,!_{Y}}^{A}:F(f)\left(F(!_{Y})(A)\right)\to F(!_{Y}\circ f)(A)=F(!_{X})(A).

That the collection
T_{LA}=\{\tau_{f}^{LA}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\}
satisfies the cocycle condition follows from the fact that the
\phi_{-,-}
are the compositor isomorphisms for the pseudofunctor
F
. The definition on morphisms is given similarly, i.e., for any morphism
\rho:A\to B
in
F(\top)
the map
L\rho
is given by

L\rho=\left\{F(!_{X})(\rho)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

That this is a functor is an immediate verification.

Let us now define the functor
R:\PC(F)\to F(\top_{\Cscr})
. For objects
A=(A,T_{A})
define
RA
by

RA=R(\{\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\},\{\tau_{f}^{A}\;|f\in\Cscr_{1}\}):=\,_{\top}{A}

and for morphisms
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
we define
RP:=\,_{\top}{\rho}
. This is even more straightforward than
R
to verify to be a functor.

To check that
R\circ L=\id_{F(\top)}
we see that for any object
A\in F(\top)_{0}
,

\displaystyle(R\circ L)(A)

\displaystyle=R\left(\{F(!_{X})(A)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}\right)=F(!_{\top})(A)=F(\id_{\top})(A)=\id_{F(\top)}(A)

\displaystyle=A

and similarly for morphisms
\rho
. Thus
R\circ L=\id_{F(\top)}
.

We now check that
L\circ R\cong\id_{\PC(F)}
. For this fix an object

\left(\left\{\,{}_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},\left\{\tau_{f}^{A}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\right\}\right)

and note that we have

\left(L\circ R\right)A=\left(\left\{F(!_{X})(\,_{\top}{A})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},\left\{\phi_{f,!_{Y}}^{\,{}_{\top}{A}}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1},f:X\to Y\right\}\right).

We now claim that for there is an isomorphism
Z_{A}:(L\circ R)(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}A
which is natural in
A
. For this we need to define
Z_{A}=\{\,_{X}{\zeta_{A}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
where for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
,

\,{}_{X}{\zeta_{A}}:F(!_{X})(\,_{\top}{A})\to\,_{X}{A}.

However, we simply set
\,{}_{X}{\zeta_{A}}:=\tau_{!_{X}}^{A}
. To see that this is a morphism in the category
\PC(F)
we need to show that for any morphism
f
of
\Cscr
, say
f:X\to Y
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{((L\circ R)(A))}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{(L\circ R)(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{\zeta_{A}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{(L\circ R)(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\zeta_{A}}}

commutes. However, as this diagram is exactly the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(F(!_{Y})(\,_{\top}{A})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(!_{X})(\,_{X}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\tau_{!_{Y}}^{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,!_{Y}}^{\,{}_{\top}{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{!_{X}}^{A}}

the commutativity of the diagram follows from the cocycle condition the transition isomorphisms of
A
satisfy and the fact that
!_{X}=!_{Y}\circ f
. Thus
Z_{A}
is an isomorphism in
\PC(F)
. Establishing that it is natural comes down to checking that if
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\circ R)(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\circ R)(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(L\circ R)(P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{B}}

commutes. But since this comes down to check the relevant diagram for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and the
X
-local components have the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(!_{X})(\,_{\top}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(!_{X})(\,_{\top}{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{!_{X}}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(!_{X})(\,_{\top}{\rho})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{!_{X}}^{B}}

and so the naturality of
Z
is equivalent to the fact that morphisms in
\PC(F)
are required to commute appropriately with the transition isomorphisms. It thus follows that
L\circ R\cong\id_{\PC(F)}
via the natural isomorphism
Z
and so, combined with the fact that
R\circ L=\id_{F(\top)}
, this gives the equivalence

\PC(F)\simeq F(\top_{\Cscr}).

∎

We now use the proposition above to give short and completely categorical proofs of the facts that
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D^{b}(G\backslash X)
whenever either
G
is a topological group
X
is a free
G
-space or when
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is an
\Sf(G)
-object.

Corollary 2.3.14
.

Let
G
be a topological group and assume that
G
acts freely on
X
. Then there is an equivalence of categories

D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D^{b}(G\backslash X).

Proof.

Because
G
acts freely on
X
, it follows that the identity morphism
\id_{X}:X\to X
is a free
G
-resolution of
X
. Because
\FResl_{G}(X)
is a subcategory of
\Top_{/X}
it follows that
\id_{X}
is a terminal object in
\FResl_{G}(X)
. Applying Proposition
2.3.13
to the pseudofunctor
D^{b}(G\backslash(-))
of Subsection
2.2
gives the equivalence

D^{b}(G\backslash X)=D^{b}(G\backslash(\id_{X}))\simeq\PC(D^{b}(G\backslash(-)))=D_{G}^{b}(X).

∎

Corollary 2.3.15
.

Let
G
be an affine smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a principal
G
-variety. Then
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
.

Proof.

The assumptions in the statement of the corollary imply that
\id_{X}:X\to X
is a terminal object in
\Resl_{G}(X)
. Applying Proposition
2.3.13
and Remark
2.1.10
we then get that

\displaystyle D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash X)

\displaystyle=D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash\top_{\Resl_{G}(X)})

\displaystyle\simeq\PC\left(D_{c}^{b}\big(G\backslash(-)\big):\Resl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\PC\left(D_{c}^{b}\big(G\backslash(-)\big):\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat\right)=D_{G}^{b}(X).

The
\ell
-adic case follows similarly.
∎

We now show that the category
\PC(F)
is the pseudolimit of the diagram
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and that
\LC(F)
is the lax limit of the diagram of shape
F
. Note that this gives a sanity check of the descriptions of lax limits and pseudolimits in
[
23
]
.

Theorem 2.3.16
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. Then
\PC(F)
is the pseudolimit of
F
in
\fCat
.

Proof.

We first show that
\PC(F)
is a category with a pseudonatural transformation
\cnst(\PC(F))\to F
, as that is what it means first to be a pseudocone over
F
. For this we first define the functors
p_{X}:\PC(F)\to F(X)
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}

where
\pi_{X}
is the usual projection out of the product and
p:\PC(F)\to\prod_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)
is the functor which on objects
(A,T_{A})
discards the transition isomorphisms and sends
A
to
(\,_{X}{A})_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}
and on morphisms sends
P
to
(\,_{X}{\rho})_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}
. Now define the natural isomorphism
\alpha_{f}:F(f)\circ p_{Y}\Rightarrow p_{X}
by defining, for all objects
(A,T_{A})\in\PC(F)_{0}
,

\alpha_{f}^{A}:=\tau_{f}^{A}.

That this is a natural transformation is a straightforward check and follows immediately from the fact that the morphisms
P:(A,T_{A})\to(B,T_{B})
must commute suitably with the transition morphisms in
T_{A}
and
T_{B}
. Moreover, that these vary pseudonaturally in
\Cscr^{\op}
also follows immediately from the cocycle condition

\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)(\tau_{g}^{A})

and hence gives that
\PC(F)
is a pseudocone over
F
. We call this pseudocone
\alpha:\cnst(\PC(F))\to F
.

We now show that any pseudocone over
F
in
\fCat
factors through
\alpha
. To this end assume that we have a category
\Dscr
equipped with functors
G_{X}:\Dscr\to F(X)
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and natural isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
which vary pseudonaturally in
\Cscr^{\op}
. Now define a functor
G:\Dscr\to\PC(F)
as follows. For an object
Z\in\Dscr_{0}
, define the collection

GZ:=\left\{G_{X}(Z)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

and define the transition isomorphisms
T_{GZ}
by

T_{GZ}:=\left\{\beta_{f}^{Z}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\right\}

so that
\tau_{f}^{GZ}=\beta_{f}^{Z}
. To see that this indeed are transition isomorphisms, we simply use that
(G_{X},\beta_{f})
describes a pseudonatural transformation
\cnst(\Dscr)\to F
in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
and so we get that the equation

\tau_{g\circ f}^{GZ}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\beta_{g\circ f}^{Z}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\beta_{f}^{Z}\circ F(f)\left(\beta_{g}^{Z}\right)=\tau_{f}^{GZ}\circ F(f)\left(\tau_{g}^{GZ}\right)

holds. For morphisms
\rho:Z\to W
in
\Dscr
we similarly define
G\rho
by

G\rho:=\left\{G_{X}(\rho)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},

which is trivially checked to be a morphism in
\PC(F)
by the naturality of the
\beta_{f}
. That this is a functor is then immediate to verify and follows from the fact that the
G_{X}
are all functors.

We now note that for any
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and any
Z\in\Dscr_{0}
we have that

(p_{X}\circ G)(Z)=p_{X}\left(\left\{G_{X}(Z)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},T_{GZ}\right)=G_{X}(Z)

and similarly for morphisms. Thus
p\circ G=G_{X}
for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
.

Consider the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}

where the empty cells denote the identity transformation. We claim this pastes to the
2
-cell describing
\beta_{f}
. To see this we simply note that since
\alpha_{f}
picks out the transition isomorphism
\tau_{f}^{A}
of an object in
\PC(F)
, for any
Z\in\Dscr_{0}
we get

\alpha_{f}^{GZ}=\tau_{f}^{GZ}=\beta_{f}^{Z}

which proves the claim. Thus it follows that the pseudocone
\Dscr
factors through
\PC(F)
.

We now show the final remaining property of
\PC(F)
being a pseudolimit. Assume that we have two pseudocones

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}

for which the
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}\ast G}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y}\circ G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X}\circ G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}\ast G^{\prime}}

coincide. However, this implies that for every
Z\in\Dscr_{0}
and
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we have that

\,{}_{X}{GZ}=p_{X}(G(Z))=(p_{X}\circ G)(Z)=(p_{X}\circ G^{\prime})(Z)=p_{X}(G^{\prime}(Z))=\,_{X}{G^{\prime}Z}

and for every
f\in\Cscr_{1}

\tau_{f}^{GZ}=\alpha_{f}\ast G=\alpha_{f}\ast G^{\prime}=\tau_{f}^{G^{\prime}Z}.

Thus we have that
GZ=(GZ,T_{GZ})=(G^{\prime}Z,T_{G^{\prime}Z})=G^{\prime}Z
for all
Z\in\Cscr_{0}
. Checking that
G\rho=G^{\prime}\rho
for all
\rho\in\Dscr_{1}
is similar. It thus follows that
G=G^{\prime}
and hence that there is a unique morphism
G\Rightarrow G^{\prime}
: the identity modification. From all this it follows that
\PC(F)
is the pseudolimit of
F
in
\fCat
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.17
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. Then
\LC(F)
is the lax limit of
F
in
\fCat
.

Proof.

This follows mutatis mutandis to the proof of Theorem
2.3.16
.
∎

2.4.
Basic Properties of the Category of Pseudocones

Let us close this introductory chapter by first introducing some terminology (so that we can talk succinctly about pseudofunctors that universally take values in additive categories and
\Vscr
-locally small categories, among other such things) before proceeding to develop some basic theory of pseudocone categories.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Fix a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. If for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and
f\in\Cscr_{1}
the category
F(X)
is additive and each functor
F(f)
is additive, we call
F
an additive pseudofunctor.

Definition 2.4.2
.

Fix a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and a Grothendieck universe
\Vscr
. If
\Cscr
is
\Vscr
-small and for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the category
F(X)
is a
\Vscr
-locally small category we call
F
a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor.

We now show a quick smallness result; as such fix, for the moment, a Grothendieck universe
\Vscr
. With applications to additive and triangulated categories in mind, we need to know that
\PC(F)
is a category in the same universe as
\Vscr
whenever we have a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
lest we have to jump universes just to define addition on hom-sets.

Lemma 2.4.3
.

Let
\Vscr
be a Grothendieck universe and let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor. Then
\PC(F)
is
\Vscr
-locally small.

Proof.

Assume that
\Vscr
has associated strongly inaccessible cardinal
\kappa
. Fix two arbitrary objects
(A,T_{A}),(B,T_{B})\in\PC(F)_{0}
; note that it suffices to show that
\PC(F)(A,B)
is a
\Vscr
-set, i.e., that
\lvert\PC(F)(A,B)\rvert<\kappa
.

Note that by assumption
\lvert\Cscr_{0}\rvert,\lvert\Cscr_{1}\rvert<\kappa
. For what follows, consider the topos
\VSet
of
\Vscr
-sets and let
\Omega\in\VSet_{0}
be the corresponding subobject classifier. Because
\kappa
is strongly inaccessible, the power objects
\Pcal_{\Vscr}(X):=[X,\Omega]
in
\VSet_{0}
satisfy
\lvert\Pcal_{\Vscr}(X)\rvert<\kappa
.

Consider now that since
P
is defined as the collection

P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{B}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{A}\in A,\,_{X}{B}\in B\}

we have that

P\subseteq\bigcup_{X\in\Sf(G)_{0}}F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B}).

Because each of the categories
F(X)
is locally
\Vscr
-small, each hom-collection

F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B})
is a
\Vscr
-set. Thus, since
\Cscr_{0}
is a
\Vscr
-set, we have that the union

Y:=\bigcup_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B})

is a
\Vscr
-set as well.

Now note that for each
P\in\PC(F)(A,B)
, we have that
\{P\}\subseteq\Pcal_{\Vscr}(Y)
. Thus it follows that

\Pcal_{\Vscr}(Y)\supseteq\bigcup_{P\in\PC(F)(A,B)}\{P\}=\PC(F)(A,B)

and hence we have that
\PC(F)(A,B)
is a
\Vscr
-set. This proves the lemma.
∎

We now conclude with one last lemma which shows that if
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor such that each category
F(X)
is enriched in
\Tcal(\VSet)
for a (potentially infinitary) Lawvere theory
\Tcal
and each functor
F(f)
is a
\Tcal(\VSet)
-functor then
\PC(F)
is enriched in
\Tcal(\VSet)
as well. The basic argument simply reduces to the fact that the various operations we need to be defined and coherences we need to satisfy are defined
X
-locally, they are defined globally as well. While we present the lemma below at a relatively large level of generality, we only need it explicitly to discuss when
\PC(F)
is
\VAb
enriched. As such we refer the reader unfamiliar with (infinitary) Lawvere theories to
[
9
, Appendix A]
and also to
[
41
]
for an introduction to the theory of limit sketches. All we need for what follows is that Lawvere theories
\Tcal
(in the universe
\Vscr
) are categories
\Tcal
with all
\Vscr
-indexed products and a distinguished object
L\in\Tcal_{0}
such that for any object
K
of
\Tcal
there is a
\Vscr
set
I
and an isomorphism
K\cong L^{I}
in
\Tcal
. Models of the theory
\Tcal
(in a category
\Cscr
) are product-preserving functors
M:\Tcal\to\Cscr
and morphisms of models are natural transformations between product-preserving functors.

Remark 2.4.4
.

Because the object assignment of a model
M:\Tcal\to\Cscr
of a Lawvere theory is largely determined by its value
M(L)
we will usually refer to the model by the object
M(L)
itself. In this vein we also will abuse notation and refer to a morphism of models
M\Rightarrow N
as
\underline{\varphi}
in reference to the morphism
\varphi=\varphi_{L}:M(L)\to N(L)
, as to a large degree this determines the natural transformation.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

Let
\Vscr
be a Grothendieck universe and let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor. Let
\Tcal
be a Lawvere theory. If each category
F(X)
is enriched in
\Tcal(\VSet)
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
then the category
\PC(F)
is enriched in
\Tcal(\VSet)
as well.

Proof.

Recall that in order to show that
\PC(F)
is enriched in
\Tcal
we need to show that each hom-set
\PC(F)(A,B)
is a model of
\Tcal
in
\VSet
for all
A,B\in\PC(F)_{0}
. For this it suffices to show that there is a product-preserving functor
M:\Tcal\to\VSet
for which
\Tcal(L)=\PC(F)(A,B)
when
L
is the distinguished object of the Lawvere theory. It then further suffices to show that if
S
is any
\Vscr
-set then for all
s\in S
the induced
S
-tuple

\left(M(\pi_{s}):M(L^{S})\to M(L)\right)_{s\in S}

is a product diagram in
\VSet
.

To define
M
we use the full skeletal subcategory of
\Tcal
consisting of powers
L^{I}
for all
\Vscr
-sets
I
for objects and taking our morphisms to be those morphisms between
L^{I}
and
L^{J}
for all
\Vscr
-sets
I
and
J
and then translate through the isomorphisms of objects
K\cong L^{I}
in order to extend
M
to
\Tcal
. For this we define
M
as follows on powers of
L

M(L)^{I}:=\PC(F)(A,B)^{I}

and on morphisms
f:L^{I}\to L^{J}
in
\Tcal
by the assignment, for
(P_{i})_{i\in I}\in\PC(F)(A,B)^{I}

M(f)(P_{i})_{i\in I}:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{M}(f)(\,_{X}{\rho_{i}})_{i\in I}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

where
\,{}_{X}{M}
is the model functor

\,{}_{X}{M}:\Tcal\to\VSet

given by
\,{}_{X}{M}(L)=F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B})
specified by the
\Tcal(\VSet)
-enrichment structure on
F(X)
. That this is a functor follows from the fact that each model
\,{}_{X}{M}
is a functor. To see that
M
preserves products fix a
\Vscr
-set
S
and consider the diagram

\left(M(\pi_{s}):M(L^{S})\to M(L)\right)_{s\in S}

and note that each morphism
M(\pi_{s})
takes the form

M(\pi_{s})=\left\{\,{}_{X}{M}(\pi_{s})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Because each
\,{}_{X}{M}(\pi_{s})
is the projection from a product it follows from a straightforward verification
2
2

2

Alternatively, the reader who is willing to accept the poor form of forward-referencing a result can use Theorem
3.1.3
below.
that
M(\pi_{s})
is a projection morphism as well. Thus
M
is product-preserving and
\PC(F)(A,B)
is a model of
\Tcal(\VSet)
.

We now must show that for any morphism
P:A\to B
in
\PC(F)
, pre-and-post-composition by
P
is a morphism of models of
\Tcal
. The pre-compositional argument is dual to the pre-compositional one, so we present only the post-compositional argument. Let
C
be an object in
\PC(F)
and recall that we must prove that the post-composition morphism
P_{\ast}:\PC(F)(C,A)\to\PC(F)(C,B)
induces a morphism of models.

For this we take the same approach as above and only show the naturality of
\underline{P}_{\ast}
along the skeletal subcategory of
\Tcal
generated by the
L^{I}
. Let
f:L^{I}\to L^{J}
be a morphism in
\Tcal
and consider the induced morphisms of
\Vscr
-sets

\PC(F)(Z,A)(f):\PC(Z,A)^{I}\to\PC(Z,A)^{J}

and

\PC(F)(Z,B)(f):\PC(F)(Z,B)^{I}\to\PC(F)(Z,B)^{J}.

By construction the maps
\PC(F)(Z,A)(f)
all have the form

\PC(F)(Z,A)(f)=\left\{F(X)(\,_{X}{Z},\,_{X}{A})(f)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Now write
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
and note that for all
\Vscr
-sets
S

(\underline{{P_{\ast}}})_{L^{J}}=\left\{\left(\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho}_{\ast}}}\right)_{L^{S}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

To see that
\underline{{P}}_{\ast}
is natural we use that each map
\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho}_{\ast}}}
is natural (as each category
F(X)
is
\Tcal(\Vscr)
-enriched) and calculate

\displaystyle\left(\underline{{P_{\ast}}}\right)_{L^{J}}\circ\PC(F)(Z,A)(f)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho}_{\ast}}}\right)_{L^{J}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}\circ\left\{F(X)(\,_{X}{Z},\,_{X}{A})(f)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho}_{\ast}}}\right)_{L^{J}}\circ F(X)(\,_{X}{Z},\,_{X}{A})(f)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{F(X)(\,_{X}{Z},\,_{X}{B})(f)\circ(\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{\ast}}}})_{L^{I}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{F(X)(\,_{X}{Z},\,_{X}{B})(f)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}\circ\left\{(\underline{{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{\ast}}}})_{L^{I}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

\displaystyle=\PC(F)(Z,B)\circ\left(\underline{{P}}_{\ast}\right)_{L^{I}}.

It thus follows that
\underline{{P_{\ast}}}
is natural and so, after proceeding with a similar argument to show that
\underline{{P^{\ast}}}:\PC(F)(B,C)\to\PC(F)(A,C)
is a natural transformation between models, we get that
\PC(F)
is enriched over
\Tcal(\VSet)
.

∎

Corollary 2.4.6
.

Assume that
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a
\Vscr
-locally small pseudofunctor and assume that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the category
F(X)
is enriched in
\VAb
. Then
\PC(F)
is enriched in
\VAb
as well.

Proof.

Simply apply Lemma
2.4.5
to the observation that
\VAb=\Ab(\VSet)
where
\Ab
denotes the Lawvere theory of Abelian groups.
∎

Chapter 3
Limits, Colimits, and Structures in the Category of Pseudocones

Some of the most important aspects when working with a category lie in its limits, colimits, and the structures it has available. For instance one of the reasons that toposes are often touted as a world in which to do logic and geometry lie in the fact that structurally toposes have not only limits and colimits, but also ample structure which is rich enough to be the world of not only higher order intuitionistic logic but also for sheaf-theoretic geometry and topology (in the sense that the categories
\Shv(\Cscr,J)
of sets are all special kinds of toposes). Because in practice we need to know about these various properties and how they interact with descent (especially if one is interested in studying equivariant cohomology or working with objects like equivariant perverse sheaves, equivariant derived hom, or other such gadgets and functors) it becomes important to examine when the categories
\PC(F)
admit limits, colimits, and other structures. Because of our motivation with the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),

\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
, and other categorical-geometric gadgets (such as the equivariant tangent categories studied in
[
50
]
) our main approach is to study limits, colimits, and structures in
\PC(F)
which arise from limits, colimits, and structures in the fibre categories
F(X)
and which of those limits, colimits, and structures are preserved by the translation functors
F(f)
.

3.1.
Limits and Colimits for Pseudocones

Our first goal in our development of pseudocone categories is to show that if each category
F(X)
admits either a zero object or finite products and if each fibre functor
F(f)
preserves these zero objects and products then
\PC(F)
. These, together with Corollary
2.4.6
, will allow us to conclude that the category
\PC(F)
is additive whenever each category
F(X)
is and each of the functors
F({f})
are additive. The first result we present below is admittedly a special case of Theorems
3.1.3
and
3.1.4
, but we present it in isolation to give a straightforward example of how these proofs work before diving into more general cases.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and assume that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
each category
F(X)
has a zero object
\,{}_{X}{0}
and that each functor
F({f})
preserves each corresponding zero object. Then the object

0=\{\,_{X}{0}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

is a zero object in
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

Begin by defining
(0,T_{0})
by setting the collection
0
as above and defining

T_{0}:=\{\tau_{f}^{0}:F({f})(\,_{Y}{0})\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{0}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\}

as the collection of unique ismorphisms witnessing the fact that the
F(f)
preserve the zero object.

We will first verify that
0
is terminal in
\PC(F)
; that it is initial follows by duality and will be omitted. First let
(A,T_{A})
be an aribtrary object in
\PC(F)
. Define the map
!_{A}:(A,T_{A})\to(0,T_{0})
by taking the
X
-local collection of the terminal maps
!
on each
\,{}_{X}{A}
; explicitly

!_{A}:=\{!_{\,{}_{X}{A}}:\,_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{0}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

That
!_{A}
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
is clear, as the diagrams

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{!_{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{0}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{0}}

all commute for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
and for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
. That this map
!_{A}
is unique follows from the fact that for any other morphism
P:A\to 0
, we would have by each
\,{}_{X}{0}
being terminal that
\,{}_{X}{\rho}=!_{\,{}_{X}{A}}
and hence that
!_{A}=P
. This proves the lemma.
∎

By reusing the proof of Lemma
3.1.1
we get the following.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

If each category
F(X)
has a terminal (or initial) object and each functor
F({f})
preserves it, then
\PC(F)
has a terminal (or initial) object.

For what follows below we will prove when it is possible to deduce that the equivariant category has limits over diagrams of specific shape based on when limits of the same shape exist in each fibre category
F(X)
. This will allow us to deduce, say in the cases of equivariant quasi-coherent sheaves, perverse sheaves, local systems,
\Ocal_{X}
-modules, and other examples where the fibre functors
F({f})
are exact/left exact, that the category
\PC(F)
has all finite limits (or limits of specific type; cf. Corollaries
3.1.5
and
3.1.6
).

Theorem 3.1.3
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and let
I
be an index category for which there is a diagram
d:I\to\PC(F)
. For each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
let
d_{X}:I\to F(X)
denote the diagram functor

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\PC(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\imath}}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{F(X)}

and assume that for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the limit
\lim d_{X}
exists in
F(X)
. Furthermore, assume that for every morphism
f\in\Cscr_{1}
with
f:X\to Y
there is an isomorphism
\theta_{f}
witnessing the limit preservation

F({f})\left(\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}d_{X}(i)\right)\cong\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}F({f})(d_{X}(i)).

Then the diagram
d
admits a limit in
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

Let
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
be a pair of composable arrows in
\Cscr
. Recall the natural isomorphism
\phi_{f,g}:F(f)\circ F(g)\Rightarrow F(g\circ f)
; we will abuse notation and write

\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}:F(f)\big(F(g)d_{Z}(i)\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(g\circ f)\left(d_{Z}(i)\right)

in place of the more cumbersome
\phi_{f,g}^{d_{Z}(i)}
. Define the collection of objects

A:=\left\{\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}d_{X}(i)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

To see how to define the transition isomorphisms
\tau_{f}^{A}
for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
, recall that for all
f:X\to Y
there are natural isomorphisms

\theta_{f}:F(f)\left(\lim(d_{Y})\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\lim(F(f)\circ d_{Y}).

Then for all
i\in I_{0}
we have commuting diagrams

\textstyle{\lim(F(f)\circ d_{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})}
\textstyle{\lim(d_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{F(f)(d_{Y}(i))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{d_{X}(i)}

and, because each
\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}
is an isomoprhism, so is
\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})
. We thus define the transition isomorphism
\tau_{f}^{A}:F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{A}
via the composition:

\textstyle{F(f)(\lim(d_{Y}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{\lim(F(f)\circ d_{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})}
\textstyle{\lim(d_{X})}

Note that because
\theta_{f}
and
\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})
are isomorphisms so is the composite
\tau_{f}^{A}
.

We now need to verify the cocycle condition

\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}

for the composable pair of morphisms
f
and
g
. With this in mind we will decompose the isomorphism
\theta_{g\circ f}
in order to get an algebraic identity that will allow us to prove the cocycle condition. Consider that by definition

\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}=\lim(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A_{i}})\circ\theta_{g\circ f}

and by the uniqueness of the ismorphism
\theta_{g\circ f}
(as it is induced from the universal property of a limit) we find that the diagram (where we recall that
d_{Z}(i)=\,_{Z}{A_{i}}
and
\lim\,_{Z}{A_{i}}=\,_{Z}{A}
)

\textstyle{F(g\circ f)(\lim(\,_{Z}{A_{i}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\left(\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\lim(F(g\circ f)(\,_{Z}{A_{i}}))}
\textstyle{F(f)\big(F(g)(\lim(\,_{Z}{A_{i}}))\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\theta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{F(f)\big(\lim F(g)(\,_{Z}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\big(\lim\tau_{g}^{A_{i}}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\lim F(f)\big(F(g)(\,_{Z}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{F(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\lim\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\big(\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\big)^{-1}}

commutes in
F(X)
.
We now consider the morphism
\psi:\lim\big(F(g\circ f)\,_{Z}{A_{i}}\big)\to\lim\,_{X}{A_{i}}
defined by the composition:

\textstyle{\lim\big(F(g\circ f)(\,_{Z}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{g\circ f}^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)\big(\lim(\,_{Z}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\big(\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}\big)^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(f)(F(g)\lim(\,_{Z}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces)}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}}
\textstyle{\lim(\,_{X}{A_{i}})}
\textstyle{F(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}

Substituting our calculation of
\theta^{-1}_{g\circ f}
above into the definition of
\psi
yields

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\circ\big(\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}\big)^{-1}\circ\theta_{g\circ f}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\circ F(f)\theta_{g}^{-1}\circ F(f)(\lim\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})^{-1}\circ\theta_{f}^{-1}

\displaystyle\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\circ F(f)\big(\theta_{g}^{-1}\circ(\lim\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})^{-1}\big)\circ\theta_{f}^{-1}

\displaystyle\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\circ F(f)\big(\tau_{g}^{A}\big)^{-1}\circ\theta_{f}^{-1}\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A}\circ\theta_{f}^{-1}\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\circ\theta_{f}\circ\theta_{f}^{-1}\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}}\circ(\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A_{i}}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\circ(\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim\tau_{g\circ f}^{A_{i}},

where we canceled the composite
\big(\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}\big)^{-1}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}
without mention in the second equality and where we used the universal property of the limit to give the equation

\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\circ\lim(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}})\circ\big(\lim\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big)^{-1}=\lim\big(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A_{i}}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}\big).

This gives that
\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\circ\big(\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}\big)^{-1}\circ\theta_{g\circ f}^{-1}=\lim\tau_{g\circ f}^{A_{i}}
, which in turn implies that

\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}=\lim\left(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A_{i}}\right)\circ\theta_{g\circ f}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{\lim A_{i}}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A}.

Therefore it follows that
T_{A}
satisfies the cocycle condition and hence the pair
(A,T_{A})
is an object in
\PC(F)
.

We now show that
(A,T_{A})
is the object vertex of a cone over the
(A_{i},T_{A_{i}})
, i.e., that there are morphisms
P_{i}:A\to A_{i}
for all
i\in I_{0}
that make the diagrams

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{j}}
\scriptstyle{P_{i}}
\textstyle{A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(\alpha)}
\textstyle{A_{j}}

commute whenever there is a morphism
\alpha\in I(i,j)
. For any
i\in I_{0}
we define the collection
P:A\to A_{i}
by

P:=\{\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}:\,_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{A_{i}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

where each
\,{}_{X}{\rho}\in F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{A_{i}})
is the corresponding cone map at
i
. That this makes
A
into a cone over the
A_{i}
is clear if
P
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
, as for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the corresponding cone identity holds. Thus all we need to do is verify that

is a morphism in
\PC(F)
, i.e., we need to show that for all

, say with

, the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}

commutes. For this first recall that
\tau_{f}^{A}=\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\circ\theta_{f}
and that the morphism
\lim(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})
is defined from the commuting diagram

\textstyle{\lim\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{\lim(\,_{X}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}

, where

\textstyle{F(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}}
\textstyle{\lim\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})}

in

. These together give the commuting diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}}
\textstyle{\lim\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lim\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

which gives the equality

\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\quo{\rho_{i}}{Y}=\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ\pi_{i}\circ\theta_{f}=\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\lim\big(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\big)\circ\theta_{f}=\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}.

Thus the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}

commutes and so

is a morphism in
\PC(F)
. This shows that

is a cone over the

.

Finally we verify that
A
is a universal cone over
\PC(F)
. For this let
(C,T_{C})
be a cone over the

with morphisms

for which

\Psi_{i}=\{\,_{X}{\psi_{i}}:\,_{X}{C}\to\,_{X}{A_{i}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Because each

is the limit in

of the

, for all

we produce unique morphisms

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\psi_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\psi_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{j}}}

commute for all

and for all
\alpha\in I(i,j)
. We now will show that the collection

\zeta:=\{\,_{X}{\zeta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

is a morphism in

. Note that this will complete the proof of the proposition, as any other cone map
\Phi:C\to A
will be component-wise equal to

by the uniqueness of the
\,{}_{X}{\zeta}
.

To see that
\zeta
is a morphism we must verify that for any
f\in\Cscr_{1}
the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{C}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\zeta}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}}

commutes where
\Dom f=X
and
\Codom f=Y
. To this end, let
i,j\in I_{0}
and let
\alpha\in I(i,j)
. Then, because
C
is a cone to the
A_{i}
and because the assignment
d:I\to\PC(F)
is a functor (so in particular
d(\alpha)
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
), we find that the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{i}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\big(d_{Y}(\alpha)\big)}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{j}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{j}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{j}}}

commutes in
F(\,_{X}{X})
. Thus there exists a unique morphism
\xi_{f}
making the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{j}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{X}{\psi_{i}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d(\alpha)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A_{j}}}

commute. Using the above diagram together with the identity

\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}=\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}

gives that on one hand

\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\xi_{f}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{i}}=\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}\circ\,_{Y}{\zeta}\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta};

following this same argument mutatis mutandis we derive also that

\,{}_{X}{\rho_{j}}\circ\xi_{f}=\,_{X}{\rho_{j}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}.

On the other hand, using that
\Psi_{i}
is an
\PC(F)
-morphism gives that

\,{}_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\,_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{C}=\,_{X}{\psi_{i}}\circ\tau_{f}^{C}=\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{i}}=\,_{X}{\rho_{i}}\circ\xi_{f}.

As before, we also derive that
\,{}_{X}{\rho_{j}}\circ\,_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{C}=\,_{X}{\rho_{j}}\circ\xi_{f}
. Putting all these identities together gives us that both
\,{}_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{C}
and
\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}
produce fillers for the universal cone diagram in the same way that
\xi_{f}
does; consequently, by the universal property of
\,{}_{X}{A}
as the limit over the
\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}
, we derive that

\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}=\xi_{f}=\,_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{C}.

This gives the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{C}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\zeta}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}}

and hence shows that
(A,T_{A})
is a universal cone over the
(A_{i},T_{A_{i}})
. Because this shows that
d
has a limit in
\PC(F)
, this completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Theorem 3.1.4
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and let
I
be an index category for which there is a diagram
d:I\to\PC(F)
. For each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
let
d_{X}:I\to F(X)
denote the diagram functor

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\PC(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\imath}}
\textstyle{\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{F(X)}

and assume that for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the colimit
\colim d_{X}
exists in
F(X)
. Furthermore, assume that for every morphism
f\in\Cscr_{1}
with
f:X\to Y
there is an isomorphism
\theta_{f}
witnessing the limit preservation

F(f)\left(\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}d_{X}(i)\right)\cong\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longrightarrow\\
i\in I\end{subarray}}F(f)(d_{X}(i)).

Then the diagram
d
admits a colimit in
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

Dualize the proof of Theorem
3.1.3
.
∎

Corollary 3.1.5
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. If
I
is an index category and each category
F(X)
has all limits of shape
I
and if each functor
F(f)
preserves these limits, then
\PC(F)
has all limits of shape
I
as well. Dually, if each category
F(X)
has all colimits of shape
I
and if each functor
F(f)
preserves these colimits, then
\PC(F)
has all colimits of shape
I
as well.

Proof.

Use Theorem
3.1.3
for every diagram
d:I\to\Cscr
, as such a diagram gives rise to a compatable family of diagrams
d_{X}:I\to\Cscr
by way of projection to the
X
-th component.
∎

Corollary 3.1.6
.

If each category
F(X)
is (finitely) complete and each functor
F(f)
is (finitely) continuous then
\PC(F)
is (finitely) complete. Dually,
\PC(F)
is (finitely) cocomplete if each category
F(X)
is (finitely) cocomplete and if each functor
F(f)
is finitely cocontinuous.

Proof.

Use Corollary
3.1.5
with the categories
I=\varnothing
to give terminal objects,
I=\coprod_{s\in S}\Delta^{0}
(where
S
is an indexing
\Vscr
-set) to give products indexed by
S
, and
I=\Lambda_{2}^{2}
to give pullbacks. Invoking now the classical categorical result that a category
\Cscr
admits all limits if and only if it admits products, terminal objects, and pullbacks we find that
\PC(F)
is complete.
∎

Remark 3.1.7
.

The finiteness assumptions above are used exclusively when determining which indexing sets
S
we are allowed to use. If one only knows of the existence of finite limits or colimits, we restrict our attention to sets of finite cardinality.

Corollary 3.1.8
.

If the pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is additive and locally
\Vscr
-small then the category
\PC(F)
is a locally
\Vscr
-small additive category.

Proof.

Corollary
3.1.5
shows that
\PC(F)
admits finite products and a zero object. Local
\Vscr
-smallness is Lemma
2.4.3
, while the
\VAb
enrichment is Corollary
2.4.6
as
\VAb=\Ab(\VSet)
. Finally, appealing to a classical result in homological algebra (cf.
[
28
, Proposition II.9.1]
, for instance) gives that
\PC(F)
is additive.
∎

Corollary 3.1.9
.

If each of the categories
F(X)
are complete and cocomplete as well as additive, and if the functors
F(f)
preserve limits and colimits, then
\PC(F)
is additive, complete, and cocomplete as well.

Proof.

Corollary
3.1.8
gives the additive structure on
\PC(F)
while Corollary
3.1.6
gives the completion and cocompletion.
∎

We now present some lemmas that allow us to deduce some cases when the pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
are Abelian. Of particular use here is that this gives proofs that the categories of equivariant
{D}
-modules, local systems, and quasi-coherent (étale) sheaves on a variety
X
are Abelian (cf. Corollary
3.1.13
below). Similar results for when
X
is a topological space or a smoooth manifold hold as well.

While illuminating the structure of Abelian pseudocone categories, we also present a proposition about descending regular categories through these pseudocone constructions. While this will not be fundamentally important for us, it will show the way in which descent arguments aid in developing a notion of pseudocone-based categorical logic.

We proceed by recalling that a category
\Cscr
is Abelian if and only if it is additive, (finitely) complete, (finitely) cocomplete, and has the property that every monomorphism is a kernel and every epimorphism is a cokernel. We have already seen in Corollary
3.1.9
that under mild assumptions the category
\PC(F)
is additive, complete, and cocomplete. Thus all we need to show are the regularity conditions, i.e., the fact that every monomorphism is a kernel and that every epimorphism is a cokernel.

Lemma 3.1.10
.

Assume that
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor, that each functor
F(f)
is continuous and cocontinuous, and that each category
F(X)
admits equalizers and cokernel pairs of morphisms. Then if every monic in each fibre category
F(X)
is a kernel, every monic in
\PC(F)
is a kernel.

Proof.

Begin by recalling that a morphism
\Psi
is a kernel of a morphism
\Phi:A\to B
if and only if there is an isomorphism
\Psi\cong\eq(I_{0},I_{1})
, where
I_{0}
and
I_{1}
are the cokernel pair of
\Phi
that appear in the pushout below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dom\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Codom\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Codom\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Codom\Phi\coprod_{\Dom\Phi}\Codom\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{0}}

To proceed we let
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}:A\to B
be a monomorphism in
\PC(F)
and we let the pair
(I_{0},I_{1})
be the cokernel pair of
P
arising from the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\coprod_{A}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{0}}

in
\PC(F)
. From Theorem
3.1.4
we know that the pushout of
B\leftarrow A\rightarrow B
is given by

B\coprod_{A}B=\left\{\,{}_{X}{B}\coprod_{\,{}_{X}{A}}\,{}_{X}{B}\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

so each
I_{k}
has the form

I_{k}=\left\{\,{}_{X}{i_{k}}:\,_{X}{B}\to\,_{X}{B}\coprod_{\,{}_{X}{A}}\,{}_{X}{B}\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},

where
k\in\{0,1\}
and where each pair
(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})
is the cokernel pair of
\,{}_{X}{\rho}
in
F(X)
. Using Theorem
3.1.3
we also find that the morphism
P
, as a monic in
\PC(F)
, has the property that each
\,{}_{X}{\rho}
is monic in
F(X)
. Because each monomorphism in each fibre category
F(X)
is a kernel, there is a unique isomorphism
\,{}_{X}{\theta}:X\xrightarrow{\cong}\Eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\,_{X}{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})}

commute in
F(X)
; note that
\Eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})
is the equalizer object of the
\,{}_{X}{i_{k}}
and
\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})
is the canonical equalizer morphism. Using Theorem
3.1.3
again, we have that the collection

\Eq(I_{0},I_{1}):=\left\{\Eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

determines the object assignment of the equalizer
\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})
in
\PC(F)
and the collection

\eq(I_{0},I_{1}):=\left\{\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

determines the equalizer morphism
\eq(I_{0},I_{1}):\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})\to B
in
\PC(F)
. Thus we will be done if we can show that the
\Vscr
-set

\Theta:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\theta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

is a morphism
\Theta:A\to\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})
in
\PC(F)
, as then
\Theta
is the unique morphism making the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eq(I_{0},I_{1})}

commute.

For this fix a morphism
f\in\Cscr_{1}
and let
f:X\to Y
. We now must show that
\,{}_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}=\tau_{f}^{\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})}\circ F(f)\eq(\,_{Y}{i_{0}},\,_{Y}{i_{1}})
. For this we calculate that

\displaystyle\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})\circ\,_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\big(\eq(\,_{Y}{i_{0}},\,_{Y}{i_{1}})\circ\,_{Y}{\theta}\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\eq(\,_{Y}{i_{0}},\,_{Y}{i_{1}})\circ F(f)\,_{X{{}^{\prime}}}{\theta}

\displaystyle=\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})\circ\tau_{f}^{\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\theta}.

Using that
\eq(\,_{X}{i_{0}},\,_{X}{i_{1}})
is monic then allows us to deduce that

\,{}_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}=\tau_{f}^{\Eq(I_{0},I_{1})}\circ F(f)\,_{X}{\theta},

which proves that
\Theta\in\PC(F)(A,\Eq(I_{0},I_{1}))
.
∎

By dualizing the above lemma, we also have the result below for cokernels and epimorphisms.

Lemma 3.1.11
.

Assume that
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor, that each functor
F(f)
is continuous and cocontinuous, and that each category
F(X)
admits coequalizers and kernels pairs of morphisms. Then if every epimorphism in each fibre category
F(X)
is a cokernel, every epimorphism in
\PC(F)
is a cokernel.

This leads us to the fact that Abelian categories descend through the equivariance, so if each fibre category
F(X)
is Abelian and if each fibre functor
F(f)
is exact then
\PC(F)
is Abelian.

Proposition 3.1.12
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor such that each category
F(X)
is Abelian and for which each functor
F(f)
is exact. Then the category
\PC(F)
is Abelian as well.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.1.9
it follows that the category
\PC(F)
is complete, cocomplete, and additive. To see that it has the necessary regularity property, simply use Lemmas
3.1.10
and
3.1.11
to derive that every monic and epic in
\PC(F)
is a kernel or cokernel, respectively.
∎

As a corollary, we have that the categories of equivariant
D
-modules, local systems, and quasicoherent sheaves are all Abelian.

Corollary 3.1.13
.

Each of the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
,
\DGMod(X)
,
\Loc_{G}(X)
,
\Per_{G}(X)
, and
\QCoh_{G}(X)
are all additive. Moreover, the categories
\DGMod(X)
,
\Loc_{G}(X)
,
\Per_{G}(X)
, and
\QCoh_{G}(X)
are Abelian.

Proof.

That all four categories are additive follows from Corollary
3.1.8
. For each
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
the quotient map

\quo_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\times X\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

is smooth by Proposition
6.1.3
. Similarly, for any
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Sf(G)
the induced map between quotient varieties

\of:G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\to G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)

is also smooth by Proposition
6.1.3
. This then implies that each functor
\overline{f}^{\ast}
is exact on the level of étale sheaves and both exists and is exact at the level of
D
-modules. Thus we can apply Corollary
3.1.9
to derive that the categories
\DGMod(X),

\Loc_{G}(X)
,
\Per_{G}(X)
, and
\QCoh_{G}(X)
are all finitely complete and finitely cocomplete. Finally, Proposition
3.1.12
shows that the categories
\DGMod(X),

\Loc_{G}(X),

\Per_{G}(X)
and
\QCoh_{G}(X)
are all Abelian categories, as each of the fibre categories
\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)),

\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))
,
\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))
, and
\QCoh(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))
are Abelian for all
\Gamma\times X\in\SfResl(G)_{0}
and have smooth fibre functors
\of^{\ast}
for all
f\times\id_{X}\in\Sf(G)_{1}
.
∎

The same proof may be used to show that the category
\Shv_{G}(X,R)
of (étale) sheaves of (left) modules over a fixed ring
R
(or modules over a fixed sheaf of rings
\CalO_{X}
) is Abelian; similarly, the category
\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
of
\ell
-adic sheaves is Abelian. We record these results in the corollary below.

Corollary 3.1.14
.

The category
\OXMod_{G}
of equivariant
\Ocal_{X}
-modules and the category
\Shv_{G}(X;R)
of equivariant sheaves of
R
-modules are Abelian. Moreover, the categories
\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
of equivariant
\ell
-adic sheaves and
\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
of equivariant
\ell
-adic perverse sheaves are also Abelian.

3.2.
Monoidal Structures on Pseudocone Categories

We now begin providing cases when the equivariant categories
\PC(F)
are monoidal. This will allow us to not only give a foundation for an equivariant monoidal theory, but also give us tools to study equivariant derived monoidal theory and equivariant dg-categories over the geometric objects about which we care. The main use for these tchniques in this paper are in homological algebraic settings, as it gives us tools with which to construct the six-functor-formalism of the equivariant derived category and other pseudocone-based descent-theoretic settings. I expect us to be able to use this in noncommutative algebraic geometry (following the foundations and methodology of
[
31
]
,
[
49
]
,
[
58
]
,
[
59
]
, among others, which define and work with noncommutative schemes in dg-categorical language) to give an equivariant notion of noncommutative (derived) algebraic geometry. Additionally, the braided monoidal category structure on
\PC(F)
could be useful in equivariant higher algebra or in a descent-based approach to TQFTs, fusion categories, tensor categories and other such structures useful in higher-categorical physics; for various references on tensor categories, TQFTs, fusion categories, and such see for example
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
15
]
,
[
17
]
, and
[
30
]
. For the moment, however, we will simply provide the main situation that allows us to deduce when the category
\PC(F)
is monoidal in terms of having a pseudofunctor with monoidal categories for fibre categories and (strong) monoidal functors for fibre functors.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. We say that
F
is monoidal if the following hold:

•

For all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, each category
F(X)
is a monoidal category

\left(F(X),\overset{X}{\otimes},\,_{X}{I},\,_{X}{\alpha},\,_{X}{\lambda},\,_{X}{\rho}\right);

•

For all
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr_{1}
,
f
is monoidal in the sense that there are natural isomorphisms

\theta_{f}^{A,B}:F(f)\left(A\overset{Y}{\otimes}B\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(f)A\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)B

which are pseudofunctorial in the sense that for all composable pairs
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
, the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Z}{X})\times F(\,_{Z}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Z}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Y}{X})\times F(\,_{Y}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Y}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)\times F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)\times F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)\times F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{Z}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\times F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{Y}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{X}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}^{-1}\times\phi_{f,g}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

is equal to the
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Z}{X})\times F(\,_{Z}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\,_{Z}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)\times F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{Z}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)\times F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{X}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g\circ f}}

•

The functors
F(f)
preserve the units pseudofunctorially in the sense that there for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
, there is an isomorphism

\sigma_{f}:F(f)\,_{Y}{I}\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{I}

such that for all composable pairs
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
, we have that

\sigma_{f}\circ F(f)\sigma_{g}=\sigma_{g\circ f}\circ\phi_{f,g}.

Remark 3.2.2
.

The functors we have called monoidal in this paper are frequently called strong monoidal in the category-theoretic literature (cf.
[
3
]
,
[
32
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
65
]
, for instance). We have chosen to drop the adjective “strong” for a few reasons: First, we will need the isomorphisms for each use of a monoidal functor in this paper; second, we do not want to confuse strong monoidal functors with monoidal functors that have tensorial strengths; and third, having monoidal functors be implicitly lax is not something that our descent formalism will allow. We need to be working with tensorial transition isomorphisms, not simply transition maps; as such, we will need the full strength of a monoidal functor which preserves the tensor and units up to natural isomorphism.

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a monoidal pseudofunctor. Then
\PC(F)
is a monoidal category.

Proof.

First note that if we can define the functor
\otimes,
the object
I
, and the natural isomorphisms
\lambda,\rho,
and
\alpha
which
X
-wise agree with the functors, objects, and transformations in the fibre category

\left(F(X),\overset{X}{\otimes},\,_{X}{I},\,_{X}{\lambda},\,_{X}{\rho},\,_{X}{\alpha}\right)

we will be done, as all triangle and pentagonal identities will hold
X
-wise, which is how we we compute these identities. We begin by defining the monoidal functor
\otimes:\PC(F)\times\PC(F)\to\PC(F)
. Define the functor on objects by setting, for
(A,T_{A})
and
(B,T_{B})
objects in
\PC(F)
,

A\otimes B:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}\;\big|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{A}\in A,\,_{X}{B}\in B\right\}.

To define the transition isomorphisms on
A\otimes B
first fix an
f\in\Cscr_{1}
and write
f:X\to Y
. Then we define the morphism
\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}
via the composite

\textstyle{F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}^{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}\otimes\tau_{f}^{B}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}}

where
\theta_{f}^{A,B}
is used as an abuse of notation for

\theta_{f}^{A,B}:=\theta_{f}^{\,{}_{Y}{A},\,_{Y}{B}}.
