We then define the transition isomorphisms via the definition

T_{A\otimes B}:=\left\{\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\right\}.

To verify that the transition isomorphisms satisfy the cocycle condition, let
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
be a pair of composable morphisms in
\Cscr
. Now, using the pseudofunctoriality of the
\otimes
functors together with the naturality of the
\theta_{f}
’s gives that

\displaystyle\tau_{g\circ f}^{A\otimes B}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{g\circ f}^{B}\right)\circ\theta_{g\circ f}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\left(\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\overset{X}{\otimes}\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{B}\big)\right)\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}\circ\theta_{g\circ f}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\left(\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\overset{X}{\otimes}\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{B}\big)\right)

\displaystyle\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}\circ\phi_{f,g}\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}\circ F(f)\theta_{g}^{A,B}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\overset{X}{\otimes}\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{B}\big)\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}\circ F(f)\theta_{g}^{A,B}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\left(F(f)\tau_{g}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)\tau_{g}^{B}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}\circ F(f)\theta_{g}^{A,B}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}\circ F(f)\left(\tau_{g}^{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\tau_{g}^{B}\right)\circ F(f)\theta_{g}^{A,B}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A\otimes B}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{-1}.

Thus we have that
\tau_{g\circ f}^{A\otimes B}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{A\otimes B}
and so

\big((A,T_{A}),(B,T_{B})\big)\mapsto(A\otimes B,T_{A\otimes B})

determines the object assignment of the
\otimes
functor.

To define
\otimes
on morphisms, fix a morphism
\Phi\in\PC(F)(A,B)
and a morphism
\Psi\in\PC(F)(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. We then define the morphism

\Phi\otimes\Psi:A\otimes A^{\prime}\to B\otimes B^{\prime}

by

\Phi\otimes\Psi:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{\psi}\;\big|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{\varphi}\in\Phi,\,_{X}{\psi}\in\Psi\right\}.

This is a morphism because for any map
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
, we have the equalities
\,{}_{X}{\varphi}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}=\tau_{f}^{A^{\prime}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\varphi}
and
\,{}_{X}{\psi}\circ\tau_{f}^{B}=\tau_{f}^{B^{\prime}}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi}
so it is immediate that

\,{}_{X}{(\Phi\otimes\Psi)}\circ\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\varphi}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{\psi}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A^{\prime}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B^{\prime}}\right)\circ\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{\varphi}\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A^{\prime}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B^{\prime}}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}\circ F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\varphi}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{\psi}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\circ F(f)(\,_{Y}{(\Phi\otimes\Psi)}).

It also follows by the fact that each of the
\overset{X}{\otimes}
are functors that
\otimes
preserves compositions and identities, giving that
\otimes
is a functor as well.

Let us show that the object
I=\{\,_{X}{I}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
determines an object in
\PC(F)
. Define
T_{I}:=\{\sigma_{f}:F(f)\,_{Y}{I}\to\,_{X}{I}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\}
and note that by assumption for any
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Y
in
\Cscr
, we have that

\tau_{f}^{I}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{I}=\sigma_{f}\circ F(f)\sigma_{g}=\sigma_{g\circ f}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{I}\circ\phi_{f,g}.

Thus
(I,T_{I})
is an object in
\PC(F)
.

Next we prove that there are natural isomorphisms
\lambda:I\otimes\id_{\PC(F)}\to\id_{\PC(F)}
and
\rho:\id_{\PC(F)}\otimes I\to\id_{\PC(F)}
; we will only show that
\lambda
exists, as the existence of
\rho
will follow mutatis mutandis.

Fix an object
A\in\PC(F)_{0}
and define
\lambda_{A}:I\otimes A\to A
by

\lambda_{A}:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}:\,_{X}{I}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{A}\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

It remains to verify that
\lambda_{A}
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
, so let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\Cscr
. Observe that since the tensor functors vary pseudofunctorially over
\Cscr
through the preservation isomorphisms, we get that the invertible
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{I}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{A}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{I}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

induces the commuting diagram:

\textstyle{F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{I}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{A}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\lambda_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}^{I,A}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{I})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{I}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}}

This gives that

\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\lambda_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)=\,_{X}{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{I}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{I,A}=\,_{X}{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{I\otimes A},

which proves that
\lambda_{A}
is an
\PC(F)
morphism (and in fact an isomorphism because each
\,{}_{X}{\lambda}
is an isomorphism). That
\lambda
is natural is immediate from the natruality of each
\,{}_{X}{\lambda}
, so we indeed get that
\lambda:I\otimes\id_{\PC(F)}\to\id_{\PC(F)}
is a natural isomorphism. Similarly, we also have that
\rho:\id_{\PC(F)}\otimes I\to\id_{\PC(F)}
is a natural isomorphism.

We now need only to verify the existence of the associator(s). For this fix three objects
A,B,C\in\PC(F)_{0}
and define
\alpha_{A,B,C}:(A\otimes B)\otimes C\to A\otimes(B\otimes C)
via

\displaystyle\alpha_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}:\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}\right)\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{C}\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{C}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

We now prove that this is an
\PC(F)
morphism. Fix an
f\in\Cscr(X,Y)
and write
f:X\to Y
. Then we compute that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{(A\otimes B)\otimes C}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}

while on the other hand

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{A\otimes(B\otimes C)}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B\otimes C}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\right)\circ\left(\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\theta_{f}^{B,C}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}.

Furthermore, using the pseudofunctoriality of the tensor functors and the naturality of the associators in each fibre category gives that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)\left(\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{C}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\overset{X}{\otimes}F(\of)(\,_{Y}{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(F(\of)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(\of)(\,_{Y}{B})\right)\overset{X}{\otimes}F(\of)(\,_{Y}{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(F(\of)(\,_{Y}{B})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(\of)(\,_{Y}{C})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{B}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{C}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\left(\,{}_{Y}{B}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{C}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\of)\,_{Y}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{A,B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(\of)\,_{Y}{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha_{F(\of)A,F(\of)B,F(\of)C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{F(\of)\,_{Y}{A}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\theta_{f}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}\right)^{-1}}

commutes. Substituting the induced identities above yields that the composite morphism
\tau_{f}^{A\otimes(B\otimes C)}\circ F(f)(\,_{Y}{\alpha_{A,B,C}})
is equal to

\displaystyle\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\right)\circ\left(\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\theta_{f}^{B,C}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\right)\circ\left(\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\theta_{f}^{B,C}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}

\displaystyle\circ\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B\otimes C}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\theta_{f}^{B,C}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\circ\,_{X}{\alpha_{F(f)A,F(f)B,F(f)C}}\circ\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\right)\circ\,_{X}{\alpha_{F(f)A,F(f)B,F(f)C}}

\displaystyle\circ\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}\circ\left(\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{C}\right)\circ\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A\otimes B,C}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}\circ\tau_{f}^{(A\otimes B)\otimes C},

which in turn proves that
\tau_{f}^{A\otimes(B\otimes C)}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}=\,_{X}{\alpha_{A,B,C}}\circ\tau_{f}^{(A\otimes B)\otimes C}.
Thus
\alpha_{A,B,C}
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
(and in fact an isomorphism because each
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
is an isomorphism). Moreover, the naturality of
\alpha
is immediate from the fact that each
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
is a natural isomorphism.
∎

Another important character in the monoidal categorical and representation-theoretic worlds is that of braided monoidal categories. These are monoidal categories
\Cscr
equipped with a braiding natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr\times\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

where
s:\Cscr\times\Cscr\to\Cscr\times\Cscr
is the switching isomorphism generated by
(A,B)\mapsto(B,A)
on objects and
(f,g)\mapsto(g,f)
on morphisms. This natural isomorphism gives a monoidally coherent way of interchanging and commuting the objects through the tensor “up to some constant/relation” and also a categorification of noncommutative algebra. Because of the use of braided monoidal categories in representation theory, the study of TQFTs, noncommutative algebra, Hopf algebras, algebraic topology, and higher algebra we are interested in when these structures can be translated to
\PC(F)
and hence when they arise on equivariant categories (in the sense of those categories defined in
[
50
]
,
[
63
]
, and below). I expect these to be of use especially when studying and working in the context of equivariant higher algebra and the study of generalized equivariant cohomology theories.

Definition 3.2.4
.

A braided monoidal category is a monoidal category
(\Cscr,\otimes,I)
together with a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr\times\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

such that for all objects
A,B,C\in\Cscr_{0}
the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(B\otimes C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes C)\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes A)\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes(A\otimes C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes(C\otimes A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A,B}\otimes\id_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A,B\otimes C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{B,C,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{B,A,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{Y}\otimes\beta_{A,C}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(B\otimes C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(C\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes C)\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C\otimes A)\otimes B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}\otimes\beta_{B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,B,C}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A\otimes B,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{C,A,Y}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A,C,B}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A,C}\otimes\id_{B}}

commute. Additionally, if
\beta_{B,A}\circ\beta_{A,B}=\id_{A\otimes B}
for all
A,B\in\Cscr_{0}
we say that
\Cscr
is a symmetric monoidal category.

Definition 3.2.5
.

Let
(\Cscr,\otimes,I,\,_{\Cscr}{\beta})
and
(\Dscr,\boxtimes,J,\,_{\Dscr}{\beta})
be braided monoidal categories. A monoidal functor
F:\Dscr\to\Cscr
is braided monoidal if for all
A,B\in\Dscr_{0}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\left(A\boxtimes B\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)\otimes F(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\left(B\boxtimes A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(B)\otimes F(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\left(\,{}_{\Dscr}{\beta_{A,B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Cscr}{\beta_{A,B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{B,A}}

commutes.

Definition 3.2.6
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor. We say that
F
is a braided monoidal pseudofunctor if
F
is a monoidal psuedofunctor and additionally every category
F(X)
is braided monoidal while every functor
F(f)
is also braided monoidal.

We now show that when the pseudofunctor
F
is braided monoidal so is the category
\PC(F)
.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a braided monoidal pseudofunctor. Then
\PC(F)
is braided monoidal with braiding
\beta_{A,B}:A\otimes B\to B\otimes A
given by

\beta_{A,B}:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

where
\,{}_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}
is a shorthand for the braiding isomorphism
\,{}_{X}{\beta_{\,{}_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B}}}
in
F(X)
. Additionally if each category
F(X)
is symmetric monoidal then so is
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

We know from Theorem
3.2.3
that
\PC(F)
is monoidal; we now only need to furnish
\PC(F)
with the braiding structure suggested in the statement of the proposition and prove that it gives the desired natural isomorphism :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)\times\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\circ s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Note that the switching functor
s:\PC(F)\times\PC(F)\to\PC(F)\times\PC(F)
is determined by
X
-locally switching the objects and morphisms.

We prove first that
\beta_{A,B}
is a morphism for any
A,B\in\PC(F)_{0}
. For this let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\Cscr
and recall that

\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}=\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\tau_{f}^{A,B}.

We now must prove that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{B}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\,_{X}{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\beta_{A,B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{B\otimes A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}}

commutes in order to conclude that
\beta_{A,B}
is a morphism. For this we simply calculate that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{B\otimes A}\circ F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\beta_{A,B}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{B,A}\circ F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\beta_{A,B}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\beta_{F(f)A,F(f)B}}\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\theta_{f}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A\otimes B},

as was desired.

We now prove that
\beta
is a natural transformation. Let
(A,B)
and
(C,D)
be objects in
\PC(F)\times\PC(F)
and let
(P,\Phi):(A,B)\to(C,D)
be a morphism. Then we calculate that for any
X\in\Cscr_{0}
,

\,{}_{X}{\left(\Phi\otimes P\right)}\circ\,_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\phi}\otimes\,_{X}{\rho}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}=\,_{X}{\beta_{C,D}}\circ\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\otimes\,_{X}{\varphi}\right)

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta_{C,D}}\circ\,_{X}{\left(P\otimes\Phi\right)}

by the fact that each
\,{}_{X}{\beta_{-,-}}
is a natural transformation. Thus the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\otimes D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\otimes C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\otimes\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi\otimes P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{C,D}}

commutes in
\PC(F)
and so
\beta
is a natural isomorphism. That it satisfies the Hexagon Axioms is straightforward by using that each
X
-local braiding isomorphism satisfies the corresponding braiding isomorphism. Thus
\PC(F)
is a braided monoidal category. Finally, if every braiding gives a symmetric monoidal structure on
F(X)
we then calculate that

\beta_{B,A}\circ\beta_{A,B}=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\beta_{B,A}}\circ\,_{X}{\beta_{A,B}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\id_{A\otimes B}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}=\id_{A\otimes B}

which shows that in this case
\PC(F)
is also symmetric monoidal.
∎

Example 3.2.8
.

There are two standard examples of monoidal pre-equivariant pseudofunctors that we should keep in mind. The first is that of the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
given on objects by
F(\XGamma)=\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and on morphisms by
F(\of)=\of^{\ast}
. Then each category
F(\XGamma)
is monoidal with tensor functor

\otimes_{X}:=(-)\overset{L}{\otimes}_{\Gamma}(-).

Note that the functor
(-)\otimes_{\Gamma}^{L}(-):\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\times\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is the (left) derived tensor functor of
\ell
-adic sheaves on
\XGamma
.
By Theorem
3.2.3
and Proposition
3.2.7
this produces a symmetric monoidal functor
(-)\otimes^{L}(-)
on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
.

The second example to keep in mind is the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
given on objects by

F(\XGamma)=\Shv(\XGamma,\text{{\'{e}}t})

and on morphisms by

F(\of)=\of^{\ast}:\Shv(\XGammap,\text{{\'{e}}t})\to\Shv(\XGamma,\text{{\'{e}}t}).

The monoidal functor in this case is defined to be the one induced by

\otimes_{\Gamma}:=(-)\times_{\Gamma}(-)

This then induces an alternative construction of the product functor on the category
\Shv_{G}(X,\text{{\'{e}}t})
by emphasizing its monoidal properties.

3.3.
Regularity and Subobject Classifiers for Pseudocones

We now move to studying some cases when equivariant categories are regular categories and when equivariant categories have subobject classifiers.(cf. Definition
3.3.2
below). We will defer the study of adjunctions to the next chapter where functors between pseudocone categories are studied in significantly higher depth than we have seen so far.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor such that each fibre category
F(X)
is regular and for which each fibre functor
F(f)
is finitely complete and preserves regular epimorphisms. Then
\PC(F)
is regular.

Proof.

Recall that a category
\Cscr
is regular if and only if
\Cscr
is finitely complete, admits all coequalizers of kernel pairs, and has the property that regular epimorphisms are stable under base change, i.e., regular epimorphisms are stable under pullback. We now proceed to show that
\PC(F)
is regular by first showing that it is finitely complete; however, for this we simply apply Corollary
3.1.6
.

To see that
\PC(F)
admits coequalizers of kernel pairs, let
P\in\PC(F)(A,B)
with
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
. Now take the pullback

\textstyle{A\times_{B}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{1}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{B}

and note that by Theorem
3.1.3
the pullback takes the form

A\times_{B}A:=\{\,_{X}{A}\times_{\,{}_{X}{B}}\,{}_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

with the induced transition isomorphisms, while for
k\in\{0,1\}
the morphisms
\Pi_{k}
take the form

\Pi_{k}:=\{\,_{X}{\pi_{k}}:\,_{X}{A}\times_{\,{}_{X}{B}}\,{}_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

where each
\,{}_{X}{\pi_{k}}
is the corresponding first or second pullback map in the fibre category
F(X)
. Now define the
\Vscr
-set

C:=\{\Coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

By assumption, each coequalizer exists in each category
F(X)
and each functor
F(f)
preserves these coequalizers. Thus by Theorem
3.1.4

(C,T_{C})
is an object in
\PC(F)
(with induced transition isomorphisms
T_{C}
) and satisfies
C\cong\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})
with
\coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1}):A\to C
given by the set

\coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})=\{\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Thus
\PC(F)
admits coequalizers of kernel pairs.

We now verify that regular epimorphisms are stable under base change. Assume that
\Sigma:A\to C
is a regular epimorphism in
\PC(F)
and let
P:B\to C
be an arbitrary morphism. Take the pullback

\textstyle{A\times_{C}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\scriptstyle{Z}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{C}

where
Z:=\{\,_{X}{\zeta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
. Now let
(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})
be the kernel pair of
Z
and let
\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})
be the coequalizer of the kernel pair; note that again by Theorem
3.1.4
, we have that

\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})=\{\Coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

and

\coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})=\{\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Because each category
F(X)
is regular, we have that
\,{}_{X}{\zeta}\cong\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})
via a unique isomorphism
\,{}_{X}{\theta}
fitting into the diagram

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\times_{\,{}_{X}{C}}\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})}
\textstyle{\Coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\,_{X}{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\times_{\,{}_{X}{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\zeta}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}}

in
F(X)
. We need to check now that
\Theta=\{\,_{X}{\theta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
is an
\PC(F)
morphism, as it is automatically an isomorphism if it is an
\PC(F)
-morphism. For this we must prove that for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
(say with
\Dom f=X
and
\Codom f=Y
),

\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\theta}\overset{?}{=}\,_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})}.

For this we recall that
Z:A\times_{B}A\to B
and that
\coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1}):A\times_{B}A\to\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})
are morphisms in
\PC(F)
. We then calculate that on one hand

\,{}_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{A\times_{C}B}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta}\circ\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\circ\tau_{f}^{A\times_{C}B}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})}\circ F(f)\coeq(\,_{Y}{\pi_{0}},\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}})

while on the other hand

\,{}_{X}{\zeta}\circ\tau_{f}^{A\times_{C}B}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\zeta}=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\theta}\circ\coeq(\,_{X}{\pi_{0}},\,_{X}{\pi_{1}})\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\theta}\circ F(f)\coeq(\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}},\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}}).

Because
F(f)
preserves regular epimorphisms, it follows that
F(f)\coeq(\,_{Y}{\pi_{0}},\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}})
is epic. Thus, after canceling
F(f)\coeq(\,_{Y}{\pi_{0}},\,_{Y}{\pi_{1}})
we get that

\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\theta}=\,_{X}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{\Coeq(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})}.

This shows that
\Theta
is a morphism in
\PC(F)
and hence proves that
Z
is a regular epimorphism. This completes the proof that
\PC(F)
is regular.
∎

Our next and final proposition for this section lies further in this direction of pseudocone-based categorical logic. We will show below that under mild assumptions that if all the fibre categories have subobject classifiers and if the fibre functors preserve these subobject classifiers, terminal objects, and subobject classification pullbacks, then
\PC(F)
has a subobject classifier as well.

Definition 3.3.2

(
[
40
, Chapter 1.3]
)
.

A category
\Cscr
has a subobject classifier
\Omega
if
\Cscr
has a terminal object
\top
and there is an object
\Omega\in\Cscr_{0}
with a morphism
\true:\top\to\Omega
such that for any monic
\mu:A\to B
in
\Cscr
, a unique morphism
\chi_{\mu}:B\to\Omega
making the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\textstyle{\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\true}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\chi_{\mu}}
\textstyle{\Omega}

into a pullback diagram.

Example 3.3.3
.

Let
\Cscr=\Set
be the category of sets. Then the set
\Omega=\{0,1\}
is the subobject classier. The map
\true:\{\ast\}\to\Omega
is given by
\ast\mapsto 1
and for any monic
\mu:A\to B
in
\Set
the morphism
\chi_{\mu}:B\to\Omega
is given by

\chi_{\mu}(b):=\begin{cases}1&\exists\,a\in A.\,\mu(a)=b;\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Example 3.3.4
.

Let
X
be a topological space. Then in the category
\Shv(X)
the subobject classifier is the sheaf
\Omega
with object assignment

\Omega(V)=\Top(V,\{\eta,s\})

where
\{\eta,s\}
is the Sierpinski space with
\eta
the open point and
s
the closed point.

Remark 3.3.5
.

Let
\Cscr
and
\Dscr
be categories with subobject classifiers
\Omega_{\Cscr}
and
\Omega_{\Dscr}
, respectively. We say that a functor
G:\Cscr\to\Dscr
preserves subobject classifing pullbacks when it preserves subobject classifiers, terminal objects, and if for a monic
\mu\in\Cscr(A,B)
which is preserved by
G
the diagram

\textstyle{GA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\mu}
\scriptstyle{G!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{G\mu}
\textstyle{G\top_{\Cscr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\true_{\Cscr}}
\textstyle{GB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\chi(\mu)}
\textstyle{G\Omega_{\Cscr}}

is isomorphic to the pullback diagram

\textstyle{GA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\mu}
\scriptstyle{!_{GA}}
\textstyle{\top_{\Dscr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\true_{\Dscr}}
\textstyle{GB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(G\mu)}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\Dscr}}

in
\Dscr
. That is, there are isomorphisms
G\top_{\Cscr}\to\top_{\Dscr}
and
G\Omega_{\Cscr}\to\Omega_{\Dscr}
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\top_{\Dscr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\top_{\Cscr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\Dscr}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\Omega_{\Cscr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{GA}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\true_{\Dscr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(G\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\chi(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\true_{\Cscr}}

commutes in
\Dscr
.

Proposition 3.3.6
.

Let
F
be a pseudofunctor such that each fibre category
F(X)
has a subobject classifier,
\,{}_{X}{\Omega}
, and for which each fibre functor
F(f)
preserves subobject classifiers, terminal objects, and subobject classifying pullbacks. Then
\PC(F)
admits a subobject classifier
\Omega
whose object collection has the form

\Omega=\{\,_{X}{\Omega}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Proof.

We begin by defining our subobject classifier. Because the statement of the proposition gave
\Omega=\{\,_{X}{\Omega}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
, we only need to give the transition isomorphisms
T_{\Omega}
. For each
f\in\Cscr_{1}
, say with
\Dom f=X
and
\Codom f=Y
, let
\theta_{f}:F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{\Omega}
be the witness isomorphism induced by the fact that each
F(f)
preserves subobject classifiers. We then define the collection of transition isomorphisms by declaring that for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
,
\tau_{f}^{\Omega}:=\theta_{f}.

Let us now verify the cocycle condition on the transition isomorphisms. Fix a composable pair of arrows
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
. We now verify that

\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(f)\tau_{g}^{\Omega}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{\Omega}\circ\phi_{f,g}.

For this begin by noting that the map
\tau_{g\circ f}^{\Omega}
is the unique isomorphism between
F(g\circ f)(\,_{Z}{\Omega})
and
\,{}_{X}{\Omega}
; similarly, since
F(f)\tau_{g}^{\Omega}
is the unique isomorphism between
F(f)\big(F(g)(\,_{Z}{\Omega})\big)
and
F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})
, while
\tau_{f}^{\Omega}
is the unique isomorphism between
F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})
and
\,{}_{X}{\Omega}
, we will be done if we know that
\phi_{f,g}^{\,{}_{Z}{\Omega}}
preserves subobject classifiers. However, this is immediate, as
\phi_{f,g}^{\,{}_{Z}{\Omega}}
is a natural isomorphism and both
F(f)(F(g)(\,_{Z}{\Omega}))
and
F(g\circ f)(\,_{Z}{\Omega})
are subobject classifiers. Thus the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\big(F(g)(\,_{Z}{\Omega})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\tau_{g}^{\Omega}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{\,{}_{Z}{\Omega}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\Omega}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)(\,_{Z}{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{g\circ f}^{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

commutes, proving that
(\Omega,T_{\Omega})
is an object in
\PC(F)
.

We now construct the map
\true:\top\to\Omega
in
\PC(F)
. Define

\true:=\{\,_{X}{\true}:\,_{X}{\top}\to\,_{X}{\Omega}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\};

to prove that this is an
\PC(F)
morphism, we must verify that for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
,

\,{}_{X}{\true}\circ\tau_{f}^{\top}\overset{?}{=}\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(f)\,_{Y}{\true}.

For this, however, note that because each functor preserves object classification pullbacks, given any monic
\mu:C\to D
in
\PC(F)
we have that the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\top}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{\top})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces!_{F(f)C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)!_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi(F(f)\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\chi(\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\true}}

commutes in
F(X)
. Taking the right-most face shows that the square

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{\top})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\true}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\top}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

commutes; because the horizontal isomorphisms in the square above are the unique isomorphisms between the objects, i.e., the witnessing isomorphisms, we have that the diagram takes the form

\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{\top})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\true}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\top}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\top}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

instead. However, this is exactly the verification that
\true
is an
\PC(F)
morphism.

We now construct the classifying map of an arbitrary monic in
\PC(F)
. Begin by letting
M=\{\,_{X}{\mu}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
be a monic
M\in\PC(F)(A,B)
; note that each map
\,{}_{X}{\mu}
is monic in
F(X)
by Theorem
3.1.3
. Then in each fibre category
F(X)
we have that there are (pullback) diagrams

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\mu}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\top}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

We thus define
\chi(M):B\to\Omega
via

\chi(M):=\{\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{\mu}\in M\};

note that we now must show that for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
with
f:X\to Y
,

\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(f)\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\overset{?}{=}\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})\circ\tau_{f}^{B}.

For this we note that since classifying maps are uniquely determined, it suffices to prove that the diagram

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\top}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\textstyle{\BGamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\big(\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\big)\circ\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\big)^{-1}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

is a pullback diagram. Because the diagram is isomorphic to the standard pullback with bottom edge given by the morphism
\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})
, showing that the diagram is a pullback is immediate once we know that it commutes. For this we calculate that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{X}{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\right)\circ F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\mu}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\circ\,_{Y}{\mu}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\true}\circ!_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\true}\right)\circ F(\of)\left(!_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\true}\circ\tau_{f}^{\top}\circ F(\of)\left(!_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\true}\circ!_{\,{}_{X}{A}}\circ\tau_{f}^{A}\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\true}\circ!_{\,{}_{X}{A}},

which proves the diagram commutes. Using the pullback observation we made above it follows that

\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})=\tau_{f}^{\Omega}\circ F(\of)\left(\chi(\,_{Y}{\mu})\right)\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{B}\right)^{-1}.

so
\chi(M)
is indeed an
\PC(F)
-morphism. Furthermore, we note immediately that the diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\scriptstyle{!_{A}}
\textstyle{\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\true}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(M)}
\textstyle{\Omega}

commutes in
\PC(F)
, as for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the diagrams

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{!_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\mu}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{!}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\true}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\Omega}}

commute. We also note that because all these diagrams are pullbacks, we get that

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M}
\scriptstyle{!_{A}}
\textstyle{\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\true}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi(M)}
\textstyle{\Omega}

is a pullback in
\PC(F)
. It also follows that the map
\chi(M)
is unique, as any other map will be equal to
\chi(M)
as it will be
X
-wise equal to
\chi(\,_{X}{\mu})
. This proves that
\Omega
is a subobject classifier.
∎

Chapter 4
Functors Between Categories of Pseudocones

As category theorists and mathematicians who make use of category theory, we know that a good knowledge of objects is important but only at most half of the story: to know a category we need to know not just about the objects but also about the
1
-and-higher morphisms. At this point, in Chapters
2
and
3
we have just got to know the main characters of our study: the pseudocone categories

\PC(F)=\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)(\cnst(\One),F).

However we also need to learn and study the ways our main characters interact with and relate to each other; we need to study the morphisms and natural transformations between these categories. While much of the structure can be inherited from the fact that
\Bicat
is a tricategory
1
1

1

As we are working generically with
\Bicat
as determined by bicategories, pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations, and modifications.
(so
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
is in particular a bicategory which, because of the strictness of
\Cscr
and
\fCat
, is actually a
2
-category), we obtain nice descriptions of morphisms and transformations between pseudocone categories. We will also discuss how to translate between pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
and
\PC(E)
both when we have pseudofunctors defined on a common domain
E,F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and when they are instead defined on different domains
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
.

Much of the reason for studying functors and transformations between pseudocone categories comes not just from categorical curiosity, but also from a desire to apply these techniques to equivariant geometric/topological situations and contexts. We would like to apply functors between equivariant categories to various flavours such as:

(1)

Functors which change fibre categories like the inclusions
\Shv_{G}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
or
\Loc_{G}(X)\to\Per_{G}(X)
for a smooth algebraic group
G
and a left
G
-variety
X
.

(2)

Functors which change spaces between fibres like
\Shv_{G}(X)\to\Shv_{G}(Y)
,
\Shv_{G}(X)\to\Shv_{G}(Y)
, or
D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
when
G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
and
Y
are left
G
-varieties, and there is a
G
-equivariant morphism
h:X\to Y
.

(3)

Combining the two changes of domains and fibres at the same time.

(4)

Functors which change groups like
\Shv_{G}(X)\to\Shv_{H}(X)
or
D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{H}^{b}(X)
when there is a morphism
\varphi:H\to G
of smooth algebraic groups.

(5)

The topological analogues of each of the situations above.

We will see below that Flavours (1) and (2) above can be captured by entirely the pseudocone formalism. Flavour (4), however, is ultimately a geometric and topological phenomenon and requires a use of induction spaces in both the variety-theoretic and topological contexts and so is deferred to Chapter
7
.

4.1.
Pseudocone Functors: Change of Fibre

We begin this subsection by studying the most straightforward type of functor between pseudocone categories: the Change of Fibre functors. These will allow us to compare different pseudocone categories over the same base category and lift adjoints to the level of pseudocone categories in terms of having fibrewise adjunctions (cf. Theorem
4.1.17
). We will see that this is arguably a straightforward technical corollary by virtue of the discussion below, but the reason we have called it a theorem is due to the power of its applications. In fact, if we are being somewhat reductionist, much of this subsection can be rephrased as showing that the pseudoconification map
\PC:\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)\to\fCat
induced by
F\mapsto\PC(F)
on objects is a strict
2
-functor.

Perhaps unsurprisingly at this point, we will need to know some things about the tricategory
\Bicat
and its various properties — for explicit and more complete details about this tricategory see
[
29
, Chapter 11]
; what we describe here can largely be seen as a summary of the definitions at the beginning of
[
29
, Chapter 11.3]
. So far we have only used the fact that for any bicategories
\Cfrak,\Dfrak
and for any two pseudofunctors
F,G:\Cfrak\to\Dfrak
we have a category of transformations and modifications
\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Dfrak)(F,G)
because
\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Dfrak)
is a bicategory. The structure of
\Bicat
is furnished so that the composition of
1
-morphisms in the bicategory
\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Dfrak)
is the vertical composition of the pseudonatural transformations while the composition pseudofunctors

\ast:\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Dfrak)\times\Bicat(\Dfrak,\Afrak)\to\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Afrak)

describe the horizontal composition of pseudonatural transformations and pseudofunctors. The horizontal composition of pseudofunctors is the usual composition

\ast(F,G):=G\circ F

but the real magic comes in at the level of
1
-and-
2
-morphisms. For any pseudonatural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfrak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Afrak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

the horizontal composition
\beta\ast\alpha:H\circ F\Rightarrow K\circ G
is determined by

\beta\ast\alpha=\ast(\alpha,\beta);

note that
\beta\ast\alpha
is defined via the whiskering equation

\beta\ast\alpha:=(K\ast\alpha)\circ(\beta\ast F).

We can perform a similar construction with modifications. If we have the diagram below of bicategories, pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations, and modifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Efrak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\underset{\rho}{\Rrightarrow}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\underset{\varphi}{\Rrightarrow}

then we define the modification

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cfrak}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Efrak}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\circ F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\ast\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\ast\alpha}
\underset{\varphi\ast\rho}{\Rrightarrow}

as follows: for any object
X\in\Cfrak_{0}
we have the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G\circ F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H\circ F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H\circ E)(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{FX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{FX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\alpha_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\gamma_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{FX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\rho_{X})}

and so we take the resulting
2
-morphism component
(\varphi\ast\rho)_{X}
to be the horizontal composite

(\varphi\ast\rho)_{X}:=H(\rho_{X})\ast\varphi_{FX}.

We will see below that these give us a clean construction of the functors
\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
for
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and natural transformations between them. Later we will also investigate the
2
-morphisms in
\Bicat(\Cfrak,\Dfrak)
and use those modifications to build adjunctions and natural transformations between pseudocone categories.

Let us proceed to actually apply these bicategorical observations to our pseudocone categories. Below we show that if we have a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

then post-composition by
\alpha
at the
2
-cell level and post-whiskering by
\alpha
at the
3
-cell level gives rise to a functor
\underline{{\alpha}}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
. While this is immediate by construction of the bicategory
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
, because of our interest in applications to algebraic geometry and algebraic topology and using the pseudocone formalism for practical calculations, we want to be more explicit than just having the abstract construction at hand. Consequently we also give an explicit description of how this transforms objects and morphisms as well as their transition isomorphisms; this will be especially useful when studying when the functor
\underline{{\alpha}}
preserves limits and colimits.

Theorem 4.1.1
.

Let
\Cscr
be a category and let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pair of pseudofunctors for which there is a pseudonatural transformation
\alpha:F\to E
. Then there is an induced functor
\underline{{\alpha}}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
induced by the vertical post-composition functor

\underline{{\alpha}}=\alpha\circ(-):\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)(\cnst(\One),F)\to\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)(\cnst(\One),E).

Explicitly, if
(A,T_{A})\in\PC(F)_{0}
then

\underline{{\alpha}}(A)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0},\,_{X}{A}\in A\right\}

and

T_{\underline{{\alpha}}A}:=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\;:\;f\in\Cscr_{1},f:X\to Y\right\}

where
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
is the natural isomorphism

\,{}_{f}{\alpha}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)\xRightarrow{\cong}E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha}.

Similarly, if
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
then

\underline{{\alpha}}P=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Proof.

The statement that the post-composition functor gives the induced functor
\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is semantics and omitted. The meat of what is to be shown here is that the objects/morphisms carry the explicit forms claimed above.

Because on one hand the vertical composition

\underline{{\alpha}}A=\alpha\circ(A,T_{A})=(\underline{{\alpha}}(A),T_{\underline{{\alpha}}A})

has object component

\underline{{\alpha}}(A)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

we only need to determine the transition isomorphisms
T_{\underline{{\alpha}}A}
. By the convention discussed in Remark
2.3.5
and the definition of the vertical composite
\alpha\circ(A,T_{A})
we get that

\tau_{f}^{\underline{{\alpha}}A}=\left(\,{}_{f}{(\alpha\circ(A,T_{A}))}\right)^{-1}=\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{f}{(A,T_{A})}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha}\right)^{-1}.

The check that this indeed gives a pseudonatural transformation (and that we did not, in fact, make an error in defining the transition isomorphisms) follows from the fact that the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
implies that if we have any composable pair of morphisms
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{h}Z

E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{g}^{A}\right)\right)=\,_{f}{\alpha}_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{\tau_{g}^{A}}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha}_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}\right)^{-1}.

This allows us to derive that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(X)\tau_{g}^{\underline{\alpha}A}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\tau_{g}^{A})\circ\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\tau_{g}^{A})\big)\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(X)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)

\displaystyle\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(F(X)\tau_{g}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\circ F(X)\tau_{g}^{A}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A}\circ\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

We again use the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
to obtain the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)\big(E(g)\big(\,_{Z}{\alpha}(\,_{Z}{A})\big)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(F(g)(\,_{Z}{A}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(X)(F(g)(\,_{Z}{A}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(g\circ f)\big(\,_{Z}{\alpha}(\,_{Z}{A})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(g\circ X)(\,_{Z}{A})\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{\phi_{f,g}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g\circ f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}^{-1}}

and substitute the induced equation to get

\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{F(g)\,_{Z}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{g}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{g\circ f}^{A})\circ\big(\,_{g\circ f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Z}{A}}}\big)^{-1}\circ\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}=\tau_{g\circ f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\circ\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}.

This shows that
\tau_{g\circ f}^{\underline{{\alpha}}A}\circ\,_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}=\tau_{f}^{\underline{{\alpha}}A}\circ E(f)(\tau_{g}^{\underline{{\alpha}}A})
and hence that
\underline{{\alpha}}(A)
has the form claimed in the statement of the theorem.

The statement regarding the description of morphisms follows from untangling the combinatorial description of the whiskering of the
2
-morphism
P
by the
1
-morphism
\alpha
in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. Explicitly we find

\underline{{\alpha}}(P)=\alpha\circ P=\alpha\ast P

and so the description

\alpha\ast P=\underline{{\alpha}}P=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

follows. That this is indeed a modification (and hence a morphism in
\PC(E)
) is either a straightforward check using the identity

E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{Y}{\rho}\right)\right)=\,_{f}{\alpha}_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha}_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}\right)^{-1}

which allows us to deduce

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(X)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{\rho})\big)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\circ\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(X)\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(X)\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(X)\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{B}\circ F(X)\,_{Y}{\rho}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{\rho})\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{\rho})\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}

which is what was to be shown.
∎

We’ll use functors of this format enough to justify naming them.

Definition 4.1.2
.

Let
\Cscr
be a category and let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors. A Change of Fibre functor
G:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is a functor such that
G
is naturally isomorphic to a post-composition functor
\underline{{\alpha}}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
for a pseudonatural transformation
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
.

We now move on to prove the first key technical result about certain cases when change of fibre functors preserve limits of fixed shape. These will let us prove that under natural
2
2

2

In the sense of “occurring in (mathematical) nature.”
conditions (and in particular the conditions we will be considering in this paper) the functors induced by pseudofunctors either preserve all limits of a specific shape (cf. Corollary
4.1.4
) or preserve all limits or colimits (cf. Corollary
4.1.5
). We use these in turn to establish when certain pseudocone functors are additive (cf. Corollary
4.1.6
) which is important in practice for doing homological algebra with pseudocone categories.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors such that for a fixed index category
I
the hypotheses of Theorem
3.1.3
are satisfied for
F
and
E
for diagram functors
d_{F}:I\to\PC(F),d_{E}:I\to\PC(E)
, and for all
C\in\Cscr_{0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{d_{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\imath}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{F}}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{d_{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\imath}_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}^{E}}

Assume furthermore that
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
is a pseudonatural transformation such that each functor
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
preserves the limit over each diagram
\,{}_{X}{d_{F}}
, i.e., the limit of
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ\,_{X}{d_{F}}
exists in
E(X)
and is isomorphic to
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
applied to the limit of
\,{}_{X}{d_{F}}
. Then
\underline{\alpha}
preserves the limit of the induced diagram
d_{F}
in
\PC(E)
.

Proof.

Appealing to Theorem
3.1.3
gives that
\lim(d_{F})
exists in
\PC(F)
and is given by the
X
-wise limit of the diagrams
\,{}_{X}{d_{F}}
; appealing to Theorem
3.1.3
again gives that the limit of
\underline{\alpha}\circ d_{F}
exists in
\PC(E)
and is given
X
-locally as

\lim(\underline{\alpha}\circ d_{F})=\{\lim(\,_{X}{\alpha}\circ\,_{X}{d_{F}})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

As such, to prove that
\underline{\alpha}
preserves the limit of
d_{F}
, we only need to verify that there is an isomorphism

\underline{\alpha}\left(\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}d_{F}(i)\right)\cong\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}\left(\underline{\alpha}\circ d_{F}\right)(i)

in
\PC(E)
.

To prove the existence of the isomorphism claimed above, for all
i\in I_{0}
let

A_{i}:=d_{F}(i)=\{\,_{X}{A_{i}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}=\{\,_{X}{d_{F}(i)}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

and let

A:=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}A_{i}=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}d_{F}(i)=\left\{\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

To construct the isomorphism

\underline{\alpha}\left(\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}d_{F}(i)\right)\cong\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}\left(\underline{\alpha}\circ d_{F}\right)(i)

we begin by defining the witness isomorphisms induced by the limit preservation of the
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
be given by

\,{}_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\right)\to\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\longleftarrow\\
i\in I_{0}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}\right).

and then define the collection

\Theta:=\{\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Because each of the
\,{}_{X}{\theta}
and
\tau_{f}^{-}
maps are isomorphisms, we only need to verify that
\Theta
is a
\PC(E)
morphism to prove the proposition, i.e., we need to check that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\lim(\,_{X}{A_{i}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim(\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{A_{i}}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{\lim\underline{\alpha}A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}}

commutes.

Recall first that
\,{}_{E}{\theta_{f}}
is the witness isomorphism

\,{}_{E}{\theta_{f}}:E(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{\alpha}\circ\,_{Y}{d_{F}})\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}\lim\big(E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha}\circ\,_{Y}{d_{F}}\big),

and similarly for
\,{}_{F}{\theta_{f}}:F(f)\big(\lim(\,_{X}{d_{F}})\big)\xrightarrow{\cong}\lim\big(F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{d_{F}}\big)
. By Theorem
4.1.1
we have that

\,{}_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}.

Note that by Theorem
3.1.3
, we also have

\tau_{f}^{\lim\underline{\alpha}A_{i}}=\lim\big(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A_{i}}\big)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}

so it follows that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\lim\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}

\displaystyle=\lim\big(\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\big)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}.

From the uniqueness of morphisms (isomorphisms in this case) induced by the universal property of a limit, we derive that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\lim(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\lim(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\lim(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\,{}_{E}{\theta_{f}}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\right)}

commutes in
E(X)
. Substituting the induced identity into the equation above gives that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\lim\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}

\displaystyle=\lim\big(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A_{i}}\big)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}

\displaystyle=\lim(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A_{i}})\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}\circ\left(\,{}_{E}{\theta_{f}}\right)^{-1}\circ\lim\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}

\displaystyle\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A_{i}})\circ\lim\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A_{i}}\circ\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\circ\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\circ\left(\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}{f}\right)^{-1}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}

\displaystyle\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\lim\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}.

We now use the uniqueness of limit cones to give the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\lim(F(f)(\,_{X}{A_{i}}))\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A_{i}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\lim(\,_{X}{A_{i}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\big(\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{X}{A_{i}}\big)\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\lim\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lim\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}}

in
E({X})
. This allows us to further derive that

\displaystyle\lim\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}})\right)\circ\,_{X}{\theta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\lim\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\lim\big(\tau_{f}^{A_{i}}\big)\circ\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}.

Thus it follows that
\tau_{f}^{\lim\underline{\alpha}A}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\theta_{\,{}_{Y}{A_{i}}}}=\,_{X}{\theta_{\,{}_{X}{A_{i}}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}
, which in turn proves that
\Theta
is a morphism in
\PC(E)
.
∎

Corollary 4.1.4
.

Assume that
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors such that each fibre category
F(X)
and
E(X)
admit all limits of shape
I
and that each fibre functor
F(f)
and
E(f)
preserves these limits. Assume furthermore that
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
is a pseudonatural transformation whose fibre-transition functors preserve limits of shape
I
. Then
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
preserves all limits of shape
I
in
\PC(F)
. Dually, if
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors such that each fibre category
F(X)
and
E(X)
admit all colimits of shape
I
and that each fibre functor
F(f)
and
E(f)
preserves these colimits. Assume furthermore that
\alpha
is a pseudonatural transformation whose fibre-transition functors preserve colimits of shape
I
. Then
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
preserves all colimits of shape
I
in
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

Simply use Proposition
4.1.3
for every limit to every diagram over the index category
I
to give the result. To prove this for colimits, dualize Proposition
4.1.3
and proceed mutatis mutandis.
∎

Corollary 4.1.5
.

Assume that
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors such that each fibre category
F(X)
and
E(X)
is complete and for which each fibre functor
F(f)
and
E(f)
is continuous, i.e., preserves limits. If
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
is a pseudonatural transformation whose fibre-transition functors are all continuous, then
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is continuous. Dually, if
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors such that each fibre category
F(X)
and
E(X)
is cocomplete and for which each fibre functor
F(f)
and
E(f)
is cocontinuous and if
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
is a pseudonatural transformation whose fibre-transition functors are all cocontinuous, then
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is cocontinuous

Corollary 4.1.6
.

Assume that
F
and
E
are additive pre-equivariant pseudofunctors and that each fibre-transition functor
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}:F(X)\to E(X)
is an additive functor. Then the functor
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is an additive functor of additive categories.

We proceed with a lonely proposition that shows that monoidal pseudonatural transformations give rise to monoidal equivariant functors. However, we first recall what it means to have a natural transformation be monoidal.

Definition 4.1.7
.

Let
(\Cscr,\otimes,I)
and
(\Dscr,\boxtimes,J)
be monoidal categories with monoidal functors
F,G:\Cscr\to\Dscr
. A natural transformation
\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
is monoidal if and only if the following hold:

•

Given the unit preservation isomorphisms
\gamma:F(I)\xrightarrow{\cong}J
and
\widetilde{\gamma}:F(I)\to J
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\gamma}}

commutes in
\Dscr
;

•

For any objects
A,B\in\Cscr_{0}
, given the tensor preservation isomorphisms

\theta_{F}^{A,B}:F(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\cong}FA\boxtimes FB

and

\theta_{G}^{A,B}:G(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\cong}GA\boxtimes GB,

the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FA\boxtimes FB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GA\boxtimes GB}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A\otimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{F}^{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A}\boxtimes\alpha_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{G}^{A,B}}

commutes in
\Dscr
.

Proposition 4.1.8
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be monoidal pseudofunctors over
X
and let
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
be a pseudonatural transformation such that each functor
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
is monoidal and each natural transformation
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
is a monoidal transformation. Then the functor
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is monoidal.

Proof.

For any
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we let
\,{}_{X}{I_{F}},\,_{X}{I_{E}}
be the tensorial units in the categories
F(X)
and
E(X)
, respectively; write

\,{}_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{I_{F}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{I_{E}}

for the unit preservation isomorphisms induced by the monoidal functors
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
; let
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
be arbitrary; and as in Theorem
3.2.3
, let

\sigma_{f}:F(f)(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\to\,_{X}{I_{F}}

and

\sigma_{f}^{\prime}:E(f)(\,_{Y}{I_{E}})\to\,_{X}{I_{E}}

be the unit preservation isomorphisms induced by the monoidal functors
F(f)
and
E(f)
. Similarly, let

\,{}_{F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}:F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\left(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\right)\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\left(F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\right)

and

\,{}_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}:\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}:E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\underset{E}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\left(E(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\left(E(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\right)

be the tensor preservation isomorphisms induced by the monoidal functors
F(f)
and
E(f)
, respectively, where we write
\otimes_{E}^{X}
and
\otimes_{F}^{X}
as the tensor functors of
E(X)
and
F(X)
, respectively. Finally let

\,{}_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{B}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\right)

be the tensor preservation isomorphism induced by
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
.

We first prove that
\underline{\alpha}
preserves the unit object. Let
I_{F}:=\{\,_{X}{I_{F}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
be the object set of the unit object
(I_{F},T_{I_{F}})
in
F_{G}(X)
; note that by Theorem
3.2.3
,
T_{I_{F}}=\{\sigma_{f}\;|\;f\in\Sf(G)_{1}\}
. Similarly, let
I_{E}:=\{\,_{X}{I_{E}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
be the object set of the unit object
(I_{E},T_{I_{E}})
in
E_{G}(X)
; as before,
T_{I_{E}}=\{\sigma_{f}^{\prime}\;|\;f\in\Sf(G)_{1}\}
. Define the collection of morphisms
P
by

P:=\{\,_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{I_{F}})\to\,_{X}{I_{E}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Because each of the
\,{}_{X}{\rho}
is an isomorphism, if we can prove that
P
is a
E_{G}(X)
-morphism, we will have proved that
\underline{\alpha}
preserves unit objects up to isomorphism. To this end fix an
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
and write
f:X\to Y
. We must show that the diagram

\textstyle{E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E(f\,_{Y}{\rho})}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}I_{F}}}
\textstyle{E(f)(\,_{Y}{I_{E}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{I_{E}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{I_{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{I_{E}}}

commutes. For this observe that the unit preservation isomorphism

\gamma:\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\big)\to\,_{X}{I_{E}}

has the form

\gamma=\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\sigma_{f})=\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{I_{F}})

while the unit preservation isomorphism
\widetilde{\gamma}:E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\big)\to\,_{X}{I_{E}}
takes the form

\widetilde{\gamma}=\sigma_{f}^{\prime}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}=\tau_{f}^{I_{E}}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}.

Using that
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
is a monoidal natural transformation gives that the diagram

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{I_{F}}}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{I_{E}}}
\textstyle{E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{I_{F}})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\gamma}}

commutes. This gives, however, that

\,{}_{X}{\rho}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}I_{F}}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\rho}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{I_{F}})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{I_{F}}}}\right)^{-1}=\gamma\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{I_{F}}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\gamma}\circ\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{I_{F}}}}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{I_{F}}}}\right)^{-1}=\widetilde{\gamma}=\tau_{f}^{I_{E}}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}.

Thus
P
is a morphism in
E_{G}(X)
and hence
\underline{\alpha}
preserves the unit object.
We now prove that
\underline{\alpha}
preserves the tensor functors up to natural isomorphism. For this fix objects
A,B\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
and consider the isomorphisms, for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
,

\,{}_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{B}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\right).

Define the collection
\Theta_{A,B}:\underline{\alpha}(A\otimes_{F}B)\to\underline{\alpha}A\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}B
by

\Theta_{A,B}:=\left\{\,{}_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}^{A,B}}\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Note that because each of the
\,{}_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}
are natural isomorphisms, if we can prove that
\Theta_{A,B}
is a
E_{G}(X)
-morphism, then it will automatically induce a natural isomorphism
\underline{\alpha}\circ\big((-)\otimes_{F}(-)\big)\Rightarrow\underline{\alpha}(-)\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}(-)
. Thus to complete the proof of the proposition it suffices to prove that the diagram

\textstyle{E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E(f)\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{Y}}^{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}(A\otimes_{F}B)}}
\textstyle{E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A})\underset{E}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{B})\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}B}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{B}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{A})\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{B})}

commutes. For this we first compute that by Theorems
3.2.3
and
4.1.1
we have one hand

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}(A\otimes_{F}B)}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A\otimes_{F}B})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1},

while on the other hand

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}B}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\right)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\alpha}B}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\right)\right)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\alpha}B}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\alpha}B}.

Now observe that the tensor preservation isomorphism

\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\right)\xrightarrow[\,{}_{\alpha\ast F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}]{\cong}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\big)\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\big)\right)

has the form

\,{}_{\alpha\ast F}{\theta_{f}}^{F(f)A,F(f)B}=\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}\big)

while the tensor preservation isomorphism

E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\underset{F}{\overset{Y}{\otimes}}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\right)\xrightarrow[\,{}_{E\ast\alpha}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}]{\cong}\left(E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A})\big)\right)\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\left(E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{B})\big)\right)

has the form

\,{}_{E\ast\alpha}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}=\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(f)\big(\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{Y}}^{A,B}\big),

where in both cases
\alpha\ast F
and
E\ast\alpha
denote the whiskering of
\alpha
by
F
and
E
by
\alpha
, respectively. Note also that since
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
is a monoidal natural isomorphism, so is
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}
. The monoidal transformation
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}^{-1}
also satisfies the identity

\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{E\ast\alpha}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}=\,_{\alpha\ast F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}.

Using these computations and identities in various ways we compute that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(f)\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{Y}}^{A,B}
