\displaystyle=\left(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\right)\circ\,_{E}{\theta_{f}}^{\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(f)\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{Y}}^{A,B}=\left(\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}B}\right)\circ\,_{E\ast\alpha}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{E\ast\alpha}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\right)\circ\,_{\alpha\ast F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\underset{E}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{B})\right)\circ\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{F(f)A,F(f)B}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\underset{F}{\overset{X}{\otimes}}\tau_{f}^{B}\right)\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big(\,_{F}{\theta_{f}}^{A,B}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}\otimes\,_{Y}{B}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}(A\otimes_{F}B)}.

This gives that
\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A\otimes_{E}\underline{\alpha}B}\circ E(f)\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{Y}}^{A,B}=\,_{\alpha}{\theta_{X}}^{A,B}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}(A\otimes_{F}B)}
which proves that
\Theta_{A,B}
is a morphism in
\PC(E)
.
∎

Corollary 4.1.9
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be braided monoidal pseudofunctors over
X
and let
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
be a pseudonatural transformation such that each functor
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
is braided monoidal and each natural transformation
\,{}_{f}{\alpha}
is a monoidal transformation. Then the functor
\underline{\alpha}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
is braided monoidal.

Proof.

By virtue of Proposition
4.1.8
we only need to show that
\underline{{\alpha}}
is braided. For this fix objects
A,B\in\PC(F)_{0}
and note that the assumptions on everything in sight imply that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\underset{F(X)}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\underset{E(X)}{\otimes}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\underset{F(X)}{\otimes}\,_{X}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{B}\right)\underset{E(X)}{\otimes}\,_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{A,B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\left(\,{}_{F(X)}{\beta_{A,B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{E(X)}{\beta_{\underline{{\alpha}}(A),\underline{{\alpha}}{B}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\theta_{B,A}}}

commutes. However, this in turn implies that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}\left(A\underset{\PC(F)}{\otimes}B\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}(A)\underset{\PC(E)}{\otimes}\underline{{\alpha}}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}\left(B\underset{\PC(F)}{\otimes}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}(B)\underset{\PC(E)}{\otimes}\underline{{\alpha}}\left(A\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}(\,_{F}{\beta_{A,B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{E}{\beta_{\underline{{\alpha}}(A),\underline{{\alpha}}(B)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{B,A}}

commutes in
\PC(E)
and hence shows that
\underline{{\alpha}}
is braided.
∎

We now show that if we have a modification between pseudonatural transformations

\eta:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta:F\Rightarrow E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\Cfrak

then there is an induced natural transformation

\underline{{\eta}}:\underline{{\alpha}}\Rightarrow\underline{{\beta}}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E).

For a reminder on the information that goes into a modification of this form, see the definition below.

Definition 4.1.10
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors for some
1
-category
\Cscr
and let
\alpha,\beta:F\Rightarrow E
be pseudonatural transformations. A modification
\eta:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
is an assignment
\eta:\Cscr_{0}\to\fCat_{2}
where each natural transformation
\eta_{A}
fits into a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}}

in
\fCat
such that for all morphisms
g:A\to B
in
\Cscr
, the diagram of functors/natural transformations

\textstyle{\alpha_{A}\circ F(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}\ast\iota_{F(g)}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{g}}
\textstyle{\beta_{A}\circ F(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{g}}
\textstyle{E(g)\circ\alpha_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{E(g)}\ast\eta_{B}}
\textstyle{E(g)\circ\beta_{B}}

commutes (where
\ast
denotes horizontal composition of natural transformations).

Lemma 4.1.11
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\alpha,\beta:F\Rightarrow E
be pseudonatural transformations. If
\eta:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
is a modification, then there exists a natural transformation
\underline{\eta}
fitting into the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\eta}}

given on objects
C=\{\,{C}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
of
\PC(F)
by

\underline{\eta}_{C}:=\{\,_{X}{\eta}_{\,{}_{X}{C}}:\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{C})\to\,_{X}{\beta}(\,_{X}{C})\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}.

Proof.

Before we prove the naturality of
\underline{\eta}
, we must prove that for all objects
A
of
\PC(F)
, the membership
\underline{\eta}_{A}\in\PC(E)(\underline{\alpha}A,\underline{\beta}A)
. That is, for all
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
(implicitly with
f:X\to Y
) we have that

\tau_{f}^{\underline{\beta}A}\circ E(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)=\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}.

To this end recall that by Theorem
4.1.1

\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A}=\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1},\qquad\tau_{f}^{\underline{\beta}A}=\,_{X}{\beta}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1},

and as usual we derive that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\big(\,_{Y}{\beta}(\,_{Y}{A})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\beta}\big(F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}

commutes. Using this and the naturality of
\,{}_{X}{\eta}
gives us that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\underline{\beta}f}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ E(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\,_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\circ\,_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\,_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}=\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\circ\tau_{f}^{\underline{\alpha}A},

which proves that
\underline{\eta}_{A}:\underline{\alpha}A\to\underline{\beta}A
is a
\PC(E)
-morphism.

We now verify the naturality of
\underline{\eta}
. For this let
P=\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}\in\PC(F)(A,B)
. We must prove that
\underline{\eta}_{B}\circ\underline{\alpha}P=\underline{\beta}P\circ\underline{\eta}_{A}
. To see this note for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we get that

\,{}_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\circ\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}\right)=\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{B}}}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{\rho})

which shows the commutativity of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\alpha}P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\eta}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\eta}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\beta}P}

in
\PC(E)
. Thus
\underline{\eta}
is a natural transformation between
\underline{\alpha}
and
\underline{\beta}
.
∎

Example 4.1.12
.

Consider the pseudocone categories
\PC(F)=D^{b}_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})=\PC(E)
induced by the pseudofunctor
D^{b}_{c}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\circ\quo^{\op}:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
and let
\alpha=[1]\circ[2]:D^{b}(-)\Rightarrow D^{b}(-)
and
\beta=[2]\circ[1]:D^{b}(-)\Rightarrow D^{b}(-)
be the degree
1+2
and degree
2+1
shift pseudonatural transformations. Then the natural isomorphisms

\,{}_{\Gamma}{[1]}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{[2]}\xrightarrow[\eta_{\Gamma}]{\cong}\,_{\Gamma}{[2]}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{1}

assemble to a modification
\eta:[2]\circ[1]\Rightarrow[1]\circ[2]
which gives the natural isomorphism witnessing that
[2]\circ[1]\cong[1]\circ[2]
.

We now show that the various constructions we performed on
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
are strictly
2
-functorial in the sense that they give rise to a strict
2
-functor
\PC:\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)\to\fCat
which we call pseudconification. This, combined with the well-known Lemma
4.1.15
below (which we reprove here for the sake of completeness) allows us to lift adjoints in the bicategory
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
to
\fCat
, which is our next main goal.

Lemma 4.1.13
.

There is a strict
2
-functor

\PC:\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)\to\fCat

which sends pseudofunctors
F
to their pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
, sends pseudonatural transformations
\alpha:F\to E
to their induced functor
\underline{\alpha}=:\PC(\alpha)
, and sends modifications
\eta
to their induced natural transformations
\underline{\eta}=:\PC(\eta)
.

Proof.

Because the
2
-functor is claimed to be strict, we have
\phi_{f,g}=\id_{\PC(f,g)}
for all composable morphisms
f
and
g
. Consequently we must verify that
\PC
strictly preserves identity transformations, composition, and vertical composition of modifications.

We fist note that the facts identity pseudonatural transformations and identity modifications get sent to the corresponding identity functors and identity natural transformations, i.e., that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}

gets sent to

\id_{\PC(F)}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\underset{\iota_{\alpha}}{\Rrightarrow}

gets sent to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\underline{{\alpha}}}}

for all relevant
F
and
\alpha
are both immediate from construction. To see that (vertical) composition of pseudonatural transformations is preserved, let
\alpha:F\to E
and
\beta:E\to L
be pseudonatural transformations. Then the composite
\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)
is given on objects
(A,T_{A})
and satisfies the equations

\displaystyle\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)A

\displaystyle=\{\,_{X}{(\beta\circ\alpha)}\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}=\{(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha})\,_{X}{A}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

\displaystyle=(\PC({\beta})\circ\PC({\alpha}))A.

We also now need to prove that
\tau_{f}^{(\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))A}=\tau_{f}^{\PC(\beta)(\PC(\alpha)A)}
for all morphisms
f\in\Cscr_{1}
. For this let
f:X\to Y
. We now compute that on one hand

\tau_{f}^{(\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))A}=\,_{X}{\beta\circ\alpha}(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}=\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\big)\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}.

On the other hand we get from the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
and
\beta
that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\PC(\beta)(\PC(\alpha)A)}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\tau_{f}^{\PC(\alpha)A}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{\PC(\alpha)A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\big)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A})}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\big)\right)\circ\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\beta_{\,{}_{Y}{\alpha}(\,_{Y}{A})}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\beta}\left(\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\big(\tau_{f}^{A}\big)\right)\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{\alpha_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{(\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))A}

which proves that
\tau_{f}^{\PC(\beta)(\PC(\alpha)A)}=\tau_{f}^{(\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))A}
. Thus it follows that

\displaystyle\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)(A,T_{A})

\displaystyle=(\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))(A,T_{A})=((\PC(\beta\circ\alpha))A,T_{\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)A})

\displaystyle=((\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha))A,T_{(\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha))A})

\displaystyle=(\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha))(A,T_{A})=(\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha))(A,T_{A}),

and similarly
\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)(P)=\PC(\beta)\big(\PC(\alpha)P\big)
on morphisms
P
. Thus
\PC
preserves
1
-compositions strictly.

To see that
2
-morphisms work out as expected, let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors with pseudonatural transformations
\alpha,\beta,\gamma:F\Rightarrow E
and modifications
\eta:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
and
\varepsilon:\beta\Rrightarrow\gamma
as in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

in the bicategory
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. We now must check that

\displaystyle\PC(\varepsilon\circ\eta)=\PC\varepsilon\circ\PC\eta.

However, this is routine as for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and for all
A\in\PC(F)_{0}

\,{}_{X}{(\PC(\varepsilon\circ\eta)_{A})}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{(\underline{\varepsilon\circ\eta})_{A}}=\,_{X}{(\varepsilon\circ\eta)_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}=\,_{X}{\varepsilon}_{\,{}_{X}{A}}\circ\,_{X}{\eta_{\,{}_{X}{A}}}=\,_{X}{\underline{\varepsilon}_{A}}\circ\,_{X}{\underline{\eta}_{A}}

\displaystyle=\,_{X}{\PC(\varepsilon)_{A}}\circ\,_{X}{\PC(\eta)_{A}}

so we conclude that
\PC(\varepsilon\circ\eta)=\PC(\varepsilon)\circ\PC(\eta)
, as was desired. The verification for horizontal composition is similar and omitted. Finally, the verification of the last remaining identity is trivial because the compositors
\phi_{\alpha,\beta}:\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha)\Rightarrow\PC(\beta\circ\alpha)
are all identity natural transformations. Thus
\PC
is a strict
2
-functor.
∎

We will now use modifications to describe adjoints that live in the
2
-category
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
in terms of equational data. One benefit of this is that because adjoints in these
2
-categories are given by equational data, they are preserved by any strict
2
-functor. In particular, the strict
2
-functor
\PC
of Lemma
4.1.13
lifts adjoints in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
to adjoints in
\fCat
. We follow the notion of adjunctions defined in a bicategory as given in
[
52
]
; this definition is equivalent to the definition of adjoints given in
[
53
]
, which defines a minimal
2
-category of the syntactic data required of an adjoint pair of functors and then gives an adjunction as a strict
2
-functor from this free-living adjunction category into a
2
-category
\Cfrak
.

Definition 4.1.14

(
[
52
, Defition 2.1.1]
)
.

An adjunction in a bicategory
\Cfrak
is a pair of objects
A
and
B
together with
1
-morphisms
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to A
such that there exist
2
-morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

which satisfy the triangle identities below. The pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

is equal to the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{f}}

and the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is equal to the
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{g}}

We write
f\dashv g
to show that the pair
(A,B,f,g,\eta,\varepsilon)
is an adjunction with
f
the left adjoint of
g
(and
g
the right adjoint of
f
).

Lemma 4.1.15

(
[
52
, Lemma 2.1.3]
)
.

Let
\Cfrak
and
\Dfrak
be bicategories. If
F:\Cfrak\to\Dfrak
is a strict
2
-functor, then
F
preserves any adjoints that exist in
\Cfrak
on the nose.

Proof.

Because
F
is strict, it preserves all equational data on the nose. Thus it sends the triangle identities in
\Cfrak
on
(A,B,f,g,\eta,\varepsilon)
to the triangle identities in
\Dfrak
on
(FA,FB,Ff,Fg,F\eta,F\varepsilon)
.
∎

Corollary 4.1.16
.

The
2
-functor

\PC:\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)\to\fCat

sends adjunctions between in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
to adjunctions between pseudocone categories.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
4.1.15
to the strict
2
-functor
\PC
of Lemma
4.1.13
.
∎

This gives us our first main strategy and source of examples for adjunctions between pseudocone categories: Those that come from modifications. However, we would also like to know when we can take Change of Fibre functors
\PC(\alpha):\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
and
\PC(\beta):\PC(E)\to\PC(F)
which have adjoints
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}:F(X)\to E(X)
, is there an adjunction
\PC(\alpha)\dashv\PC(\beta)
? We prove that, perhaps surprisingly
3
3

3

The surprising part is that if we have pseudonatural transformations
\alpha,\beta
for which for all
X
in
\Cscr_{0}
there is an adjunction
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}
then this is enough to determine the unit and counit modifications
\eta
and
\varepsilon
which witness
\alpha\dashv\beta
in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
.
, this is the case below.

Theorem 4.1.17
.

Let
F,E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\alpha:F\Rightarrow E
and
\beta:E\to F
be pseudonatural transformations. Then if
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}
in
\fCat
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, there is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta}}
\dashv

in the
2
-category
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
. In particular, this gives rise to an adjunction of categories:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(\beta)}
\dashv

Proof.

For all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, let
\,{}_{X}{\eta}
be the unit of adjunction

\,{}_{X}{\eta}:\id_{F(X)}\Rightarrow\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}

and let
\,{}_{X}{\varepsilon}
be the counit of adjuction

\,{}_{X}{\varepsilon}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\circ\,_{X}{\beta}\Rightarrow\id_{E(X)}

We begin by showing that the collection
\eta=\{\,_{X}{\eta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
forms a modification
\eta:\iota_{F}\to\beta\circ\alpha
; proving that
\varepsilon:=\{\alpha\circ\beta\to\iota_{E}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
is a modification is dual and will be omitted.

Let
f\in\Cscr_{1}
be arbitrary and write
f:X\to Y
. We now must show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\big((\,_{Y}{\beta}\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha})\,_{Y}{A}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}

commutes for all
\,{}_{Y}{A}
in
F(\,_{Y}{X})_{0}
. Consider the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\big((\,_{Y}{\beta}\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha})\,_{Y}{A}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)(\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}

By the adjunction
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}
there exists a unique map
\rho:F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\to F(f)\,_{Y}{A}
making the above diagram commute. It is routine to check using the pseudonaturality of
\alpha
and
\beta
, as well as the fact that units of adjunction are given uniquely up to unique isomorphism, that the morphism
\rho
must be the identity on
F(f)\,_{Y}{A}
, giving that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\big((\,_{Y}{\beta}\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha})\,_{Y}{A}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\,_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\big)^{-1}}

commutes. By post-composing the induced equation by
\,{}_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)}
at
F(f)\,_{Y}{A}
we get

\,{}_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\circ\,_{X}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{X}{A}}}

\displaystyle=\,_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\circ\left(\,{}_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)^{-1}\circ F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right)

\displaystyle=F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}\right),

which shows that the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{Y}{\eta_{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}}}}
\textstyle{\big(\,_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\big)F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{f}{(\beta\circ\alpha)_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\eta_{\,{}_{Y}{A}}}}
\textstyle{F(f)\big((\,_{Y}{\beta}\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha})\,_{Y}{A}\big)}

commutes and hence that
\eta
is a modification. That
\varepsilon
is a modification is given dually.

For the triangle identities, consider the pasting diagram in the bicategory

\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

To prove that this glues (or untwists, if you prefer) to the correct diagram, note that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
there is an equivalence of pasting diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\varepsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}}

which in turn shows that the pasting diagram in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
is equal to the
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\alpha}}

The other triangle identity is given dually and omitted. Thus we get that the
6
-tuple
(F,E,\alpha,\beta,\eta,\varepsilon)
describes an adjunction in
\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)
, and hence by Corollary
4.1.16
in
\fCat
as well.
∎

Corollary 4.1.18
.

Let
\PC(\alpha)\dashv\PC(\beta):\PC(F)\dashv\PC(E)
be psuedocone functors such that the adjoints are induced as in Theorem
4.1.17
. Then if the adjoints
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}
are all equivalences of categories, so is
\PC(\alpha)\dashv\PC(\beta)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.1.17
, we have that the adjoint equivalences
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\dashv\,_{X}{\beta}
lift/descend to an adjunction
\PC(\alpha)\dashv\PC(\beta):\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
. From the
X
-local natural isomorphisms
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ\,_{X}{\beta}\cong\id_{E(X)}
and
\,{}_{X}{\beta}\circ\,_{X}{\alpha}\cong\id_{F(X)}
induced by the units and counits of adjunction, we get isomorphisms
\PC(\beta)\circ\PC(\alpha)\cong\id_{\PC(F)}
and
\PC(\alpha)\circ\PC(\beta)\cong\id_{\PC(E)}
induced by the unit and counit of adjunction.
∎

Let us now proceed to examine the fact that Theorem
4.1.17
provides us a clean way to discuss when pseudocone categories are monoidal closed categories, toposes, and other such things because it allows us to deduce the existence of adjoints based on the existence of
X
-local adjoints. In particular, this gives us the necessary tools to provide a categorical foundation for the various adjunctions on
\Bicat(\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op},\fCat)
and/or
\Bicat(\FResl_{H}(Y)^{\op},\fCat)
we need to perform and study equivariant algebraic geometry or equivariant algebraic topology; note in this case
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety while
H
is a topological group and
Y
is a left
H
-space.

Definition 4.1.19
.

Let
F:(\Cscr,\otimes,I)\to(\Dscr,\boxtimes,J)
be a functor between

monoidal closed categories. We say that
F
is monoidal closed if
F
is simultaneously monoidal and closed. Additionally we say that
F
is symmetric monoidal closed if it is simultaneously symmetric monoidal and closed.

Proposition 4.1.20
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a monodial closed pseudofunctor for which each functor
F(f)
is monoidal closed. Then if each category
F(X)
is monoidal closed or symmetric monoidal closed, so is
\PC(F)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.2.3
and Proposition
3.2.7
it follows that in either case we have that
\PC(F)
is monoidal or symmetric monoidal, repsectively. We now prove this lemma by providing the case in which
\PC(F)
is only know to be monoidal; the symmetric monoidal case follows mutatis mutandis. From Theorem
4.1.17
and the fact that we know there are adjoints

\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}(-)\dashv[\,_{X}{A},-]_{X}

for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, it suffices to prove that for any object
A\in\PC(F)_{0}
, the functors
A\otimes(-):\PC(F)\to\PC(F)
and
[A,-]:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)
arise from pseudonatural transformations as in Theorem
4.1.1
. To this end let

\theta_{f}^{A,B}:F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})

and

\xi_{f}^{A,B}:F(f)[\,_{Y}{A},\,_{Y}{B}]_{Y}\xrightarrow{\cong}[F(f)(\,_{Y}{A}),F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})]_{X}

be the preservation isomorphisms.

Fix an object
A\in\PC(F)_{0}
. We then define pseudonatrual transformation
A\otimes(-):F\to F
as follows: For all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, define the functor

\,{}_{X}{(A\otimes(-))}:F(X)\to F(X)

by

\,{}_{X}{(A\otimes(-))}:=\,_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}(-).

Fix a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
. Now observe that on one hand we have that for all
\,{}_{Y}{B}\in F(\,_{Y}{X})_{0}
,

\big(\,_{X}{(A\otimes(-))}\circ F(f)\big)(\,_{Y}{B})=\,_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})

while on the other hand we have

\big(F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{(A\otimes(-))}\big)(\,_{Y}{B})=F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\right).

There is then an isomorphism

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)\,_{Y}{B}}}
\textstyle{F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\overset{X}{\otimes}F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(f)\big(\,_{Y}{A}\overset{Y}{\otimes}\,_{Y}{B}\big)}

which is natural in
\,{}_{Y}{B}
by the bifunctoriality of
\otimes
and the natruality of
\theta_{f}
(and hence its inverse). This determines our natural transformation
\,{}_{f}{(A\otimes(-))}
component of the pseudonatural transformation
A\otimes(-)
; its object assignment is given by

\,{}_{f}{(A\otimes(-))_{\,{}_{Y}{B}}}:=\left(\theta_{f}^{A,B}\right)^{-1}\circ\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)^{-1}\overset{X}{\otimes}\id_{F(f)(\,_{Y}{B})}.

A routine check shows that the collection

A\otimes(-)=\{\,_{X}{(A\otimes(-))},\,_{f}{(A\otimes(-))}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0},f\in\Cscr_{1}\}

determines a pseudonatrual transformation which we identify (through Lemma
4.1.13
) with the functor
A\otimes(-)
.

The fact that
[A,-]
gives rise to a pseudonatural transformation is dual to the argument above. More explicitly, the object functors
\,{}_{X}{[A,-]}
are given by

\,{}_{X}{[A,-]}(\,_{X}{B}):=[\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B}]_{X}

and the natural isomorphism
\,{}_{f}{[A,-]}:\,_{X}{[A,-]}\circ F(f)\Rightarrow F(f)\circ\,_{Y}{[A,-]}
are given by

\,{}_{f}{[A,-]_{-}}:=\left(\xi_{f}^{A,-}\right)^{-1}\circ[\tau_{f}^{A},\id_{F(f)(-)}].

As before, we identify the pseudonatural transformation
[A,-]
with the functor
[A,-]:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)
. Now appealing to Theorem
4.1.17
gives the adjunction
A\otimes(-)\dashv[A,-]
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 4.1.21
.

If
G
is a smooth algebraic group and if
X
is a left
G
-variety then
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
D_{G}^{b}(X)
are symmetric monoidal closed categories.

Proof.

Because each morphism
\overline{f\times\id_{X}}:G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\to G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)
is smooth it follows that each functor
(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}
is monoidal closed (that each functor is monoidal is a standard result in étale cohomology while the closed aspect follows from
[
4
, Equation 4.2.5.1]
). Consequently it follows that
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is symmetric monoidal closed.
∎

Corollary 4.1.22
.

If
G
is a topological group which admits
n
-acyclic free
G
-spaces which are manifolds and if
X
is a left
G
-space then
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is a symmetric monoidal closed category.

Proof.

By the assumptions on
G
it follows that for any
n\in\N
if
M
is a specified
n
-acyclic free
G
-space which is a manifold then the second projection
\pi_{2}:M\times X\to X
is a smooth
n
-acyclic resolution of
X
. From here taking the subcategory
\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)
of
G
-resolutions of
X
generated by the projections
M\times X\to X
, their pullbacks against each other, and the smooth maps between them, we get that there is an equivalence

D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq\PC(D^{b}_{c}(G\backslash(-)):\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat)

by
[
6
, Section 2.4.4]
. However, because every resolution
p^{\ast}:P\to X
in
\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)
is smooth and every morphism
f:P\to Q
between such resolution is smooth, the pullback functors
p^{\ast}
and
f^{\ast}
all commute with the tensor functors
(-)\otimes_{D^{b}(G\backslash P)}(-)
and
[-,-]_{D^{b}(G\backslash P)}
by
[
6
, Section 1.8, Appendix A]
. As such it follows that we induce pseudonatural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\otimes A}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(-))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[A,-]}

for all objects
A\in\PC(D^{b}(G\backslash(-)))_{0}
. We now apply Theorem
4.1.1
to get our tensor and internal hom functors
(-)\otimes A,[A,-]:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
and then apply Theorem
4.1.17
to deduce the desired adjunction
(-)\otimes A\dashv[A,-]
.
∎

Remark 4.1.23
.

This is an alternative, arguably less direct, approach to showing that
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is symmetric monoidal when
G
is a topological group, but the point of our proof is to illustrate that ultimately it is a consequence of the pseudocone formalism that the symmetric monoidal structure exists in the first place. Moreover, by
[
6
, Section 3.1]
this holds whenever
G
is either a linear Lie group (a closed subgroup of
\GL_{n}(\R)
for some
n\in\N
) or whenever
G
is a Lie group with a finite number of connected components. Note that in both cases this implies that the category
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is symmetric monoidal whenever
G
is a finite group.

Corollary 4.1.24
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor such that each category
F(X)
has finite products and each functor
F(f)
preserves these products. Assume further that each category
F(X)
is Cartesian closed and that each fibre functor
F(f)
is a Cartesian closed functor. Then
\PC(F)
is Cartesian closed as well.

Proof.

This is simply Proposition
4.1.20
applied to a Cartesian monoidal closed structure.
∎

This leads us to examine when
\PC(F)
is a topos as determined by pseudofunctors
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
whose fibre categories are toposes and whose fibre functors are inverse images of geometric morphisms.

Proposition 4.1.25
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor such that each category
F(X)
is a topos and each morphism
F(f)
is the pullback of a geometric morphism which preserves the subobject classifier and internal hom functors. Then
\PC(F)
is a topos.

Proof.

Recall that a topos is a category
\Cscr
which has finite limits, is Cartesian closed, and has a subobject classifier (cf.
[
40
]
). By Proposition
3.3.6
,
\PC(F)
has a subobject classifier and by Corollary
3.1.6

\PC(F)
is finitely complete. Finally by Corollary
4.1.24
we have that
\PC(F)
is Cartesian closed. Therefore
\PC(F)
is a topos.
∎

Example 4.1.26
.

An example of such a topos is one whose fibre functors
F(f)
are all the inverse image functors of an atomic geometric morphism. In particular, if each functor
F(f)=f^{\ast}:F(Y)\to F(X)
is an inverse image of an étale geometric morphism
f:F(X)\to F(Y)
, then
\PC(F)
is a topos.

We now close this section with a topic of interest for equivariant homotopy theory (and in particular for a consideration of equivariant homological algebra): pseudocone localization. Our primary motivation for this is to determine in what sense, if any, the equivariant derived category
D^{b}_{G}(X)
arises as a localization of the category of bounded chain complexes
\Ch^{b}_{G}(X)
at some suitable class of quasi-isomorphisms
4
4

4

Or as close as we can get, anyway.
. While it is known that the equivariant derived categories are not derived categories (in the sense that generically it is the case that
D_{G}^{b}(X)\not\simeq Q^{-1}\Shv_{G}^{b}(X)
) we
do
want to know if it is the case that there is a class of morphisms in a pseudocone category which allow us to see
D_{G}^{b}(X)
as a ‘‘pseudolocalization
5
5

5

This term is left vague and undefined primarily to give a sense of what we are looking for.
” as determined by some pseudofunctor and special class of morphisms cut out by the pseudofunctor. More precisely, this is akin to asking if
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a pseudofunctor which takes values in localizations of categories, in what (necessarily weaker) sense can we see
\PC(F)
as a localization? The moderate technical conditions we will impose show when we can essentially localize the fibre categories of a pseudofunctor over the data specified by the categories
F(X)
and the fibre functors
F(f)
.

Begin by letting
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and, for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, let
\,{}_{X}{S}\subseteq\PC(F)_{1}
. Our goal now is to study when we can descend the data of the localizations
\,{}_{X}{S}^{-1}F(X)
through the fibre functors
F(f)
, i.e., when there are functors
(S^{-1}F)(f):(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F(Y)\to(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
which allow us to define a suitable localization
\lambda_{S}:\PC(F)\to S^{-1}\PC(F)
(which we will call a pseudocone localization). The evident condition we use to produce the pseudofunctor
S^{-1}F
is that we need
F(f)
to carry every
\,{}_{Y}{S}
-morphism to a
\,{}_{X}{S}
-morphism; it turns out this is the only structure constraint we need in order to derive the following proposition, which shows that taking localizations in this way gives rise to a pseudofunctor
S^{-1}F
whose fibre categories are all the localizations of the fibre categories of
F
. Subsequently, we show that there is a pseudonatural transformation
\lambda:F\implies S^{-1}F
which realizes the category
S^{-1}\PC(F)
as a pseudocone localization. Finally, after discussing pseudocone localization and the property it has (cf. Propositions
4.1.27
,
4.1.28
and Theorem
4.1.29
), we will compare this with the naïve localization. In particular, we show that the pseudocone localization is not a literal localization (cf. Example
4.1.31
) but does have an analogous universal property when restricted to pseudocone categories and functors and then provide an explicit description of in what precise sense can we see the equivariant derived category as a localization of
\Ch^{b}_{G}(X)
.

Proposition 4.1.27
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor and for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
assume that there is a subclass
\,{}_{X}{S}\subseteq F(X)_{1}
such that for all
f\in\Cscr_{1}
,
F(f)(\,_{Y}{S})\subseteq\,_{X}{S}
where
f:X\to Y
. Then if
S\subseteq\PC(F)_{1}
is the subclass of morphisms

S:=\{P\in\PC(F)_{1}\;|\;\forall\,X\in\Cscr_{0}.\,\forall\,\,_{X}{\rho}\in P.\,\,_{X}{\rho}\in\,_{X}{S}\}

there is a pseudofunctor
S^{-1}F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
which sends objects
X
to
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
and sends morphisms
f\times\id_{X}:X\to Y
to the unique functor
(S^{-1}F)(f)
fitting in the commuting square:

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\,{}_{Y}{S}}}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{S}}}
\textstyle{(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}

Proof.

We first note that the categories
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
all exist by
[
20
, Section 1.1]
. The functor
(S^{-1}F)(f)
exists by first observing that for any
\varphi\in\,_{Y}{S}
since
F(f)(\varphi)\in\,_{X}{S}
,
(\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{S}}\circ F(f))(\varphi)
is an isomorphism in
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
; applying the universal property of the localization to the diagram

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{S}}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\,{}_{Y}{S}}}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}
\textstyle{(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!(SF)^{-1}(f)}

gives the existence of
(S^{-1}F)(f)
.

Note that since the diagram

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}

commutes, we have that
(S^{-1}F)(\overline{\id}_{X})=\id_{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}
. Thus we only need to establish the existence of the compositor natural isomorphisms, for
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
,

\phi_{f,g}:(S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\Rightarrow(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)

and their identities to prove that
S^{-1}F
eixsts. For this fix
f
and
g
as above and consider that because
F
is a pseudofunctor we have natural transformations
\phi_{f,g}
fitting into a pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}

We now recall (cf.
[
8
]
,
[
20
]
, or
[
39
]
) that from the construction of the categories
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
we have that for all
Y\in\Cscr_{0}
,

(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F({Y})_{0}=F({Y})_{0}.

In particular, it follows from the universal property of the localizations and the constructions of the
\lambda
functors that the object assignments of each functor
(S^{-1}F)(f)
,
(S^{-1}F)(g)
, and
(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)
must be the same as the object assignments of
F(f)
,
F(g)
, and
F(g\circ f)
, respectively. Then for any
A\in(\,_{Z}{S})^{-1}F(Z)_{0}
, we have

\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)A=\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)A

(and similarly for
F(g\circ f)
). Thus any natural transformation

\alpha:(S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\Rightarrow(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)

must be a collection of
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
-morphisms

\alpha=\{\alpha_{A}:(F(f)\circ F(g))A\to F(g\circ f)A\;|\;A\in(\,_{Z}{S})^{-1}F(Z)\}.

We now prove that
\phi_{f,g}
extends to a compositor natural isomorphism

S^{-1}\phi_{f,g}:(S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\Rightarrow(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)

where

S^{-1}\phi_{f,g}:=\{\phi_{f,g}^{A}:(F(f)\circ F(g))A\to F(g\circ f)A\;|\;A\in(\,_{Z}{S})^{-1}F(Z)_{0}\}.

That this is an isomorphism for each
A
is trivial from the fact that
\phi_{f,g}
is; as such, we only need to prove the naturality holds, i.e., that for any zig-zag describing a morphism
\alpha:A\to B
in
(\,_{Z}{S})^{-1}F(Z)
, the diagram

\textstyle{\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\alpha}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)\alpha}
\textstyle{\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{B}}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)B}

commutes.

To prove naturality of the square above, let
\alpha
be represented by some zig-zag of the form

\textstyle{A=A_{0}}
\textstyle{A_{1}}
\textstyle{\cdots}
\textstyle{A_{n}}
\textstyle{A_{n+1}=B}
\textstyle{C_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{0}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{n}}

where
\rho_{i}\in F(Z)_{1}
and where
\psi_{i}\in\,_{Z}{S}
for all
0\leq i\leq n
and for
n\in\N
. Note that not only is the argument we will present independent of the representation of
\alpha
, but proving the other cases (where
\alpha
starts with a
\rho_{i}
, where
\alpha
ends with a
\psi_{i}^{-1}
, etc.) follows mutatis mutandis and will be omitted.

By construction, since

\alpha=\prod_{i=0}^{n}\rho_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}

from the universal properties defining all the objects we have that

\displaystyle\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\alpha

\displaystyle=\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}\rho_{i}\circ\psi_{i}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n}\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\rho_{i}\circ\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\psi_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{n}\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\rho_{i}\circ\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)(\psi_{i})^{-1}

and similarly

(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)\alpha=\prod_{i=0}^{n}F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}.

Now note that from the naturality of
\phi_{f,g}
the diagrams

\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{C_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\rho_{i}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}}
\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)A_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i+1}}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)A_{i+1}}

commute in both
F(X)
and
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
for all
0\leq i\leq n
. Similarly, the diagrams

\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{C_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\psi_{i}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g\circ f)\psi_{i}}
\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)A_{i}}

commute in both
F(X)
and
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
for all
0\leq i\leq n
; however, upon inverting both vertical morphisms, we find that the diagrams

\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)C_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{C_{i}}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)C_{i}}
\textstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{f,g}^{A_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)(\psi_{i})^{-1}}
\textstyle{F(g\circ f)A_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}}

must also commute in
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
for all
0\leq i\leq n
. Now observe that

\displaystyle(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)\alpha\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=0}^{n}F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}\right)\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}\right)\circ F(g\circ f)\rho_{0}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{0})^{-1}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}\right)\circ F(g\circ f)\rho_{0}\circ\phi_{f,g}^{C_{0}}

\displaystyle\circ\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)(\psi_{0})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}F(g\circ f)\rho_{i}\circ F(g\circ f)(\psi_{i})^{-1}\right)\circ\phi_{f,g}^{A_{1}}\circ\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\rho_{0}

\displaystyle\circ\big(F(f\circ g)\big)(\psi_{0})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\phi_{f,g}^{A_{n+1}}\circ\prod_{i=0}^{n}\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\rho_{i}\circ\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)(\psi_{i})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\phi_{f,g}^{A_{n+1}}\circ\big(F(f)\circ F(g)\big)\alpha=\phi_{f,g}^{B}\circ\big((S^{-1}F)(f)\circ(S^{-1}F)(g)\big)\alpha.

Thus
S^{-1}\phi_{f,g}
is a natural isomorphism. That it satisfies all the required relations of a compositor follows in a straightforward but tedious calculation using the fact that
\phi_{f,g}
is a compositor, proving that
S^{-1}F
is a pseudofunctor.
∎

Proposition 4.1.28
.

Assume
F
and
S
are as in the statement of Proposition
4.1.27
. There is a pseudonatural transformation
\lambda_{S}:F\Rightarrow S^{-1}F
whose object functor
\,{}_{X}{\lambda}
is given by

\,{}_{X}{\lambda}:=\lambda_{\,{}_{X}{S}}:F(X)\to(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X).

Proof.

Since we have already defined the
\,{}_{X}{\lambda}
, it suffices to define the natural isomorphisms
\,{}_{f}{\lambda}
. For this recall that the morphism
(S^{-1}F)(f)
is defined via the universal property of the localization making the triangle

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{Y}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}
\textstyle{(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!(S^{-1}F)(f)}

commute. However, this says that the diagram

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{Y}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{(\,_{Y}{S})^{-1}F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\textstyle{(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)}

commutes on the nose, so we define
\,{}_{f}{\lambda}
to simply be the equality natural transformation witnessing
\,{}_{X}{\lambda}\circ F(f)=(S^{-1}F)(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\lambda}
. The required identity on pasting diagrams then holds immediately based on the fact that the
\,{}_{f}{\lambda}
are equalities and from how we defined our compositors in the proof of Proposition
4.1.27
.
∎

Theorem 4.1.29
.

Let
F
and
E
be pseudofunctors on
X
and let

S=\{\,_{X}{S}\;|\;\,_{X}{S}\subseteq F(X)_{1},X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

be a collection of morphism classes as in Proposition
4.1.27
. Assume there is a pseudonatural transformation
\alpha:F\to E
such that for all
X\in\Cscr
the functors
\,{}_{X}{\alpha}
make

\,{}_{X}{\alpha}(\,_{X}{S})\subseteq\Iso(E(X))

hold. Then there exists a unique pseudonatural transformation
\eta:S^{-1}F\to E
for which the induced diagram

\textstyle{\PC(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\PC(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\underline{\lambda}_{S}}
\textstyle{\PC(E)}
\textstyle{\PC(S^{-1}F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\,\underline{\eta}}

commutes in
\Cat
.

Proof.

We first construct the functor component of the pseudonatural transformation
\eta
. For this fix a
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and define the functor
\,{}_{X}{\eta}:(S^{-1}F)(X)\to E(X)
as the unique functor filling the commuting diagram:

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E(X)}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\,_{X}{\eta}}

To define the natural isomorphisms
\,{}_{f}{\eta}
, fix a morpshism
f\in\Cscr_{1}
and write
f:X\to Y
. We observe that by assumption we have a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\alpha}}

On one hand, note that the upper arrow in the
2
-cell factors in the strictly commuting square

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\eta}}
\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)}
\textstyle{E(X)}

while on the other hand the bottom arrow in the
2
-cell factors in the strictly commuting square:

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{Y}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E(X)}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{Y}{\eta}}
\textstyle{E(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E(f)}

Combining these gives a pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\alpha}}

It is routine to check that since the square

\textstyle{F(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\textstyle{(S^{-1}F)(X)}

commutes on the nose and the categories
(S^{-1}F)(X)
and
(S^{-1}F)(Y)
arise as localizations, there is a unique (from the fact that the localization arises as a
(2,1)
-colimit) natural isomorphism

\,{}_{f}{\eta}:\,_{X}{\eta}\circ(S^{-1}F)(f)\to E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\eta}

which fits into and factorizes the above pasting diagram as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\eta}}

However, this is exactly the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}\circ(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)\circ\,_{Y}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\eta}}

that we will prove gives the morphism assignment of a pseudonatural transformation.

We now prove the pseudonaturality of
\eta
. For this fix a composable pair
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
. We begin by considering the pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{S^{-1}F}{\phi_{f,g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g\circ f}{\eta}}

A routine, but extremely tedious, check using that
\,{}_{S^{-1}F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{A}=\,_{F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{A}
for all
A\in(S^{-1}F)(Z)_{0}=F(Z)_{0}
, the fact that
\,{}_{f}{\lambda}
is an identity transformation, and the universal property that induced the
\,{}_{f}{\eta}
allows us to translate the above diagram through the pseudonatruality of
\alpha
(much like how we proved
\,{}_{F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{A}=\,_{S^{-1}F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{A}
), and then move back to the localizations to derive that the above pasting diagram is equivalent to the pasting diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Z),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{-1}F)(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}}

Thus we have that
\eta
is a pseudonatural transformation. Finally, we note that
\eta
is even the unique pseudonatural transformation making

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{S^{-1}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\eta}

commute, as both
\,{}_{X}{\eta}
and
\,{}_{f}{\eta}
are induced by the universal properites of
1
-categories. This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Definition 4.1.30
.

A pseudocone localization of a category
\PC(F)
is a pair
(\PC(S^{-1}F),\underline{\lambda}_{S})
where
S\subseteq\PC(F)_{1}
is a collection of morphisms as in Theorem
4.1.29
.

Example 4.1.31
.

This example shows that if we naïvely localize an equivariant category at a class of morphisms, we can lose the equivariant nature of the category. This means in particular that equivariant localization is a delicate process; when localizing and preserving equivariant data, we need to make sure that we localize in a way that respects the descent data we have at hand.

Fix a variety
X
over
\Spec K
, let
G=\Spec K
be the trivial group, and let
R
be a ring for which
a\in R
is not a right zero divisor and
\afrak=Ra
is a left ideal for which
\RMod(R/\afrak,R)=0
and
R/\afrak
is a nonzero simple
R
-module (a specific example is
R=\Z_{p}
and
a=p
). Now consider the pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
given by
F(X)=\Ch(\RMod)
and
F(f)=\id_{\Ch(\RMod)}
. Consider now the complexes
A^{\bullet}
and
B^{\bullet}
in
\Ch(\RMod)
, where
A^{\bullet}
is defined as

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{a}}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

where
\rho_{a}
is the right multiplication by
a
map and
R
appears in degrees
0
and
1
, and
B^{\bullet}
is defined by

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R/\afrak\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots}

where
R/\afrak
appears in degree
0
. It is routine to check that

H^{n}(A^{\bullet})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\,n\neq 0;\\
R/\afrak&\text{if}\,n=0;\end{cases}

and that

H^{n}(B^{\bullet})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\,n\neq 0;\\
R/\afrak&\text{if}\,n=0.\end{cases}

Because
R/\afrak
is nonzero and simple, any nonzero morphism
\varphi^{\bullet}:A^{\bullet}\to B^{\bullet}
extends to a quasi-isomorphism
H^{\ast}(\phi):H^{\ast}(A^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{\ast}(B^{\bullet})
. We take the morphism
\varphi^{\bullet}:A^{\bullet}\to B^{\bullet}

\varphi^{n}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\,n\neq 0;\\
\pi_{\afrak}&\text{if}\,n=0\end{cases}

as our nonzero map, which is a quasi-isomorphism
A^{\bullet}\to B^{\bullet}
.

Now define
\,{}_{X}{Q}\subseteq F(X)_{1}
by
\,{}_{X}{Q}:=\{f\in F(X)_{1}\;|\;f\,\text{is\, a\, quasi-isomorphism}\}
and set
Q:=\{P\in\PC(F)\;|\;\forall\,X\in\Cscr_{0}\forall\,\,_{X}{\rho}\in P,\,_{X}{\rho}\in\}
. Write
(Q^{-1}F)_{G}(X)
for the equivariant localization implied by Theorem
4.1.29
and Definition
4.1.30
and write
Q^{-1}(\PC(F))
for the localization of the category
\PC(F)
at
Q
.

Our goal now is to show that
(Q^{-1}F)_{G}(X)\neq Q^{-1}(\PC(F))
. To show this we need only show that
(Q^{-1}F)_{G}(X)_{0}\neq Q^{-1}(\PC(F))_{0}
. To this end consider the object
(A,T_{A})\in Q^{-1}\PC(F)
given by saying that the
\,{}_{X}{A}\in A
take the form

\,{}_{X}{A}=\begin{cases}A^{\bullet}&\text{if}\,X\neq G;\\
B^{\bullet}&\text{if}\,X=G;\end{cases}

and defining the
\tau_{f}^{A}\in T_{A}
by

\tau_{f}^{A}=\begin{cases}\varphi^{\bullet}:A^{\bullet}\to B^{\bullet}&\text{if}\,\exists\,X\in\Cscr_{0}.f=\nu_{X}:X\to\Spec K;\\
\id_{A^{\bullet}}:A^{\bullet}\to A^{\bullet}&\text{else}.\end{cases}

It is a trivial verification that
(A,T_{A})\in(Q^{-1}F)_{G}(X)_{0}
. However, note that
(A,T_{A})\notin\PC(F)_{0}
because
\varphi^{\bullet}
is a quasi-isomorphism (and hence an isomorphism in
Q^{-1}\Ch(\RMod)
which is not an isomorphism in
\Ch(\RMod)
). As such we have
\PC(Q^{-1}F)_{G}(X)_{0}\neq Q^{-1}(\PC(F))_{0}
and hence
\PC(Q^{-1}F)\neq Q^{-1}(\PC(F))
.

Remark 4.1.32
.

It remains to be characterized when, if the pseudocone localization
\PC(S^{-1}F)
is induced by categories where each localization
(\,_{X}{S})^{-1}F(X)
is induced by a calculus of fractions, if
\PC(S^{-1}F)
is itself an Ore localization of
\PC(F)
in any way. Determining this seems difficult in practice.

Proposition 4.1.33
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety or let
G
be a topological group and let
X
be a left
G
-space. Then in either case we have that

D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq\PC\left(\operatorname{QIso}^{-1}\Ch^{b}\big(G\backslash(-)\big)\right).

Proof.

Recall that in either case we have that
D_{G}^{b}(X)=\PC\left(D_{c}^{b}\big(G\backslash(-)\big)\right)
by Section
2.1
in the geometric case and
D_{G}^{b}(X)=\PC\left(D^{b}\big(G\backslash(-)\big)\right)
in the topological case by Section
2.2
. Now recall that in both the topological and geometric cases we have unique isomorphisms

D_{c}^{b}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{X}\right)\cong\operatorname{QIso}^{-1}\Ch_{c}^{b}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{X}\right)

which vary pseudonaturally in
\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}
due to the universal property of localizations of categories
6
6

6

Depending on how you define the derived category, these isomorphisms could be actually be equalities; however, our approach here is to be representation-agnostic.
. But it then follows the corresponding pseudofunctors
D_{c}^{b}\left(G\backslash(-)\right)
and
\operatorname{QIso}^{-1}\Ch_{c}^{b}\left(G\backslash(-)\right)
are pseudonaturally equivalent and hence by Corollary
4.1.18
we have that
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq\PC\left(\operatorname{QIso}^{-1}\Ch_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(-))\right)
in the geometric case. The topological case follows mutatis mutandis.
∎

4.2.
Pseudocone Functors: Change of Domain

We now discuss when we can give functors which change the domain of a pseudocone category, i.e., functors
\alpha:\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
where
F
and
E
are pseudofunctors in a cospan

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}

for some
1
-categories
\Cscr,\Dscr
. The section prior to this and the strict
2
-functor
\PC:\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)\to\fCat
suggest that we should look for some sort of pseudonatural transformation, or something close to one, between
E
and
F
in order to define a functor
\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
. We will see that this can be done, save with some technical alterations to account for the fact that
E
and
F
have distinct domains and so need not be directly comparable automatically. This section is less abstract and general than its predecessor, but is arguably more important than because it allows us to define the functors between equivariant categories which we think of as “Change of Space” equivariant functors (cf. Corollaries
4.2.7
,
4.2.8
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.8
, for instance) in the geometric and topological situation. For instance, the existence of the functors

h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X),\qquad Rh_{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(Y)

and

h^{!}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X),\qquad Rh_{!}:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(Y)

when there is a
G
-equivariant morphism of varieties
h:X\to Y
(or a
G
-equivariant morphism of topological spaces) follow from the arguments in this section (cf. Corollaries
4.2.7
,
4.2.12
,
4.2.13
,
4.2.14
,
4.2.15
). The definition and conditions we described have appeared briefly and with minimal detail in
[
50
]
, so we present a systematic discussion and explain them in detail here.

To define what are essentially pseudonatural transformations from
F
to
E
, even in spite of the fact that the domains of the pseudofunctors differ, we use a slight parametrization trick to move our pseudocones around. The basic idea is that if we have a functor
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
we can view
\gamma
in some sense as a path from
\Cscr
to
\Dscr
which can allow us to pick out a part of
\fCat
in which the image of the pseudofunctors
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
interact and have a pseudonatural transformation moving between them. While this is ultimately a pseudonatural transformation between
F
and the induced pre-composite pseudofunctor
E\circ\gamma^{\op}:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
, I am of the opinion that the intuition just described is not just helpful but also that the results in this section are useful enough for practical situations that they warrant explicit statements.

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
\Cscr,\Dscr
be categories with
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
a functor and
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
pseudofunctors. We say that
F
admits pseudocone translations of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
along
\gamma
to
E
if
h
is a pseudonatural transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Similarly, we say that
E
has an optranslation along
\gamma
to
F
of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
if there is a pseudonatural transformation:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

When no confusion will arise we will often be brief and simply say that
F
has a translation to
E
(respectively
E
has a translation to
F
) and leave
h
and
\gamma
implicit.

Remark 4.2.2
.

Let
\Cscr,\Dscr
be categories with
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
a functor and
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
pseudofunctors. We now unwrap the definition of what it means to have translations along
\gamma
so as to have an explicit description of the information at hand. That
F
admits pseudocone translations of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
along
\gamma
to
E
means the following hold:

•

For every
X\in\Cscr_{0}
there is a functor
\,{}_{X}{h}:F(X)\to E(\gamma X)
;

•

For every
f\in\Cscr_{1},f:X\to Y
, there is a natural isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

•

For any composable arrows
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma(g\circ f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{E}{\phi_{f,g}}}

is equal to the pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma(g\circ f))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{g\circ f}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{F}{\phi_{f,g}}}

An explicit description of what it means for
E
to have optranslations along
\gamma
to
F
of shape
h
is omitted.

Definition 4.2.3
.

Let
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
be a functor such that if
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors then
F
admits pseudocone translations of shape
\alpha=(\,_{X}{\alpha},\,_{f}{\alpha})
and
\beta=(\,_{X}{\beta},\,_{f}{\beta})
along
\gamma
to
E
or
E
admits optranslations of shape
\alpha,\beta
along
\gamma
. Then we say that there is a descent modification
\rho:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
if
\rho
is a modification fitting in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\underset{\rho}{\Rrightarrow}

or

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\underset{\rho}{\Rrightarrow}

Remark 4.2.4
.

As before, let us unwrap what this means explicitly. Fix categories, functors, and pseudofunctors as in Definition
4.2.3
when
F
admits pseudocone translations of shape
\alpha,\beta
along
\gamma
to
E
. Then
\rho
is determined by a collection of natural transformations

\rho=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{\alpha}\Rightarrow\,_{X}{\beta}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}

such that for any morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
the diagram of functors and natural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\alpha}\circ F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma f)\circ\,_{Y}{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\beta}\circ F(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma f)\circ\,_{Y}{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho}\ast F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\gamma f)\ast\,_{Y}{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{\beta}}

commutes. The case in which
E
admits pseudocone optranslations of shape
\alpha,\beta
along
\gamma
to
F
is similar but omitted.

Because of the descriptions above we immediately get that if
F
admits cone translations to
E
, then there is a functor
\PC(F)\to\PC(E)
as an application of Theorem
4.1.1
; similarly if there are descent modifications from
h
to
k
then there is a natural transformation between. While this is a special case of the material we presented in the subsection prior, because of its application to the various equivariant pushforward/pullback, cohomology, and various
t
-structure functors we present (cf. Chapter
5.1
) in detail here for future use.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

Let
F:\Cscr{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
be a functor. Then if
F
admits pseudocone translations of shape
h
along
\gamma
to
E
there is a functor
\underline{{h}}:\PC(F)\to\PC(E\circ\gamma^{\op})
and if
E
admits optranslations of shape
h
along
\gamma
to
F
then there is a functor
\underline{{h}}:\PC(E\circ\gamma^{\op})\to\PC(F)
. Similarly if
F
admits translations of shape
\alpha
and
\beta
along
\gamma
to
E
and there is a descent modification
\rho:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
then there is a natural transformation
\underline{{\rho}}:\underline{{\alpha}}\Rightarrow\underline{{\beta}}
(respecitvely if
E
admits optranslations of shape
\alpha,\beta
along
\gamma
to
F
and there is a descent modification
\rho:\alpha\Rrightarrow\beta
then there is a natural transformation
\underline{{\rho}}:\underline{{\alpha}}\Rightarrow\underline{{\beta}}
).

Proof.

Apply Lemma
4.1.11
.
∎

We now show a useful proposition which determines when we can get pseudocone translation functors as induced from a natural transformation between subcategory inclusions and a pseudofunctor defined on the larger category. Note that as a corollary we can deduce the existence of the equivariant pullback functors

h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X),\qquad h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

whenever
h:X\to Y
is a
G
-equivariant
7
7

7

The astute reader will note that I have not specified whether or not
G
is a topological group or an algebraic group or whether or not
X
and
Y
are schemes or spaces. In truth it does not matter provided the formalisms are all chosen appropriately: if
G
is an algebraic group then
X
and
Y
are schemes and if
G
is a topological group then
X
and
Y
are topological spaces.
morphism.

Theorem 4.2.6
.

Let
\Cscr\xrightarrow{\varphi}\Escr\xleftarrow{\psi}\Dscr
be a cospan of categories together with a functor
\gamma:\Cscr\to\Dscr
. If there is a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

and a pseudofunctor
F:\Escr^{\op}\to\fCat
then
E\circ\varphi^{\op}
admits pseudocone translations along
\gamma^{\op}
to
F\circ\psi^{\op}
of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
where, for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
,

\,{}_{X}{h}:=F(\alpha_{X}):F(\gamma X)\to F(X)

and for all
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
,

\,{}_{f}{h}:=\phi_{\varphi f,\alpha_{Y}}^{-1}\circ\phi_{\alpha_{X},(\psi\circ\gamma)f}.

Proof.

Note that upon reversing all arrows in each underlying category in the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

we get a corresponding
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\op}}

Taking the pseudofunctor
F:\Escr^{\op}\to\fCat
and applying the post-composition pseudofunctor

F\circ(-):\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\Escr^{\op})\to\Bicat(\Cscr^{\op},\fCat)

then sends the diagram above to a
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast\alpha^{\op}}

The object functors of the pseudonatural transformation
F\ast\alpha^{\op}
are

\,{}_{X}{(F\ast\alpha^{\op})}:=F(\alpha_{X})

and the morphism natural transformations are

\,{}_{f}{(F\ast\alpha^{\op})}:=\phi_{\alpha_{X},(\psi\circ\gamma)f}^{-1}\circ\phi_{\varphi f,\alpha_{Y}}

for objects
X
in
\Cscr
and morphisms
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
. Taking
h:=F\ast\alpha^{\op}
then proves the theorem.
∎

We now present two corollaries/examples of the theorem above in order to show how the equivariant pullback functors exist as a formal consequence of our pseudocone formalism.

Corollary 4.2.7
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X,Y
be left
G
-varieties with
h:X\to Y
a
G
-equivariant morphism. Then there is an equivariant pullback functor

h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and similarly for the non-
\ell
-adic derived categories.

Proof.

The categories
\SfResl_{G}(X)
and
\SfResl_{G}(Y)
are isomorphic via the maps
\Gamma\times X\mapsto\Gamma\times Y
and
\Gamma\times Y\mapsto\Gamma\times X
for all
\Sf(G)
-varieties
\Gamma
8
8

8

Note that we are not saying that there is an isomorphism from
\Gamma\times X
to
\Gamma\times Y
in the category of
G
-varieties but instead simply that the assignment above induces an isomorphism of object collections
\SfResl_{G}(X)_{0}\cong\SfResl_{G}(Y)_{0}
which extends correspondingly to morphism classes.
. Furthermore, both admit
G
-quotient functors to
\Var_{/K}
(one named
\quo_{X}:\SfResl_{G}(X)\to\Var_{/K}
an the other named
\quo_{Y}:\SfResl_{G}(Y)\to\Var_{/K}
) of the forms

\quo_{X}(\Gamma\times X):=G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

and

\quo_{Y}(\Gamma\times Y):=G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y),

respectively (cf. Proposition
6.1.4
; note also the definition of each functor on morphisms is similar and omitted). There is a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

where the structure map

\varphi_{\Gamma\times X}:G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)

is given by
\varphi_{\Gamma\times X}:=\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h}
, where this is the unique map making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h}}
