commute. That this is natural follows from the commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}}

for any morphism
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Sf(G)
. The corollary now follows by applying Theorem
4.2.6
to the pseudofunctor
D^{b}_{c}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}):\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
given by sending a variety to its (constructible)
\ell
-adic derived category and by sending a morphism to its corresponding pullback functor. The non-
\ell
-adic version follows mutatis mutandis.
∎

Corollary 4.2.8
.

Let
G
be a topological group and let
X,Y
be left
G
-spaces with
h:X\to Y
a
G
-equivariant morphism. Then there is an equivariant pullback functor

h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X).

Proof.

This follows mutatis mutandis to the proof of Corollary
4.2.7
.
∎

Much of what remains to discuss in this section is simply an application of the material in Section
4.1
; however, we present it explicitly because of its importance in applications to scheme-theoretic and topological situations. In particular, constructing the pushforward
Rh_{\ast}
, the proper pushforward
Rh_{!}
, and the exceptional pullback
h^{!}
rely on these constructions while the adunctions they satisfy (namely that
h^{\ast}\dashv Rh_{\ast}
and
Rh_{!}\dashv h^{!}
) follow from what we describe below. We will also see later in Section
6.2
that the existence of an “equivariance” isomorphism satisfying the GIT cocycle condtion (cf. Defintion
6.2.16
) exists for pseudocone categories
\PC(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op})
for pseudofunctors
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
and
\quo:\SfResl_{G}(X)\to\Var_{/K}
by applying the formalism we develop here, so it is worth having some technical but precise results displayed explicitly. First we present a proposition which states when the functors
\underline{{\alpha}}
preserve various limits, colimits, and monoidal structures and then describe adjoints between Change of Domain functors.

Proposition 4.2.9
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\gamma:\Cscr^{\op}\to\Dscr^{\op}
. Assume either:

Case 1:

F
admits pseudocone translations along
\gamma
of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
to
E
;

Case 2:

E
has an optranslation along
\gamma
to
F
of shape
k=(\,_{X}{k},\,_{f}{k})
.

Then the following hold in Case 1:

(1)

If
I
is an index category and each functor
\,{}_{X}{h}
preserves all limits of shape
I
then
\underline{{h}}
does as well;

(2)

If
I
is an index category and each functor
\,{}_{X}{h}
preserves all colimits of shape
I
then
\underline{{h}}
does as well;

(3)

If
F
and
E
are (braided) monoidal and each of
\,{}_{X}{h}
and
\,{}_{f}{h}_{\ast}
are (braided) monoidal functors and transformations then
\underline{{h}}
is (braided) monoidal as well;

and analogously for Case 2.

Proof.

Claims (1) and (2) hold in both cases by virtue of Proposition
4.1.3
. The monoidal statement for Claim (3) follows in Cases 1 and 2 follows from Proposition
4.1.8
while the braided monoidal statement follows by applying Corollary
4.1.9
.
∎

As an immediate application of the above proposition we get the corollary below.

Corollary 4.2.10
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\gamma:\Cscr^{\op}\to\Dscr^{\op}
. Assume either:

Case 1:

F
admits pseudocone translations along
\gamma
of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
to
E
;

Case 2:

E
has an optranslation along
\gamma
to
F
of shape
k=(\,_{X}{k},\,_{f}{k})
.

Then in both cases:

•

If the pseudofunctors
F
and
E
are additive and every functor
\,{}_{X}{h}
is additive, then the functor
\underline{{h}}
is additive as well.

•

If every functor
\,{}_{X}{h}
is (left/right/two-sided) exact, so is
\underline{{h}}
.

•

If every functor
\,{}_{X}{h}
is continuous or cocontinuous, so is
\underline{{h}}
.

We now discuss shortly adjunctions which arise between translation functors. As in Theorem
4.1.17
it suffices in all cases to specify adjoint pairs of functors
\,{}_{X}{h}\dashv\,_{X}{k}
which both vary pseudonaturally.

Theorem 4.2.11
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
E:\Dscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be pseudofunctors and let
\gamma:\Cscr^{\op}\to\Dscr^{\op}
. Assume that
F
admits pseudocone translations along
\gamma
of shape
h=(\,_{X}{h},\,_{f}{h})
to
E
and that
E
has an optranslation along
\gamma
to
F
of shape
k=(\,_{X}{k},\,_{f}{k})
. Then if for all objects
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we have adjoints
\,{}_{X}{h}\dashv\,_{X}{k}
there is an adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{h}}}
\dashv

Proof.

The assumptions in the statement of the theorem provide us with pseudonatural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

together with adjoints
\,{}_{X}{h}\dashv\,_{X}{k}
for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
. From here the result follows by applying Theorem
4.1.17
.
∎

We now show some basic results regarding the use of these theorems and illustrate why we explicitly presented them. In particular, we’ll be able to deduce the existence of equivariant pullback and pushforward adjunctions and tensor-hom adjunctions directly from these results. While the existence of the functors we describe is well-known (especially in the geometric and topological cases; cf.
[
6
]
and
[
38
]
, for instance), we present the results more to illustrate how to apply this formalism to situations which need not be the equivariant derived category. For instance, these techniques may be adapted to give rise to an equivariant derived perverse push-pull adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(\Per(X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(\Per(Y))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}Rh_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}Lh^{\ast}}
\dashv

although we do not spell this out explicitly here.

Corollary 4.2.12
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism of
G
-varieties. Then there is an equivariant pushforward functor
Rh_{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
which satisfies the adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\underline{{h}}_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{h}}^{\ast}}
\dashv

Proof.

Consider the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

described in Corollary
4.2.7
. Now define the psuedofunctor
F:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
given by

F(Z):=D^{b}_{c}(Z;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

for objects and on morphisms
f:Z\to W
by

F(f):=f^{\ast}:D^{b}_{c}(W;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D^{b}_{c}(Z;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

Then if
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\Sf(G)
, the morphisms
\overline{f\times\id_{X}}
and
\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}
are smooth by Proposition
6.1.4
. Thus, since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}}

is a pullback diagram which is preserved by taking
G
-quotients, we find that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}}

is a pullback. Consequently the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes up to natural isomorphism by the Smooth Base Change Theorem (which may be applied because all the maps in sight are maps between varieties and hence quasi-compact, finitely presented, and separated, as well as because the maps
\overline{f\times\id_{X}}
and
\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}
are smooth), and this in turn varies pseudonaturally over
\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}
. This gives us the pseudonatural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\quo_{(-)\times Y}^{\op}\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
\,{}_{\Gamma\times X}{h}:=R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{\ast}
for objects
\Gamma\times X
in
\SfResl_{G}(X)
and where
\,{}_{f}{h}
is given as above. This gives us our desired equivariant pushforward
R\underline{{h}}_{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
by simply applying Proposition
4.2.5
. Finally that
R\underline{{h}}_{\ast}
is right adjoint to
\underline{{h}}^{\ast}
follows from the fact that since
\underline{{h}}^{\ast}
is the functor induced by applying Corollary
4.2.7
to the psuedofunctor
F
and because
(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{\ast}\dashv R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{\ast}
for all
\Gamma
, our result follows from Theorem
4.2.11
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.13
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism of
G
-varieties. Then there are exceptional equivariant pullback functors
h^{!}:D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and exceptional equivariant pushforward functors
Rh_{!}:D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
which satisfy the adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{h}}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\underline{{h}}_{!}}
\dashv

Proof.

From the proof of Corollary
4.2.12
we need only show that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h_{!}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h^{!}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

form invertible
2
-cells. However, this follows on one hand from the Smooth Base Change with Compact Support and on the other hand from the fact that smooth pullback commutes with exceptional pullback. Consequently the corollary follows from Theorem
4.2.11
and the adjunctions
R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{!}\cong(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{!}
which hold for all
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.14
.

Let
G
be a topological group such that for every
n\in\N
there is an
n
-acyclic free
G
-space
M
which is a manifold. If
h:X\to Y
is a
G
-equivariant morphism of
G
-spaces then there is an equivariant pullback/pushforward adjunction.

Proof.

By the assumptions on
G
it follows that for any
n\in\N
if
M
is a specified
n
-acyclic free
G
-space which is a manifold then the second projection
\pi_{2}:M\times X\to X
is a smooth (in the sense of
[
6
, Section 1.7]
; cf. also
[
34
, Section 1.2]
— the connection between the two notions follows from the existence of smooth homeomorphisms
(0,1)^{n}\cong\R^{n}
)
n
-acyclic resolution of
X
. From here taking the subcategory
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)
of
G
-resolutions of
X
generated by the projections
M\times X\to X
, their pullbacks against each other, and the smooth maps between them, we get that there is an equivalence

D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq\PC(D^{b}_{c}(G\backslash(-)):\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat)

by
[
6
, Section 2.4.4]
.

We now argue the foundations required in order to define the pullback/pushforward
h^{\ast},h_{\ast}
both hold. Observe that analogous to the scheme-theoretic situation, there is an isomorphism of categories

\gamma:\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(Y)

induced on objects by the assignment
M\times X\mapsto M\times Y
. Moreover the existence of the map
h:X\to Y
, analogously to the scheme-theoretic case, gives rise to a commuting cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(M\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(N\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(M\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(N\times X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{M\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{N\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{M\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{M}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{N}\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{M}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{N}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{N\times X}}

in
\Top
; note here that
M,N\in\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(\ast)
. This allows us to define the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where for all
M\times X\to X
in
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)_{0}
, we have
\alpha_{M\times X}:=\overline{\id_{M}\times h}.

Now consider the
9
9

9

By which we really mean “any equivalent model” and leave the compositors unspecified.
derived category pseudofunctor
D^{b}:\Top^{\op}\to\fCat
. We then get the pullback functor
h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
by using Theorem
4.2.6
applied to the current situation. Alternatively, since we have that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(M\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(M\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(N\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(N\times Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{M}\times h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{N}\times h}}

is a pullback in
\Top
for every map
f
in
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(\ast)
(analgously to how it was in
\Var_{/K}
in the scheme-theoretic case), it follows from the topological Smooth Base Change Theorem (cf.
[
6
, Section 1.8, Appendix A]
) that there is a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times Y))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{N}\times h})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h}}

which varies pseudonaturally in
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)^{\op}
. Assembling these natural isomorphisms together with the fibre functors
h_{M\times X}:=R(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})_{\ast}
gives rise to a psuedonatural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}\circ\quo_{(-)\times X}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}\circ\quo_{{-}\times Y}^{\op}\circ\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{\ast}}

and hence gives us our equivariant pushforward by Theorem
4.1.1
. Finally using the adjoints
(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})^{\ast}\dashv R(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})_{\ast}
for all objects
M\times X
gives us the adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\ast}}
\dashv

by Theorem
4.2.11
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.15
.

Let
G
be a topological group such that for every
n\in\N
there is an
n
-acyclic free
G
-space
M
which is a manifold. If
h:X\to Y
is a
G
-equivariant morphism of
G
-spaces then there is an equivariant exceptional pullback/pushforward adjunction.

Proof.

Follow the proof of Corollary
4.2.14
mutatis mutandis to get the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Top}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

of functors where
\alpha_{M\times X}=\overline{\id_{M}\times h}
and consider the derived category pseudofunctor
D^{b}:\Top^{\op}\to\fCat
. We now claim that there are natural isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times Y))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{N}\times h})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{Rh_{!}}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(N\times X))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times Y))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(G\backslash(M\times X))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\id_{N}\times h})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{f\times\id_{Y}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{f}{h^{!}}}

which vary pseudonaturally in
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)^{\op}
. To see this, we note that the existence of
\,{}_{f}{Rh_{!}}
foll follows from
[
6
, Section 1.8]
, as does the existence of
\,{}_{f}{h^{!}}
. Finally applying
4.2.5
twice gives the existence of the functors
Rh_{!}:D^{b}_{G}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(Y)
and
h^{!}:D^{b}_{G}(Y)\to D^{b}_{G}(X)
; using the adjoints
R(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})_{!}\dashv(\overline{\id_{M}\times h})^{!}
together with Theorem
4.2.11
gives the adjunction:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{!}}
\dashv

∎

Chapter 5
Pseudocone Homological Algebra

We now move to the homological algebraic chapter of the monograph: that of triangulated pseudocone categories and the
t
-structures which arise upon categories. Much of the structure that we describe and study in this section can be seen as an application to some of the results proved about pseudocone categories and their functors which involve additive and Abelian categorical structures. However, it will be important for applications in practice (such as when studying how the equivariant derived category relates to equivariant perverse sheaves, equivariant sheaves, equivariant cohomology, and the relations between each corresponding category and the functors which we can define; cf. Theorems
8.1.17
and
8.1.18
) to have developed the language necessary that will allow us to discuss such homological algebraic situations at a reasonable level of generality. Of particular importance is the theorem below (cf. Theorem
5.1.24
) which says that if
F
is a triangulated pseudofunctor on
X
such that each functor
F(f)
restricts to an appropriate functor
F(f)^{\heartsuit}
between the hearts of the fibre categories
F(X)^{\heartsuit}
, then there is a canonical
t
-structure on
\PC(F)
such that there is an
equality

\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}=\PC(F^{\heartsuit}).

I like to think of this as the “Change of Heart” theorem.
In the journey of describing the triangulations on equivariant categories, we will begin by showing how to take an additive pseudofunctor with suspensions on each fibre category and turn it into a suspended additive pseudocone category.

5.1.
Triangulated Pseudocone Categories

Our study of psuedocone homological algebra begins by describing when, given a pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
, the category
\PC(F)
is triangulated with its triangulation induced by having each fibre category
F(X)
be a triangulated category. Because of this we first need to recall what it means to be a category with suspension, deduce when
\PC(F)
is a category with suspension, and then go on to define triangulated pseudofunctors.

Definition 5.1.1
.

A category with suspension is a pair
(\Cscr,[1])
where
\Cscr
is an additive category and
[1]:\Cscr\to\Cscr
is an autoequivalence. In such a case the functor
[1]
is called the suspension functor.

Remark 5.1.2
.

We have gone with the algebraic-geometric naming convention for the suspension functor in our definition above; algebraic-topological naming coneventions tend to use
\Sigma
for what we have called
[1]
. Occasionally we will use the topological notation, but generically we will use the geometric notation.

Proposition 5.1.3
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be an additive pseudofunctor such that each category
F(X)
is equipped with an autoequivalence
\,{}_{X}{\Sigma}:F(X)\to F(X)
and assume that these equivalences vary pseudonaturally in
\Cscr^{\op}
. Then
(\PC(F),\underline{{\Sigma}})
is a category with suspension where
\Sigma
is the autoequivalence
\Sigma:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)
induced by the
\,{}_{X}{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.1.8
we have that
\PC(F)
is additive and the assumptions in the statement of the proposition imply that the
\,{}_{X}{\Sigma}
are the object functors in a pseudonatural equivalence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

Let
\Omega:F\to F
be an inverse pseudonatural transformation to
\Sigma
and note that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we have an adjunction
\,{}_{X}{\Omega}\dashv\,_{X}{\Sigma}
. Applying Theorem
4.1.17
gives the adjoint
\underline{{\Omega}}\dashv\underline{{\Sigma}}
which is an equivalence by Corollary
4.1.18
. Thus
(\PC(F),\underline{{\Sigma}})
is a category with suspension.
∎

Corollary 5.1.4
.

For any smooth algebraic group
G
and left
G
-variety
X
the shift functors
[1]:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
[1]:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
given on objects by

A[1]:=\left\{\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}[1]_{\Gamma}\;|\;\Gamma\times X\in\SfResl_{G}(X)_{0},\,_{\Gamma}{A}\in A\right\},

where each
[1]_{\Gamma}
is the corresponding shift functor, determine categories with suspension.

Proof.

Checking that the functors
[1]
are well-defined and induced by a pseudonatural transformation is immediate by noting that for any morphism
f\times\id_{X}:\Gamma\times X\to\Gamma^{\prime}\times X
, the corresponding map

\overline{f\times\id_{X}}:G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\to G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)

is smooth so the pullback
(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}
commutes with shift functors up to isomorphism. The corollary now follows from an immediate application of Proposition
5.1.3
.
∎

Remark 5.1.5
.

In the topological case, expressing the existence of
[1]:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
is surprisingly subtle in the non-pseudocone model due to the definition of objects as given in
[
6
]
; you need a notion of locally acyclic base change. This can be done directly when
X
is some sort of manifold or pseudomanifold and
G
is some sort of Lie group, but does hold more generally.

The definitions we present below are the final remaining ingredients we need to be able to prove that if the pseudofunctor
F
is triangulated then so is
\PC(F)
. However, when proving that
\PC(F)
is triangulated we will follow the refinement of the triangulation axioms as developed by May and presented in
[
43
]
as opposed to the original axiomatization of Verdier in
[
61
]
. This refinement has the benefit of having fewer technical conditions to check: May’s refinement only asks us to check Verdier’s axiom TR1, a weaker form of Verdier’s axiom TR2 (we only need to be able to rotate triangles in one direction), and Verdier’s axiom TR4 (the Octahedral Axiom); TR3 (the diagram filling axiom) is entirely redundant. We also follow the naming and numbering of the triangulation axioms of
[
43
]
instead of
[
61
]
.

Definition 5.1.6
.

Let
(\Ascr,[1])
be a category with suspension with autoequivalence
[1]:\Ascr\to\Ascr
. A triangle in
\Ascr
is a triple of morphisms in
\Ascr
of the form:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A[1]}

Two triangles are said to be isomorphic if there is a commuting diagram of the form

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{A[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho[1]}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X[1]}

where
\rho,\varphi,\psi
are all isomorphisms. We will describe triangles either visually as above or as a sextuple
(A,B,C,f,g,h)
.

Definition 5.1.7

(
[
43
]
)
.

A triangulated category is an additive category
\Ascr
with an autoequivalence
[1]:\Ascr\to\Ascr
equipped with a collection of triangles called distinguished triangles which satisfy the following axioms:

T1.

Let
A\in\Ascr_{0}
and let
f:A\to B
be in
\Ascr_{1}
. Then:

–

The triangle
A\xrightarrow{\id}A\xrightarrow{0}0\to A[1]
is distinguished;

–

There exists a distinguished triangle which starts at
f
, i.e., there exist maps
g:B\to C,h:C\to A[1]
such that the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is distinguished in
\Ascr
;

–

Any triangle isomorphic to a distinguished triangle is itself distinguished.

T2.

If the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is distinguished, then so is:

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-f[1]}
\textstyle{B[1]}

T3.

Consider a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}[1]}

where the triangles
(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2})
and
(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2})
are distinguished,
h_{0}=g_{0}\circ f_{0}
, and
k_{2}=f_{1}[1]\circ g_{2}
. If there are morphisms
h_{1}:C\to M
and
h_{2}:M\to A[1]
which make the triangle
(h_{0},h_{1},h_{2})
distinguished, then there exist maps
k_{0}:L\to M
and
k_{1}:M\to N
which make the triangle
(k_{0},k_{1},k_{2})
distinguished and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}[1]}

commute in
\Ascr
. We call such a diagram a braid of triangles.

Definition 5.1.8
.

Let
\Ascr,\Bscr
be triangulated categories and let
F:\Ascr\to\Bscr
be a functor. We say that
F
is triangulated if
F
is additive and sends distinguished triangles in
\Ascr
to distinguished triangles in
\Bscr
.

Definition 5.1.9
.

A pseudofunctor
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is triangulated if for every object
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the category
F(X)
is triangulated and if for every morphism
f\in\Cscr_{1}
the functor
F(f)
is triangulated.

Theorem 5.1.10
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a triangulated pseudofunctor. Then the category
\PC(F)
is a triangulated category with triangulation induced by saying that a triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is distinguished if and only if for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the triangle

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}

is distinguished in
F(\XGamma)
.

Proof.

We begin by verifying Axiom T1 of
[
43
]
. With this in mind let
A\in\PC(F)_{0}
. Then because for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the triangle

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}

is distinguished in
F(\XGamma)
, it follows that the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is as well.

We now verify the next aspect of Axiom T1. Assume that the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is distinguished in
\PC(F)
and that the triangle

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}[1]}

is isomorphic to
(A,B,C,P,\Sigma,\Phi)
. Then for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the triangle

(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B},\,_{X}{C},\,_{X}{\rho},\,_{X}{\sigma},\,_{X}{\varphi})

is distinguished and
(\,_{X}{A}^{\prime},\,_{X}{B}^{\prime},\,_{X}{C^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\rho^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\sigma^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\varphi^{\prime}})
is isomorphic to this triangle as well. Thus the triangles
(\,_{X}{A}^{\prime},\,_{X}{B}^{\prime},\,_{X}{C^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\rho^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\sigma^{\prime}},\,_{X}{\varphi^{\prime}})
are distinguished for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and so we conclude that the triangle

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}[1]}

is distinguished in
\PC(F)
.

We now verify the last item of Axiom T1. Let
P:A\to B
be a morphism in
\PC(F)
and note that we now must find a distinguished triangle in
\PC(F)
which begins at
P
. For this note that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we can find distinguished triangles

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}

in each category
F(\XGamma)
. Now using that each functor
F(\overline{f})
is triangulated allows us to produce the commuting diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{B}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A[1]}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}

in
F(X)
where
f\in\Cscr_{1}
with
f:X\to Y
. Applying
[
43
, Lemmas 2.2, 2.3]
then allows us to deduce the existence of an isomorphism
\alpha:F(f)\,_{Y}{C}\to\,_{X}{C}
which make the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{B}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A[1]}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}

commute. Note that if we can prove that the isomorphisms
\alpha_{f}
satisfy the cocycle condition they automatically make

\Sigma:=\{\,_{X}{\sigma}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

and

\Phi:=\{\,_{X}{\varphi}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}

morphisms in
\PC(F)
. These morphisms can be shown to make the diagrams, for any composable
f,g\in\Cscr_{1}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)F\,_{Y}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)F(\overline{g})\,_{Z}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\overline{g}\circ f)\,_{Z}{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\alpha_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{f,g}^{\,{}_{Z}{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{g\circ f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commute up to a unique invertible
2
-cell in
F(X)
because each morphism
\alpha_{f}
is induced from a pseudocolimit in
F(\XGamma)
; this pseudocolimit exists because
F(X)
is a triangulated category and
\alpha_{f}
is induced by the corresponding pseudouniversal property. As such we invoke the Axiom of Choice to select our isomorphisms
\alpha_{f}
such that for all composable arrows
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
in
\Cscr
the identity
\alpha_{g\circ f}\circ\phi_{f,g}=\alpha_{f}\circ F(f)\alpha_{g}
hold. With this choice of isomorphisms we find that the pair

(\{\,_{X}{C}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\},\{\alpha_{f}\;|\;f\in\Cscr_{1}\})

determines an object with morphisms
\Sigma:B\to C
and
\Phi:C\to A[1]
which make the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{A[1]}

distinguished in
\PC(F)
. This completes the verification of Axiom T1.

We now proceed to verify Axiom T2. Assume that the triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{A[1]}

is distinguished in
\PC(F)
. Then for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the triangle

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\rho}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A[1]}}

is distinguished in
F(\XGamma)
, so applying Axiom T2 in
F(\XGamma)
tells us that the triangle

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\,_{X}{\rho[1]}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{B}[1]}

is distinguished as well. Because this happens for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and these give the corresponding
X
-local descriptions of the objects in
\PC(F)
, we conclude that the triangle

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{A[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-P[1]}
\textstyle{B[1]}

is distinguished in
\PC(F)
. This establishes Axiom T2.

Finally we verify that Axiom T3 (Verdier’s Axiom) holds in
\PC(F)
. Assume that we have a diagram in
\PC(F)
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}[1]}

in
\PC(F)
in which the triangles
(P_{0},P_{1},P_{2})
and
(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2})
are both distinguished. Now assume that there are morphisms
\Phi_{1}:C\to M
and
\Phi_{2}:M\to A[1]
such that the triangle
(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})
is distinguished. We now need to construct morphisms
\Psi_{0}:L\to M
and
\Psi_{1}:M\to N
which make the triangle
(\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
and render the diagram commutative. To go about this, let
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and invoke Axiom T3 for this
X
. We then produce morphisms
\,{}_{X}{\psi_{0}}:\,_{X}{L}\to\,_{X}{M}
and
\,{}_{X}{\psi_{1}}:\,_{X}{M}\to\,_{X}{N}
which make the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{L[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{B}[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{A}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\varphi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\varphi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\sigma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\psi_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\sigma_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\sigma_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\psi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\varphi_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho_{1}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\psi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\rho_{0}[1]}}

commute with the triangle
(\,_{X}{\psi_{0}},\,_{X}{\psi_{1}},\,_{X}{\psi_{2}})
distinguished in addition to the others induced from the distinguished triangles given by assumption. Now using that for any
f\in\Cscr_{1}
the functor
F(f)
is triangulated, together with the fact that each of
P_{1},P_{2},P_{0}[1],\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Phi_{1},
and
\Phi_{2}
are
\PC(F)
-morphisms allows us to deduce via tedious diagram chases that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)\,_{Y}{\psi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\psi_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\psi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

both commute up to a natural isomorphism induced from the
2
-cells that compare any two morphisms which both provide braids of triangles. We once again invoke the Axiom of Choice to redefine the
\,{}_{X}{\psi_{i}}
if any of these natural isomorphisms are not strict identities and replace them with their strict counterparts. This allows us to deduce that
\Psi_{0}:=\{\,_{X}{\psi_{0}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
and
\Psi_{1}:=\{\,_{X}{\psi_{1}}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}
are morphisms which makes the triangle
(\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})
distinguished and renders the briad

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}[1]}

commutative. This completes the verification of the Axiom T3 and finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 5.1.11
.

In the above proof we have had to use the Axiom of Choice to artificially select morphisms to satisfy strictness conditions of our transition isomorphisms. This is largely because we are considering
1
-categorical information (strictly commuting diagrams) of what are inherently
2
-categorical (or
\infty
-categorical) constructions: the diagrams we construct only generically commute only up to coherent isomorphism provided by the pseudouniversal properties of pseudolimits and pseudocolimits. These issues disappear, however, in the
\infty
-categorical formalism, as the lack of strict equality becomes a feature rather than a bug; we record all such coherences and ways of moving between such isomorphisms as various higher morphisms and higher cells. In fact, by working with a notion of pseudocone
\infty
-category (the category defined by, for an
\infty
-pseudofunctor “
F:\mathsf{Ner}(\Cscr^{\op})\to\mathfrak{QCat}
” for
\mathfrak{QCat}
the
(\infty,2)
-category of quasicategories) into the
(\infty,2)
-category of quasi-categories (or “
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\mathscr{K}
” where
\mathscr{K}
is an
\infty
-cosmos with a notion of stable
\infty
-category) whose fibre
\infty
-categories are all stable with triangulated
\infty
-fibre functors, we can sidestep these issues entirely. In such a case we should have that our
(\infty,1)
-category of pseudocones arises as the
(\infty,1)
-category

\PC(F)=(\infty,2)\mathsf{Cat}(\mathsf{Ner}(\Cscr^{\op}),\mathfrak{QCat})(\cnst\One,F)

and most, if not all, of the properties here will follow from taking the corresponding homotopy categories and homotopy
2
-categories. In particular, we expect a result analogous to Proposition
2.3.11
: It should be the case that

\PC(F)\simeq\mathbf{CSect}_{B}(p:\El(F)\to\mathsf{Ner}(\Cscr^{\op}))

for the corresponding
\infty
-categorical elements Cartesian fibration (cf.
[
22
]
). We will not explore these notions here and leave them entirely speculative and as future work for the author; however, such a formalism will naturally lead to a development of an equivariant six functor formalism and pseudocone six functor formalism for pseudocone
\infty
-categories in the style of Mann as well as Scholze (cf.
[
42
, Appendix A.5]
,
[
54
]
) and following the philosophy of
[
21
]
.

An immediate corollary of Theorem
5.1.10
above is that it gives triangulated structures on the equivariant derived categories in the case where
G
is a smooth algebraic group and when
X
is a left
G
-variety.

Corollary 5.1.12
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then the categories
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
D_{G}^{b}(X)
are triangulated.

When
G
is a topological group and
X
is a left
G
-space it is also true that
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is a triangulated category, but we must instead use the proof of
[
6
, Section 2.5]
. However, if we restrict to the following situation we can use Theorem
5.1.10
.

Corollary 5.1.13
.

Let
G
be a topological group such that for every
n\in\N
there is an
n
-acyclic free
G
-space
M
which is a manifold. Then for any left
G
-space
X
the category
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is triangulated.

Proof.

Compare this to the proof of Corollary
4.1.22
. By the assumptions on
G
it follows that for any
n\in\N
if
M
is a specified
n
-acyclic free
G
-space which is a manifold then the second projection
\pi_{2}:M\times X\to X
is a smooth
n
-acyclic resolution of
X
. From here taking the subcategory
\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)
of
G
-resolutions of
X
generated by the projections
M\times X\to X
, their pullbacks against each other, and the smooth maps between them, we get that there is an equivalence

D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq\PC(D^{b}_{c}(G\backslash(-)):\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat)

by
[
6
, Section 2.4.4]
. However, because every resolution
p^{\ast}:P\to X
in
\mathbf{SResl}_{G}(X)
is smooth and every morphism
f:P\to Q
between such resolution is smooth, the pullback functors
p^{\ast}
and
f^{\ast}
all commute with suspension functors
[1]_{X}
. As such we can apply Theorem
5.1.10
and translate the triangulation across the equivalence to deduce the fact that
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is triangulated.
∎

We will need
t
-structures for what follows because arguably the crucial feature of the derived category is the presence of the universal property (as a localization) and the presence of both perverse and standard
t
-structures. As such, we recall their definition here.

Definition 5.1.14

(
[
4
, Definition 1.3.1]
)
.

Let
\Tscr
be a triangulated category. A
t
-structure on
\Tscr
is a pair of full subcategories
(\Tscr^{\leq 0},\Tscr^{\geq 0})
which are both stable under equivalence such that the following are satisfied (where for any
n\in\Z
, we define
\Tscr^{\leq n}:=\Tscr^{\leq 0}[-n]
and
\Tscr^{\geq n}:=\Tscr^{\geq 0}[-n]
):

•

If
A
is an object of
\Tscr^{\geq 1}
and
B
is an object of
\Tscr^{\leq 0}
, then
\Tscr(A,B)=0
;

•

We have inclusions of categories
\Tscr^{\leq 0}\hookrightarrow\Tscr^{\leq 1}
and
\Tscr^{\geq 1}\hookrightarrow\Tscr^{\geq 0}
;

•

For any object
A\in\Tscr_{0}
there exists a truncation distinguished triangle

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X[1]}

where
X
is an object of
\Tscr^{\leq 0}
and
Y
is an object of
\Tscr^{\geq 1}
.

Furthermore, given a
t
-structure
(\Tscr^{\leq 0},\Tscr^{\geq 0})
, we define the heart of
\Tscr
to be the category

\Tscr^{\heartsuit}:=\Tscr^{\leq 0}\cap\Tscr^{\geq 0}.

Example 5.1.15
.

Let
\Ascr
be an Abelian category and let
D(\Ascr)
be the derived category of chain complexes of
\Ascr
. Define the subcategory
D(\Ascr)^{\leq 0}
(respectivly
D(\Ascr)^{\geq 0}
) to be the subcategory of
D(\Ascr)
generated by the complexes
X^{\bullet}
for which
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})=0
for all
n>0
(respectively generated by the complexes
X^{\bullet}
for which
H^{n}(X^{\bullet})=0
for all
n<0
). The pair
(D(\Ascr)^{\leq 0},D(\Ascr)^{\geq 0})
is a
t
-structure on
D(\Ascr)
and its heart is the subcategory of objects whose cohomology is concentrated in degree
0
. In particular, there is an equivalence

D(\Ascr)^{\heartsuit}\simeq\Ascr.

Remark 5.1.16
.

For any
t
-structure on a triangulated category
\Tscr
, the heart
\Tscr^{\heartsuit}
is an Abelian category by
[
4
, Théoréme 1.3.6]
. If there are multiple
t
-structures on a category
\Tscr
, we will denote their corresponding subcategories as
({}^{t}\Tscr^{\leq 0},{}^{t}\Tscr^{\geq 0})
and
({}^{t^{\prime}}\Tscr^{\leq 0},{}^{t^{\prime}}\Tscr^{\geq 0})
while writing the corresponding hearts as
\Tscr^{\heartsuit_{t}}
and
\Tscr^{\heartsuit_{t^{\prime}}}
, respectively.

Definition 5.1.17
.

Let
\Dscr
be a (bounded) derived category of some Abelian category or the bounded derived category of
\ell
-adic sheaves on some variety. We define the standard
t
-structure on
\Dscr
to be the
t
-structure generated as in Example
5.1.15
.

We now show how to build
t
-structures on
\PC(F)
based on
t
-structures that come from those on
F(X)
. After proving the theorem (cf. Theorem
5.1.21
) that says these are indeed
t
-structures on
\PC(F)
, we will then give some explicit examples of these
t
-structures as they may arise in nature (as much as perverse sheaves are natural, anyway). However, before proving this theorem, we will define truncated pseudofunctors, which essentially are triangulated pseudofunctors for which each fibre functor admits a
t
-structure and these
t
-structures vary pseudofunctorially through
F
. With this we will prove how to produce
t
-structures from pseudofunctors and also how a base category
\Tscr
can give rise to
t
-structures on
\PC(F)
.

Definition 5.1.18
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a triangulated pseudofunctor. Assume each fibre category has
t
-structure with truncation functors
(\,_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}},\,_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}})
such that these give rise to pseudofunctors
F^{\leq 0}:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
and
F^{\geq 0}:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
for which the functors
F^{\geq 0}(f)
and
F^{\leq 0}(f)
are
t
-exact functors which coincide on all common subcategories. Then if for all morphisms
f:X\to Y
in
\Cscr
there are natural isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)^{\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq 0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{\leq 0}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq 0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{\geq 0}}

which satisfy the coherence diagrams presented below, we say that the pseudofunctor is truncated. The pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)^{\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)^{\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq 0}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq 0}(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq 0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{F^{\leq 0}}{\phi_{f,g}}}

is equal to the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)^{\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq 0}(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g\circ f}^{\leq 0}}

and dually the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq 0}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq 0}(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq 0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{F}{\phi_{f,g}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{F^{\geq 0}}{\phi_{f,g}}}

is equal to the
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Z)^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{Z}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq 0}(g\circ f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g\circ f}^{\geq 0}}

Proposition 5.1.19
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a truncated pseudofunctor. Then there are truncation functors

\tau^{\leq 0}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F),\qquad\tau^{\geq 0}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F).

The truncation
\tau^{\leq 0}
is defined on objects by

\tau^{\leq 0}\left(\left\{\,{}_{X}{A}\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}\right)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}(\,_{X}{A})\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},

with transition isomorphisms
\tau_{f}^{A\leq 0}
given by the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\,_{Y}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A\leq 0}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)}

and defined on morphisms by

\tau^{\leq 0}\left(\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}\right)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Similarly, the other truncation
\tau^{\geq 0}
is defined on objects by

\tau^{\geq 0}\left(\left\{\,{}_{X}{A}\;:\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}\right)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\},

with transition isomorphisms
\tau_{f}^{A\geq 0}
given by the diagram

\textstyle{F(f)\left(\,{}_{Y}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\,_{Y}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{f}^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}^{A\leq 0}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\left(F(f)\,_{Y}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\left(\tau_{f}^{A}\right)}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)}

and defined on morphisms by

\tau^{\geq 0}\left(\{\,_{X}{\rho}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}\right)=\left\{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{\rho}\right)\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\right\}.

Proof.

The technical conditions of this proposition are formally similar to the tensor functors in Theorem
3.2.3
. As such, proceeding mutatis mutandis as in the proof of Theorem
3.2.3
proves the proposition.
∎

By taking appropriate shifts and setting
\PC(F)^{\leq n}:=\PC(F)^{\leq 0}[-n]
together with
\PC(F)^{\geq n}:=\PC(F)^{\geq 0}[-n]
for
n\in\Z
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.1.20
.

Let
F
be a truncated pseudofunctor on
X
. Then for all
n\in\Z
there are subcategories
\PC(F)^{\geq n}
and
\PC(F)^{\leq n}
of
\PC(F)
equipped with truncation functors
\tau^{\geq n}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)^{\geq n}
and
\tau^{\leq n}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)^{\leq n}
. As usual, these truncation functors satisfy the adjunctions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)^{\geq n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\incl_{\geq n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\geq n}}
\dashv

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F)^{\leq n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\leq n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\incl_{\leq n}}
\dashv

Proof.

The existence of the subcategories
\PC(F)^{\leq n}
and
\PC(F)^{\geq n}
together with the inclusion functors
\incl_{\leq n}:\PC(F)^{\leq n}\to\PC(F)
and
\incl_{\geq n}:\PC(F)^{\geq n}:\PC(F)^{\geq n}\to\PC(F)
follow from Proposition
5.1.19
by taking negative shifts of the subcategories and inclusions of
\PC(F)^{\leq 0}\to\PC(F)
and
\PC(F)^{\geq 0}\to\PC(F)
. The existence of the truncations follow from the fact that
\tau^{\geq n}
and
\tau^{\leq n}
correspond, from the definition of a truncated pseudofunctor and the fact that each fibre functor
F(f)
is
t
-exact, to degree
-n
shifts of the pseudonatural transformations
\tau^{\geq 0}
and
\tau^{\leq 0}
. Consequently they arise from the pseudonatural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\geq n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\geq n}}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\leq n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\leq n}}

From here the adjunctions
\incl_{\geq n}\dashv\tau^{\geq n}
and
\tau^{\leq n}\dashv\incl_{\leq n}
follow from the
X
-local adjunctions
\,{}_{X}{\incl_{\geq n}}\dashv\,_{X}{\tau^{\geq n}}
and
\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq n}}\dashv\,_{X}{\incl_{\leq n}}
together with an application of Theorem
4.1.17
.
∎

Using these truncation functors allows us to deduce that truncated psuedofunctors give rise to
t
-structures on
\PC(F)
.

Theorem 5.1.21
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a truncated pseudofunctor. The truncation functors, when shifted for all
n\in\Z
, give rise to a
t
-structure on
\PC(F)
for which the category of bounded above by degree
0
objects of
\PC(F)
is the
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
while the category of objects of
\PC(F)
bounded below by degree
0
is the category
\PC(F^{\geq 0})
.

Remark 5.1.22
.

In the situation of Theorem
5.1.21
, we will freely interchange the notations

\PC(F^{\leq 0})=\PC(F)^{\leq 0},\qquad\PC(F^{\geq 0})=\PC(F)^{\geq 0}.

depending on context and how we want to think of each category at any given moment.

Proof.

We first prove that we can realize
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
and
\PC(F)
as
full
subcategories of
\PC(F)
; note that they are subcategories has already been shown. For this we observe first that since each category
F(X)^{\leq 0}
and
F(X)^{\geq 0}
is a strictly full subcategory of each fibre category
F(X)
. Now let
A\in\PC(F^{\geq 0})
; the case for
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
follows dually and so is omitted here. Because
A\in\PC(F^{\geq 0})
, for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we have that

\,{}_{X}{A}\in F^{\geq 0}(X)_{0}=\left(F(X)^{\geq 0}\right)_{0}

so in turn each
A\in\PC(F)_{0}
. Now any morphism
P:A\to B
in
\PC(F)
with
A,B\in\PC(F^{\geq 0})_{0}
has
X
-local components

\,{}_{X}{\rho}:\,_{X}{A}\to\,_{X}{B}.

Because
F(X)^{\geq 0}
is full, we get that
\,{}_{X}{\rho}\in(F(X)^{\geq 0})_{1}
, and because this happens for each
X\in\Cscr_{0}
, it follows that
P\in\PC(F^{\geq 0})_{1}
as well. This shows the fullness of
\PC(F^{\geq 0})
; that it is strictly full (i.e., the fact that the subcategory is closed with respect to isomorphisms) follows similarly. We thus conclude that
\PC(F^{\geq 0})
and
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
are both strictly full subcategories of
\PC(F)
.

We now prove that
\PC(F)^{\geq 0}
and
\PC(F)^{\leq 0}
do indeed induce a
t
-structure on
\PC(F)
. For this we recall that the category
\PC(F)^{\geq n}
, for any
n\in\Z
, is induced by the construction on objects

\left(\PC(F)^{\geq n}\right)_{0}=\PC(F)^{\geq 0}[-n]=\left\{A[-n]\;:\;A\in\PC(F^{\geq 0})_{0}\right\}.

We now establish that for any
A\in\PC(F^{\leq 0})
and any
B\in\PC(F)^{\geq 1}
, the hom-set
\PC(F)(A,B)=0
. For this we note that since
A\in(\PC(F)^{\leq 0})_{0}
and
B\in(\PC(F)^{\geq 1})_{0}
, for every
X\in\Cscr
we have that
\,{}_{X}{A}\in(F(X)^{\leq 0})_{0}
and
\,{}_{X}{B}\in(F(X)^{\geq 1})_{0}
. Thus we get that

F(X)(\,_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B})=\left\{\,{}_{X}{0}\right\}

so we calculate that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\{\,_{X}{0}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}\subseteq\PC(F)(A,B)

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}F(X)\left(\,{}_{X}{A},\,_{X}{B}\right)=\bigcup_{X\in\Cscr_{0}}\{\,_{X}{0}\}=\{\,_{X}{0}\;|\;X\in\Cscr_{0}\}=0,

which establishes the claim.

We now prove that the categories
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
and
\PC(F^{\geq 0})
are stable under shifts in the sense that
\PC(F^{\leq-1})
is a subcategory of
\PC(F^{\leq 0})
and
\PC(F^{\geq 0})
is a subcategory of
\PC(F^{\geq-1})
. However, because these identities hold
X
-locally and the shift functors are defined by shifting
X
-locally, this is a trivial observation.

We now prove that for any object
A
that there is a distinguished triangle in
\PC(F)
of the form

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z[1]}

with
Z\in\PC(F^{\leq 0})_{0}
and
Y\in\PC(F^{\geq 1})_{0}
. For this, however, we define
Z:=\tau^{\leq 0}(A)
,
Y:=\tau^{\geq 1}(A)
, and let the morphisms be induced from the
X
-local distinguished triangles

\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\geq 1}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\,{}_{X}{\tau^{\leq 0}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)[1]}}

in each fibre category
F(X)
. However, because each of these triangles are distinguished we get that the triangle

\textstyle{\tau^{\leq 0}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\geq 1}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{\leq 0}(A)[1]}

is distinguished in
\PC(F)
, which in turn completes the proof of the claim and hence the theorem.
∎

Example 5.1.23
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. We now give two different (families of) examples of truncated pseudofunctors
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
by using the pseudofunctor
F=\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({G\backslash(-)};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. Note that in in principle we can give four (families of) examples by using the same
t
-structures on
D_{c}^{b}(-)
and proceeding mutatis mutandis. We can even get six (families of) examples in the topological case by letting
G
be either a (closed) linear subgroup of
\GL_{n}(\R)
for some
n\in\N
or a Lie group with finitely many connected components and letting
X
be a left
G
-space.

The first truncated pseudofunctor we describe fully is the standard
t
-structure on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
(cf. Definition
5.1.17
). For each
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
we say that

F^{\leq 0}(\XGamma):={}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\leq 0}

and

F^{\geq 0}(\XGamma):={}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}

where stand denotes the standard
t
-structure on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and the truncations
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq 0}}
and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq 0}}
are given from the standard
t
-structure on the derived
\ell
-adic category. Then because the morphism
\of
is smooth, the functor
\of^{\ast}
is
t
-exact for the standard
t
-structure and so we set
F^{\leq 0}(\of)=F^{\geq 0}(\of)=\of^{\ast}
. Then the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\of^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\textstyle{{}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\leq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\of^{\ast}}
\textstyle{{}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}

\textstyle{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\of^{\ast}}
\textstyle{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\textstyle{{}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\of^{\ast}}
\textstyle{{}^{\text{stand}}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}}

commute strictly, which implies that in this case the trucantion coherences are satisfied automatically. This is the standard
t
-structure on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and it is routine (cf. Corollary
5.1.27
)to see that
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\text{stand}}}\simeq\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
.

The second truncated pre-equivariant pseudofunctor we construct on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is induced from the perverse
t
-structure. For each
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
we define

F^{\leq 0}(\XGamma)={}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\leq 0}

and

F^{\geq 0}(\XGamma)={}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}.

Now for each morphism
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
let
d_{f}\in\Z
denote the relative (pure) dimension of
\of
. Then because
\of
is smooth, we have from
[
4
, Page 108]
that
\of^{\ast}[d_{f}]
is a
t
-exact functor for the pervese
t
-structure, so we set for all
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}

F^{\geq 0}(\of)=F^{\leq 0}(\of)=\of^{\ast}[d_{f}].

Let each truncation functor
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq 0}},\,_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq 0}}
be truncations for the perverse
t
-structure; we will omit the
{(-)}^{p}
left-handed superscript to reduce notational clutter. Then a routine calculation shows that for any
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\leq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\leq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of^{\ast}[d_{f}]}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGammap};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({\XGamma};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\geq 0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq 0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of^{\ast}[d_{f}]}

commutes up to an invertible
2
-cell because of the
t
-exactness
1
1

1

A functor
F:\Ascr\to\Bscr
of triangulated categories is
t
-exact if it is triangulated,
F(\Ascr^{\leq 0})
is a subcategory of
\Bscr^{\leq 0}
, and
F(\Ascr^{\geq 0})
is a subcategory of
\Bscr^{\geq 0}
.
of
\of^{\ast}[d_{f}]
, where the commutativity isomorphisms are induced based on how we identify and move between pullbacks. This defines the equivariant perverse
t
-structure on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and Theorem
5.1.24
below shows that

(\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell}))^{\heartsuit_{\text{per}}}=\Per_{G}(X).

We now observe that if
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
is a truncated pseudofunctor, because
\PC(F)
admits a
t
-structure the full subcategory
\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}
of
\PC(F)
induced by object assignment

\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}_{0}:=\PC(F)^{\leq 0}_{0}\cap\PC(F)^{\geq 0}_{0}

is an Abelian subcategory of
\PC(F)
with cohomological functor

\tau^{\geq 0}\circ\incl_{\leq 0}\circ\tau^{\leq 0}:\PC(F)\to\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}

by
[
4
, Théorème 1.3.6]
2
2

2

In
[
4
]
the statement listed is that
\tau_{\geq 0}\circ\tau_{\leq 0}
is the cohomological functor; however, because our functors
\tau_{\leq n}
and
\tau_{\geq n}
are typed to have domain
\PC(F)
, we require an inclusion to the category
\PC(F)
for things to type correctly. This will not cause any significant issue for what we do, but is worth pointing out for the curious reader.
. A natural question then is whether or not the category
\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}
is itself equivalent to a category of pseudocones for some pseudofunctor
E:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. We show that this is in fact the case below, and in a surprising way. The categories
F^{\heartsuit}(X)
vary pseudofunctorially in
\Cscr^{\op}
because the fibre functors
F(f)
are
t
-exact and so form a pseudofunctor
F^{\heartsuit}:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
. We show below that it is in fact the case that
\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}=\PC(F^{\heartsuit})
; note the strict equality!

Theorem 5.1.24

(Change of Heart)
.

Let
F:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
be a triangulated pseudofunctor. Define the Abelian pseudofunctor
F^{\heartsuit}:\Cscr^{\op}\to\fCat
by setting

F(X):=F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)=F(X)^{\heartsuit}

and defining the fibre functors by

\left(F^{\geq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f)=:F^{\heartsuit}(f).

Then

\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}=\PC\left(F^{\heartsuit}\right).

Remark 5.1.25
.

By Definition
5.1.9
the definition of the fibre functor
F^{\heartsuit}(f)

\left(F^{\geq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f)=:F^{\heartsuit}(f)

does not depend seriously on
F^{\geq 0}
; we could have also chosen to use
F^{\leq 0}(f)
instead as

\left(F^{\geq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f)=\left(F^{\leq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f).

The decision to use
F^{\geq 0}
was simply to provide an actual explicit choice to ensure that
F^{\heartsuit}(f)
exists.

Proof.

Begin by observing that on one hand an object
A\in\PC(F^{\heartsuit})_{0}
if and only if for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
the object
\,{}_{X}{A}\in F(X)^{\heartsuit}
and if and only if each transition isomorphism
\tau_{f}^{A}:F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{A}
is an isomorphism in
F(X)^{\heartsuit}
. On the other hand, by construction of the
t
-structure on
\PC(F)
given in Theorem
5.1.21
an object
A
in
\PC(F)
is in
(\PC(F))^{\heartsuit}_{0}
as well if and only if
A\in(\PC(F^{\geq 0})\cap\PC(F^{\leq 0}))_{0}
