. However, unwinding this definition we find that
A
lies in the heart of this
t
-structure if and only if
X
-locally we have that

\,{}_{X}{A}\in\left(F(X)^{\leq 0}\cap F(X)^{\geq 0}\right)_{0}.

This in turn implies that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
we must have that
\,{}_{X}{A}\in F(X)^{\heartsuit}_{0}
, so the only aspect of morphisms that may differ between
\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}
and
\PC(F^{\heartsuit})
lies in the transition isomorphisms. Because
\tau_{f}^{A}:F(f)(\,_{Y}{A})\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{X}{A}
is a morphism in both
F(X)^{\geq 0}
and
F(X)^{\leq 0}
, the isomorphism
\tau_{f}^{A}
is an isomorphism in
F(X)^{\geq 0}\cap F(X)^{\leq 0}
and hence in the heart of
F(X)
. As such, we find that
\PC(F^{\heartsuit})_{0}=(\PC(F)^{\heartsuit})_{0}
. The verification that
(F^{\heartsuit})_{G}(X)_{1}=(\PC(F))^{\heartsuit}_{1}
follows a similar analysis as what we just performed using that the fibre functors all satisfy

F^{\heartsuit}(f)=\left(F^{\geq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f)=\left(F^{\leq 0}\right)|_{F^{\geq 0}(X)\cap F^{\leq 0}(X)}(f)

is
t
-exact, allowing us to conclude that
\PC(F^{\heartsuit})_{1}=(\PC(F)^{\heartsuit})_{1}
as well. Finally, since composition, source, and target maps must also coincide by construction, it follows that
\PC(F)^{\heartsuit}=\PC(F^{\heartsuit})
.
∎

Remark 5.1.26
.

The Change of Heart Theorem shows the heart of the standard
t
-structure on
\PC(F)
coincides with the category of pseudocones with values in
F^{\heartsuit}
.

We now show that when combined with some specific knowledge of the equivariant derived category of a variety, our pseudocone formalism gives rise to equivalences of categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{stand}}}\simeq\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. In particular, this shows that the pseudocone formalism gives a language which allows for the simultaneous study for equivariant sheaves, equivariant perverse sheaves, and equivariant derived categories.

Corollary 5.1.27
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. If
D:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is an
\ell
-adic derived category pseudofunctor

D(\Gamma\times X)=D^{b}_{c}(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),\qquad D(f)=(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}

then

\PC(D)^{\heartsuit}\simeq\PC(\Shv(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}))\simeq\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

Proof.

The fact that for all
\Gamma\times X\in\SfResl_{G}(X)_{0}
we have

D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{stand}}}\simeq\Shv(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

is a classical fact regarding
\ell
-adic sheaves and that these equivalences vary pseudonaturally in
\SfResl_{G}(X)
is routine. Regard
D
as a truncated pseudofunctor with each truncation functor
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq n}},

\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq n}}
the standard truncation functors, i.e., equip
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
with the standard
t
-structure (cf. the first example given in Example
5.1.15
). Applying Theorem
5.1.24
then gives the equivalence

\PC(D)^{\heartsuit}=\PC\left(D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{stand}}}\right)\simeq\PC(\Shv(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

For the remaining equivalence recall that by
[
1
, Lemma 6.4.8]
(see Remark
2.1.10
for where we used this) there is an equivalence of categories
\PC(\tilde{D}:\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat)\simeq\PC(D)
where
\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)
is the category of all free
G
-resolutions of
X
and
\tilde{D}
is the extension of
D
to
\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}
. Using
[
1
, Exercise 6.4.3]
gives an equivalence
\PC(\tilde{D})^{\heartsuit}\simeq\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and hence allows us to conclude

\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq\PC(D)^{\heartsuit}\simeq\PC(\Shv(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

∎

Corollary 5.1.28
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. If
D:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is an
\ell
-adic derived category pseudofunctor

D(\Gamma\times X)=D^{b}_{c}(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),\qquad D(f)=(\overline{f\times\id_{X}})^{\ast}

then

\PC(D)^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\PC(\Shv(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}))\simeq\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

Proof.

Regard
D
as a truncated pseudofunctor with each truncation functor
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\geq n}}=\,_{\Gamma}{{}^{p}\tau^{\geq n}}
and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tau^{\leq n}}=\,_{\Gamma}{{}^{p}\tau^{\leq n}}
the perverse truncation functors, i.e., equip
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
with the perverse
t
-structure (cf. the second example given in Example
5.1.15
). The fact that for all
\Gamma\times X\in\SfResl_{G}(X)_{0}
we have

D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

is definition while verifying that these equivalences themselves vary pseudonaturally in
\SfResl_{G}(X)
is routine. Applying Theorem
5.1.24
then gives the equivalence

\PC(D)^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}=\PC\left(D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\right)\simeq\PC(\Per(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

For the remaining equivalence recall that by
[
1
, Lemma 6.4.8]
(see Remark
2.1.10
for where we used this) there is an equivalence of categories
\PC(\tilde{D}:\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat)\simeq\PC(D)
where
\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)
is the category of all free
G
-resolutions of
X
and
\tilde{D}
is the extension of
D
to
\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}
. Equip
\tilde{D}
with a truncation structure induced by translating the peverse structure on
D
. Using
[
1
, Theorem 6.4.10]
gives an equivalence
\PC(\tilde{D})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and hence allows us to conclude

\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\PC(\Per(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

∎

5.2.
Pseudocone Categories and (Classical) Six Functor Formalisms

We now prove the last main purely homological algebraic result in this monograph and describe when and how (the classical) perspectives on six functor formalisms can arise on
\PC(F)
. We have already seen in Corollaries
4.1.21
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.12
,
4.2.13
that for a smooth algebraic group
G
and a
G
-equivariant morphism
h:X\to Y
of left
G
-varieties, the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and their non-
\ell
-adic versions are symmetric monoidal and have adjunctions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\underline{{h}}_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{h}}^{\ast}}
\dashv

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{h}}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\underline{{h}}_{!}}
\dashv

Similar results hold in the case that
G
is a topological group which admits free
G
-spaces that are manifolds and
G
-equivariant maps
h:X\to Y
by Corollaries
4.1.22
,
4.2.8
,
4.2.14
, and
4.2.15
. Additionally the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are triangulated by Example
5.1.15
and we have already seen that the existence of the six functors (and the corresponding topological versions of the six functors on
D_{G}^{b}(X)
) depend primarily on properties of the pseudofunctors
D_{G}^{b}(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and the various assemblies we can perform with these pseudofunctors and the myriad
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

induced by the morphism
h:X\to Y
.

We now would like to study to what degree and in what sense the remaining properties and relations of the equivariant six functor formalism depends only on the pseudofunctors, their local properties, and their categories of pseudocones. Additionally, we also have not seen how these equivariant six functor formalisms relate to the forgetful functors mapping to the standard six functor formalisms. While relations with the forgetful functors and the open/closed morphism distinguished triangles will be deferred to Section
5.2
, as we need to have developed more of the actual geometry involved in equivariant geometry and topology, in this section we will examine the more algebraic and categorical relations we can study. In particular, we will show that the pseudocone analogues of the identities

\displaystyle(Rh_{!}A)\otimes_{Y}B

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{!}\left(A\otimes_{X}h^{\ast}(B)\right)

\displaystyle\left[Rh_{!}(A),B\right]_{Y}

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{\ast}\left(\left[A,h^{!}(B)\right]_{X}\right)

\displaystyle h^{!}\left(\left[B,C\right]_{Y}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[h^{\ast}(B),h^{!}(C)\right]_{X}

which hold and are natural for all
A\in D_{c}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
and for all
B,C\in D_{c}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
.

As we proceed in this section, assume that we have a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

in
\fCat
and a symmetric monoidal closed pseudofunctor
F:\Escr^{\op}\to\fCat
so that we have the induced
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Escr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Dscr^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\op}}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\rho^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}\ast\alpha^{\op}}

Write the functor induced by
\iota_{F}\ast\alpha^{\op}
through Theorem
4.2.6
as
(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}
. Assume also that we have three additional pseudonatural transformations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\rho^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\rho^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Cscr^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\rho^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}}

Assume also that the fibre functors all satisfy the adjunctions

(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}\dashv(F\ast\alpha)_{\square},\qquad(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}\dashv(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}.

Note that we are writing
(F\ast\alpha)^{\square},(F\ast\alpha)_{\square},(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger},
and
(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}
suggestively as if they are pullback/pushforward functors and exceptional pullback/pushforward functors, but the notation is otherwise simply meant to represent how we want to argue with these functors; the notations otherwise carry no extra meaning. From Theorem
3.2.3
, Corollary
4.1.9
, Theorem
4.1.1
, and Theorem
4.1.17
we know that these give rise to adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[A,-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\otimes A}
\dashv

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[B,-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\otimes B}
\dashv

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}}
\dashv

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}}
\dashv

where
A\in\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})_{0}
and
B\in\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})_{0}
are arbitrary. When we have that
F
is a truncated or triangulated pseudofunctor, we now have the basic set-up for a six functor formalism between
\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})
and
\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})
. We now show that if these functors all
X
-locally in
\Cscr
satisfy the natural isomorphisms required of our six functors, then it will be the case that all the functors above satisfy them as well.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Assume that we have
\Cscr,

\Dscr,

\Escr,

F
,
\alpha
,
\varphi,

\psi,

\rho,

(F\ast\alpha)_{\square},

(F\ast\alpha)^{\square},

(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger},
and
(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}
be given as in the setup prior to the theorem and assume that for all
X\in\Cscr_{0}
and for all objects
A\in F(\rho X)_{0}
and for all objects
B,C\in F((\psi\circ\varphi)X)_{0}
we have natural isomorphisms

\,{}_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\right)\underset{F((\psi\circ\varphi)X)}{\otimes}\,_{X}{B}

\displaystyle\cong\,_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}}\left(\,{}_{X}{A}\underset{F(\rho X)}{\otimes}(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}(\,_{X}{B})\right)

\displaystyle\left[\,{}_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}}(\,_{X}{A}),\,_{X}{B}\right]_{F((\psi\circ\varphi)(X))}

\displaystyle\cong\,_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}}\left(\left[\,{}_{X}{A},\,_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}}(\,_{X}{B})\right]_{F(\rho X)}\right)

\,{}_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}}\left(\left[\,{}_{X}{C},\,_{X}{B}\right]_{F((\psi\circ\varphi)X)}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[\,{}_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}}(\,_{X}{C}),\,_{X}{(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}}(\,_{X}{B})\right]_{F(\rho X)}

Then for any objects
A\in\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})_{0}
and
B,C\in\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})_{0}
there are natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}(A)\underset{\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}{\otimes}B

\displaystyle\cong(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}\left(A\underset{\PC(F\ast\rho^{\op})}{\otimes}(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}(B)\right)

\displaystyle\left[(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}(A),B\right]_{\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}

\displaystyle\cong(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}\left(\left[A,(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}(B)\right]_{\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}\right)

\displaystyle(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}\left(\left[C,B\right]_{\PC(F\circ\psi^{\op}\circ\varphi^{\op})}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}(C),(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}(B)\right]_{\PC(F\circ\rho^{\op})}

Proof.

Because each of the natural isomorphisms above hold at the
X
-local level for all
X\in\Cscr^{\op}
, we only need to check that they may be assembled into morphisms at the pseudocone category level to deduce that they exist. However, this is a routine but extremely tedious check using both the pseudonaturality of the various pseudonatural transformations coupled with the naturality of the isomorphisms applied to the transition morphisms
\tau_{f}^{A}:F(\rho^{\op}(f))(\,_{Y}{A})\to\,_{X}{A}
, and similarly for
B
and
C
.
∎

It now follows from the theorem above that for a smooth algebraic group
G
and a
G
-equivariant morphism
h:X\to Y
of left
G
-varieties, the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
D_{G}^{b}(X)
both admit six functor formalisms with the relations above with
\square=\ast
and
\dagger=!
.

Corollary 5.2.2
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism of left
G
-varieties. Consider the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{c}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{(-)\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{-}\times h}}

and define the pseudonatural transformations/functors by assigning
(F\ast\alpha)^{\square}=h^{\ast}
,
(F\ast\alpha)_{\square}=Rh_{\ast},

(F\ast\alpha)^{\dagger}=h^{!}
, and
(F\ast\alpha)_{\dagger}=Rh_{!}
. Then there are natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle Rh_{!}(A)\underset{Y}{\otimes}B

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{!}\left(A\underset{X}{\otimes}h^{\ast}(B)\right),

\displaystyle\left[Rh_{!}(A),B\right]_{Y}

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{\ast}\left(\left[A,h^{!}(B)\right]_{X}\right),

\displaystyle h^{!}\left([C,B]_{Y}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[h^{\ast}(C),h^{!}(B)\right]_{X}

where
A\in D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
and
B,C\in D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
.

Proof.

By Corollaries
4.1.21
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.12
,
4.2.13
we know that we satisfy the technical assumptions setting up Theorem
5.2.1
. Consequently, since each
\Gamma
-local fibre category and corresponding functors satisfy the natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{!}(\AGamma)\underset{\otimes}{G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}\BGamma

\displaystyle\cong R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{!}\left(\AGamma\underset{\otimes}{G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{\ast}(\BGamma)\right),

\displaystyle\left[R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{!}(\AGamma),\BGamma\right]_{G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}

\displaystyle\cong R(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})_{\ast}\left(\left[\AGamma,(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{!}(\BGamma)\right]_{G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}\right),

\displaystyle(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{!}\left([\,_{\Gamma}{C},\BGamma]_{G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{\ast}(\,_{\Gamma}{C}),(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h})^{!}(\BGamma)\right]_{G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}

for all
\AGamma\in A,\BGamma\in B
, and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{C}\in C
we simply apply Theorem
5.2.1
to give our result.
∎

Chapter 6
Equivariant Descent and Categories of Pseudocones

We now begin to study equivariant algebraic geometry in earnest. While the first half of our work has shown that we can develop and study the theory of equivariant derived categories in topological and geometric contexts almost entirely from the perspective of pseudocone categories. In particular, the theory we have developed shows that there is a strong formal analogy that in a working sense equivariant descent comes in two moving components which meet transversally:

(1)

The first component is the geometric/topological component. This is the part of the story that occupies the study of the category of
G
-resolutions, simplicial arguments and objects, which
G
-resolutions we should use or consider and why, properties of resolutions, etc.

(2)

The second component is the algebraic/categorical component. This is the part of the story that focus on the various categories, pseudofunctors, and other gadgets we use and probe our objects with, as well as what algebraic properties these gadgets satisfy (or fail to satisfy) and what they tell us about the geometry/topology of the action
G\times X\to X
.

Of course, these two components are transversal in nature and need not meet orthogonally: they need not move independently from each other and in fact can and do inform what techniques or settings we should use from either component in any given moment but also do not move parallel to each other.

In this chapter we will begin to focus on the geometric and topological side of the story and start our in-depth study of equivariant descent. In particular, we will provide the framework that shows the category
\SfResl_{G}(X)
has the properties we have claimed and used in Section
2.1
(such as the existence of the quotient functor
\quo:\SfResl_{G}(X)\to\Var_{/K}
). Afterwards we will define what it means to be an equivariant category, what equivariant functors are, and introduce the “equivariance” isomorphism and cocycle condition attached to an equivariant category.

6.1.
Equivariant Categories and Smooth Free Varieties

In Section
2.1
we saw some of the properties of the category
\SfResl_{G}(X)
that allowed us to define
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. The most important ingredients in doing this were the fact that
\Gamma\times X
admits a geometric quotient for any
G
-variety
X
and for any locally isotrivial smooth free variety
\Gamma
and these quotients extend to a functor
\quo:\SfResl_{G}(X)\to\Var_{/K}
; because
\Var_{/K}
rarely admits coequalizers, this requires careful justification and set-up. In this and the next section (cf. Section
6.2
) we will show how these facts arise as well as study some extra properties of the category
\Sf(G)
as we vary groups and how these categories interact with the equivariant categories
F_{G}(X)=\PC(F)
for a class of pseudofunctors which we call pre-equivariant.

Perhaps the best place to start this story is at the beginning; because of how important they are to what we do, we record various important properties of smooth morphisms and quotients of smooth morphisms both for the sake of completeness but also for the sake of non-algebraic geometers.. Recall that the definitions of smooth free
G
-varieties, the categories
\Sf(G)
and
\SfResl_{G}(X)
, and other basics can be found in Section
2.1
(cf. Definitions
2.1.1
,
2.1.5
,
2.1.6
).

Proposition 6.1.1
.

If
\Gamma
is a
G
-variety in
\Sf(G)_{0}
then the quotient morphism
\quo_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\to G\backslash\Gamma
is smooth and the diagram

\textstyle{G\times\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{G\backslash\Gamma}

is a pullback square.

Proof.

This is
[
47
, Proposition 0.9]
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.2

(
[
56
,
Tag 02K5
]
)
.

Consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z}

of schemes for which
f
is surjective and smooth and
h
is smooth. Then
g
is smooth as well.

Proposition 6.1.3
.

Let
Y,Z
be
G
-schemes with smooth quotient morphisms
\quo_{Y}:Y\to G\backslash Y
and
\quo_{Z}:Z\to G\backslash Z
. Then if there is a smooth
G
-equivariant morphism
f:Y\to Z
there exists a unique morphism
\of:G\backslash Y\to G\backslash Z
making the diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{Y}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\quo_{Z}}
\textstyle{G\backslash Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\of}
\textstyle{G\backslash Z}

commute. Moreover,
\of
is smooth as well.

Proof.

The existence of the morphism
\of
follows from the universal property of the quotient (as a coequalizer of
\alpha_{Y},\pi_{2}:G\times Y\to Y
) applied to the morphism
(\quo_{Z}\circ f):Y\to G\backslash Z
. That
\of
is smooth follows from Lemma
6.1.2
together with the observations that
\quo_{Y}
is smooth and surjective while the composite
\quo_{Z}\circ f
is smooth.
∎

The next proposition is a crucial technical result which allows us to deduce that for any
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and any left
G
-variety
X
, the product
\Gamma\times X
is free and admits a smooth geometric quotient. This allows us to resolve the action of
G
on
X
by looking at
G
-resolutions of the form
\pi_{2}^{\Gamma}:\Gamma\times X\to X
as the
\Gamma
vary through
\Sf(G)
. As we have seen, this is the fundamental technical tool which allows us to describe equivariant information by taking descent data indexed by a pseudofunctor defined on these resolutions.

Proposition 6.1.4
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth free
G
-variety and let
Y
be a
G
-variety. Then the variety
\Gamma\times Y
is free with smooth quotient morphism

\quo_{\Gamma\times Y}:\Gamma\times Y\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y).

Furthermore, if there is a
G
-equivariant morphism of smooth free
G
-varieties
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
then there is a smooth morphism making the diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{q_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!\overline{f}}
\textstyle{G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times Y)}

commute in
\Var_{/\Spec K}
.

Proof.

Our argument for the existence of the quotient
G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)
follows the sketch given in
[
1
, Proposition 6.1.13]
. Because each quotient
\Gamma\to G\backslash\Gamma
is locally isotrivial, by
[
55
, Proposition 4]
it follows that if we can show for all finite subsets
F\subseteq\lvert Y\rvert
there is an open affine
U
of
Y
for which
F\subseteq\lvert U\rvert
, then it follows that
\Gamma\times Y
is free and admits a geometric quotient
\quo:\Gamma\times Y\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)
. However, by
[
36
, Proposition 3.3.36]
because
Y
is quasi-projective
1
1

1

Recall from Remark
1.3.1
that we only consider quasi-projective varieties in this monograph.
over
\Spec K
, for any finite set
F\subseteq\lvert Y\rvert
we can find an open affine subscheme
U
of
Y
for which
F\subseteq\lvert U\rvert
so it follows that
\Gamma\times Y
is a free
G
-variety. From this it follows that the morphism

\quo_{\Gamma\times Y}:\Gamma\times Y\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times Y)

is smooth, which establishes the first claim. For the second claim we simply apply Proposition
6.1.3
to the
G
-equivariant morphism
f\times\id_{Y}
, which is smooth because
\id_{Y}
is étale,
f
is smooth by assumption, and because the product of smooth morphisms is smooth.
∎

Remark 6.1.5
.

By
[
1
, Proposition 6.1.13]
(see also
[
51
, Lemme XIV 1.4]
) it follows that when
G
is a smooth affine algebraic group then asking for the quotient map
\Gamma\to G\backslash\Gamma
to be locally isotrivial is equivalent to asking for the quotient map to be étale locally trivializable. In particular, when
G
is affine smooth then the
\Sf(G)
-varieties are exactly the pure dimensional smooth free
G
-varieties which admit a geometric quotient.

Remark 6.1.6
.

We suggest defining our smooth free
G
-varieties to be the (pure dimensional) locally isotrivial principal
G
-fibrations
\Gamma
in
\Var_{/K}
precisely because this is the class of
G
-varieties which allow us to have a quotient functor
\quo:\SfResl_{G}(X)\to\Var_{/K}
. Because we have the induced equivalences of categories
D_{G}^{b}(\Gamma)\simeq D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash\Gamma)
by virtue of Corollary
2.3.15
we know that this is a reasonable categorical language for performing equivariant descent and so suggests giving a comparison between
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X])
.

In the case where
G
is an affine algebraic group we have equivalences of categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X])
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X];\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
; these are either definitional (as in
[
1
]
) or the result of theorems (cf.
[
63
]
) depending on the perspective taken on the derived category of the quotient stack
[G\backslash X]
. When
G
is nonaffine we still have the equivalences

D_{G}^{b}(\Gamma)\simeq D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash\Gamma)\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash\Gamma])

for all
\Sf(G)
-varieties
\Gamma
(and similarly for the
\ell
-adic versions) precisely because in this case
[G\backslash\Gamma]
is represented by the variety
G\backslash\Gamma
. This suggests that by descending through the varieties
\Gamma\times X
we get equivalences of categories

D_{G}^{b}(\Gamma\times X)\simeq D_{c}^{b}\left(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\right)\simeq D_{c}^{b}\left(\left[G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\right]\right)

again because the stack
\left[G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)\right]
is represented by the variety
G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)
. By using a simplicial presentation of
D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X])
as the category of groupoid sheaves
D_{\text{eq}}^{b}(\underline{{G\backslash X}}_{\bullet})
(where the simplicial scheme
\underline{{G\backslash X}}_{\bullet}
is the simplicial presentation of the translation groupoid of the group action
\alpha_{X},\pi_{2}:G\times X\to X
and has
n
-simplexes given by
\underline{{G\backslash X}}_{n}=G^{n}\times X
) we should be able to proceed with the techniques of
[
63
, Chapter 8]
in order to ultimately derive the equivalence
D_{G}^{b}(X)\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X])
. This gives rise to the conjecture below which can be seen as justifying a “model-agnostic” perspective on the equivariant derived category.

Conjecture 6.1.7
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then there are equivalences of categories

\PC(D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(-)))\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X])

and

\PC(D_{c}^{b}(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}))\simeq D_{c}^{b}([G\backslash X];\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

Before proceeding to discuss the theory of equivariant categories, we record a crucial, but straightforward, scheme-theoretic lemma on algebraic groups. In particular we need to know that for any smooth algebraic group
G
, the group
G
is itself an object in
\Sf(G)
. While this is obvious enough, it is fundamental to the constructions we will do later on (especially involving the change of group functors; cf. Theorem
7.1.13
) that we make sure to record it.

Lemma 6.1.8
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over a field
K
. Then
G\in\Sf(G)_{0}
.

Proof.

The fact that
G
admits a geometric quotient given by
G\backslash G\cong\Spec K
is routine by
[
16
, Theorem V.3.2]
(cf. also
[
44
]
). That this is étale locally trivializable and in particular locally isotrivial follows from the fact that the diagram

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec K}

is a pullback and
\id_{\Spec K}
is a finite étale cover of
\Spec K
. Finally, since
G
is pure dimensional by
[
16
, Lemma VIB.1.5]
, the lemma follows.
∎

We now define pre-equivariant pseudofunctors. These are the necessary ingredients we need in order to define both equivariant categories on varieties and equivariant categories on spaces. These pseudofunctors are particularly well-suited for equivariant descent and both capture and generalize the equivariant derived categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
.

Definition 6.1.9
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. We define a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
to be a pair of pseudofunctors
(F,\overline{F})
where
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
and
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors such that the diagram of (bi)categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}}

commutes.

Remark 6.1.10
.

The definition given here for pre-equivariant pseudofunctors is slightly different than the one given in
[
63
]
where they first appeared; it does, however, match with that given in
[
50
]
.

We also now extend the definition of a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor to topological spaces.

Definition 6.1.11
.

Let
G
be a topological group and let
X
be a left
G
-space. A pre-equivariant pseudofunctor (for the space
X
) is a pair
(F,\overline{F})
where
F:\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
and
\overline{F}:\Top^{\op}\to\fCat
are pseudofunctors for which there is a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Resl}_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Top^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P\mapsto G\backslash P)^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}}

of (bi)categories.

To a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
(F,\overline{F})
there is an evident pseudocone category we can and should consider: the category

\PC(F)=\PC(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}).

This is by far and away the most important class of categories to consider for our applications. When studying the categories
\PC(F)
, we will see in the remainder of this monograph that it is frequently important to leverage the properties of
\overline{F}
in order to derive properties about
\PC(F)
indirectly. For instance, it is relatively straightforward to show that if
\overline{F}
is a triangulated pseudofunctor then so is
F
.

Example 6.1.12
.

Here is a large list of pre-equivariant pseudofunctors for both schemes and spaces. Note that many of these examples overlap with Example
2.3.12
.

(1)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then there is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor pair
(F,\overline{F})
for the category category of (
\ell
-adic) equivariant perverse sheaves. This pre-equivariant pseudofunctor is given by letting
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
be a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto{}^{p}\of^{\ast}:\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),

respectively. It’s companion pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
is then a pseudofunctor with
\overline{F}(Z):=\Per(Z;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and with
\overline{F}(h):={}^{p}h^{\ast}
for morphisms of varieties
h
.

(2)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over
K
and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then there is a local system pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
given as follows. The pseudofunctor
L:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is defined with object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X);\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto\of^{\ast}:\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X))\to\Loc(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)),

respectively. The companion pseudofunctor
\overline{L}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
is given by
\overline{L}(Z)=\Loc(Z;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
on objects and
\overline{L}(f)=f^{\ast}
for morphisms.

(3)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group over
K
and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Then the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor of the derived category of perverse sheaves on
X
, is determined by a pseudofunctor
F:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
where
F
has object and morphism assignments

\Gamma\times X\mapsto D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)))

and

f\times\id_{X}\mapsto{}^{p}L\of^{\ast}:D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X)))\to D^{b}(\Per(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))),

respectively. The companion pseudofunctor is a pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
with
\overline{F}(Z)=D^{b}(\Per(Z))
and
\overline{F}(h)={}^{p}Lh^{\ast}
on morphisms. If
G
is a complex Lie group and
X
is a complex analytic variety then there is a corresponding topological version as well defined mutatis mutandis.

(4)

Let
G
be a topological group and let
X
be a left
G
-space. The pre-equivariant pseudofuncotr of perverse sheaves on
X
is given as follows. Define the pseudofunctor
F:\FResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
where
F
has object and morphism assignments

P\mapsto\Per(G\backslash P)

and

f\mapsto{}^{p}\of^{\ast}:\Per(G\backslash Q)\to\Per(G\backslash P)

respectively. The companion
\overline{F}:\mathbf{TopStrat}^{\op}\to\fCat
is given by sending a stratified space
Z
to its category of perverse sheaves
\Per(Z)
and sending a morphism
h
of stratified spaces to the corresponding category of perverse sheaves.

(5)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. We can then describe the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor of
G
-equivariant
D
-modules as follows. Let
D:\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
is a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

D(\Gamma\times X):=\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))

and

D(f\times\id_{X}):=\of^{\ast}:\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma^{\prime}\times X))\to\DMod(G\backslash(\Gamma\times X))

respectively. Note that in this case the functor
\of^{\ast}
exists by
[
5
, Section 4.1]
because each morphism
\of
is smooth by Proposition
6.1.4
. We similarly define
\overline{D}:\Var_{K,\text{sm}}^{\op}
to send a
K
-variety
Z
to its category
\DMod(Z)
of
\Dcal
-modules and send a morphism to the corresponding
\Dcal
-module pullback (which also exists by
[
5
, Section 4.1]
and the smoothness of morphisms in
\Var_{/K,\text{sm}}
given by assumption).

(6)

Let
G
be a Lie group, regarded as a topological group, and let
X
be a smooth manifold (regarded as a topological space) with a smooth
G
-action. Then the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor modeling
G
-equivariant sheaves on
X
is given as follows. Define
F:\FResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat
as a pseudofunctor with object and morphism assignments

P\mapsto\Shv(G\backslash P)

and

f\mapsto\of^{\ast}:\Shv(G\backslash Q)\to\Shv(G\backslash P),

respectively. The companion
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
then sends a
K
-variety to its corresponding category of sheaves and sends a morphism of varieties to the corresponding pullback between sheaf categories. There is a topological version of this example given mutatis mutandis as well.

The definitions of pre-equivariant pseudofunctors for both schemes and spaces allow us to define equivariant categories as well as a convention of convenience we will use throughout the remainder of this work. As we work with these categories (especially in the cases of working with the categories
\SfResl_{G}(X)
when
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety or when
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)
when
G
is a topological group which has
n
-acyclic free spaces which are manifolds and
X
is a left
G
-space) we will need to be indexing our objects and morphisms in the pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
by the quotient spaces
G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)
or
G\backslash(M\times X)
; however, as we have seen already (cf. Corollaries
4.1.21
,
4.1.22
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.8
,
4.2.13
,
4.2.12
,
4.2.15
,
4.2.14
,
5.2.2
, for instance) this can be not only notationally cumbersome but also hides an important aspect to the story. Because each of the categories
\SfResl_{G}(X)
carries an isomorphism
\SfResl_{G}(X)\cong\Sf(G)
in the geometric case (and similarly in the topological case each category
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)
carries an isomorphism
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)\cong\mathbf{FAMan}(G)
where
\mathbf{FAMan}(G)
denotes the category whose objects are free
G
-spaces which are manifolds
n
-acyclic for some
n\in\N
and whose morphisms are smooth
G
-equivariant morphisms between such resolutions). As such to a large degree what is important is that we are indexing our objects all by the smooth free
G
-spaces
\Gamma
, and baking this into our notation will not only reduce the notational clutter in our discussion and formalism but also will introduce a convenient formalism for keeping track of objects which may require multiple indexes.

Definition 6.1.13
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. For any variety
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
we define the shorthand

\XGamma:=G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

Similarly, if
G
is a topological group admitting free
n
-acyclic
G
-spaces which are manifolds,
X
is a left
G
-space, and
M\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{0}
, we define

\,{}_{M}{X}:=G\backslash(M\times X).

Definition 6.1.14
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. For any morphism
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Sf(G)
we define the map

\of:\XGamma\to\XGammap

by taking
\of=\overline{f\times\id_{X}}=G\backslash(f\times\id_{X})
. Similarly, if
G
is if
G
is a topological group admitting free
n
-acyclic
G
-spaces which are manifolds and
X
is a left
G
-space, for any map
f:M\to N
in
\mathbf{FAMan}(G)
we define

\of:\,_{M}{X}\to\,_{N}{X}

by taking
\of=\overline{f\times\id_{X}}=G\backslash(f\times\id_{X})
.

We now define equivariant categories and show that there is a forgetful functor which “forgets” the
G
-equivariance recorded by the equivariant descent contained in
\SfResl_{G}(X)
and the composite pseudofunctor
\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}
. In what follows we give the definition of equivariant categories for both topological and geometric situations, but will mainly focus on the geometric situation. Note that the two definitions differ functionally only in the domain of the pseudofunctors
F
.

Definition 6.1.15
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety. Let
(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. We define the equivariant category
F_{G}(X)
on
X
to be the category

F_{G}(X):=\PC(F)=\PC(F\circ\quo^{\op}).

Definition 6.1.16
.

Let
G
be a topological space and let
X
be a left
G
-space. Let
(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. We define the equivariant category
F_{G}(X)
on
X
to be the category

F_{G}(X):=\PC(F)=\PC(F\circ\quo^{\op}).

Let us do a quick analysis of the situation that surrounds an equivariant category and a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor. For any smooth algebraic group
G
, there is a non-equivariant isomorphism of varieties

\psi:X\xrightarrow{\cong}\,_{G}{X}=G\backslash(G\times X)

which is induced, in the set-theoretic language, by the map
x\mapsto[1,x]
. There is a similar non-equivariant isomorphism when
G
is a topological group and
X
is a left
G
-space as well. From Theorem
2.3.16
we know that

\PC(F)=\PC(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op})

and so there is a functor

p_{G}:\PC(F)\to\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})

induced by the pseudouniversal property. Post-composing this with the equivalence
\overline{F}(\psi)
then gives rise to a functor

F_{G}(X)\xrightarrow{p_{G}}\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})\xrightarrow{\overline{F}(\psi)}\overline{F}(X)

which serves as our forgetful functor
2
2

2

And in fact does so in a strong way by Proposition
7.1.19
.
(and in fact we will call this functor
\Forget
because it forgot the “
G
” information).

Proposition 6.1.17
.

Let
G
and
X
be given as in either case:

(1)

G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety;

(2)

G
is a topological group and
X
is a left
G
-space;

and let
(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. There is a forgetful functor

\Forget:F_{G}(X)\to\overline{F}(X).

Moreover, there is also a functor
\Eq:\overline{F}(X)\to F_{G}(X)
which gives rise to an invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Eq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

The existence of
\Forget:F_{G}(X)\to\overline{F}(X)
as
\Forget=\overline{F}(\psi)\circ p_{G}
follows from the description prior to the statement of the proposition. We prove the existence of the
\Eq
in the scheme-theoretic case; the topological case follows mutatis mutandis by replacing every
\Gamma
with an
M
.

To see the existence of the equivariantification functor we note that for any
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
, the morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{-1}}

is
G
-trivial by virtue of ending in the object
\,{}_{G}{X}
. It then follows from the universal property of the quotient object
\XGamma
that there is a unique morphism
\overline{\Gamma}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\overline{\Gamma}}

commute. The various morphisms
\overline{\Gamma}
satisfy the property that for any
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Sf(G)
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGammap}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\of}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}

commutes in
\Var_{/K}
. From this we define, for any
A\in\overline{F}(X)_{0}
the object

\Eq A:=\left\{\overline{F}(\overline{\Gamma})\left(F(\psi^{-1})(A)\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}\right\}

with transition isomorphisms induced by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\of)\left(\overline{F}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime})\left(\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})A\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}\circ\of)\left(\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\overline{F}(\overline{\Gamma})\circ\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})\right)(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\of)\left(\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\Eq A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\Eq A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\of,\overline{\Gamma}}^{\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{f}^{\Eq A}}

for any
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
in
\Sf(G)_{1}
. That these transition isomorphisms satisfy the cocycle condition is immediate from the pseudofuncoriality of
\overline{F}
, so we do indeed have an object of
F_{G}(X)
. The assignment on morphisms is given similarly: For any
\alpha\in\overline{F}(X)_{1},

\Eq\alpha:=\left\{\overline{F}(\overline{\Gamma})\left(\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})\alpha\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}\right\}

and that these do indeed define morphisms follows from the fact that the compositors
\phi
are natural isomorphisms. Thus we get our desired functor
\Eq
.

For the claim on the natural isomorphism we simply calculate that

(\Forget\circ\Eq)(A)=\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\overline{F}(\psi^{-1})A\right)

so setting
\nu:=\phi_{\psi,\psi^{-1}}
proves the proposition.
∎

6.2.
The Equivariance Isomorphisms

We now study the geometric information carried by the objects of an equivariant category and precisely in what sense it is that the objects “respect the
G
-action on
X
3
3

3

Analogously to how each of the objects in
\Shv_{G}(X)
,
\Per_{G}(X)
, etc. contain sheaves that respect the
G
-action on
X
.
.” This ultimately means prove first that equivariant categories
F_{G}(X)
are formally naïevely equivariant in the sense that there are functors
\pi_{2}^{\ast},\alpha^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
where
\pi_{2}
is induced by the projection map
\pi_{2}:G\times X\to X
and
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
is induced by the action map
\alpha_{X}:G\times X\to X
. We then use these functors to construct a natural isomorphism

\Theta:\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A\xRightarrow{\cong}\pi_{2}^{\ast}A

which satisfies a pseudofunctorial generalization of the cocycle condition of
[
47
, Definition 1.6]
(which we spell out later; cf. Definition
6.2.16
). Our first step here, consequently, is to introduce some language and then construct the functors
\pi_{2}^{\ast}
and
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}
.

Remark 6.2.1
.

For the impatient, the GIT cocycle condition is the condition described in
[
47
, Definition 1.6]
as well as
[
6
, Chapter 0]
and often stated as writing
\Theta
as an isomorphism of sheaves
\Theta:d_{0}^{\ast}\Fscr\Rightarrow d_{1}^{\ast}\Fscr
which satisfies the equation
d_{0}^{\ast}\Theta\circ d_{2}^{\ast}\Theta=d_{1}^{\ast}\Theta
. In this case the maps
d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}
come from the simplicial presentation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

of the translation groupoid of the action
\alpha_{X}:G\times X\to X
.
We explain and describe this condition in more detail in Definition
6.2.16
below.

Definition 6.2.2
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
and
Y
be left
G
-varieties or let
G
be a topological group and let
X
and
Y
be left
G
-spaces. We say that a simultaneous pre-equivariant equivariant pseudofunctor over
X
and
Y
is a triple
\left((F,\overline{F}),(F^{\prime},\overline{F}^{\prime}),e:\overline{F}\to\overline{F}^{\prime}\right)
where
(F,\overline{F})
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
,
(F^{\prime},\overline{F}^{\prime})
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
Y
, and
e:\overline{F}\Rightarrow\overline{F}^{\prime}
is an equivalence
\overline{F}\simeq\overline{F}^{\prime}
in the bicategory
\Bicat(\Var_{/K}^{\op},\fCat)
.

Remark 6.2.3
.

It follows immediately from the pseudoconification
2
-functor described in Lemma
4.1.13
that there is an equivalence of categories
\PC(\overline{F})\simeq\PC(\overline{F}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 6.2.4
.

For any smooth algebraic group
G
and any left
G
-variety
X
or any topological group
G
and any left
G
-space
X
, there is an inclusion functor
\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)\to\SfResl_{G}(X)
or
\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(G\times X)\to\mathbf{SRes}_{G}(X)
. In particular, any pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
(F,\overline{F})
on
X
induces a simultaneous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor over
X
and
G\times X
.

Proof.

We show this for the scheme-theoretic situation; the topological case follows mutatis mutandis. The first claim in the lemma follows because each variety
\Gamma\times(G\times X)\in\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)_{0}
also satisfies

\Gamma\times(G\times X)\cong(\Gamma\times G)\times X\in\SfResl_{G}(X)_{0}

because
\Gamma\times G\in\Sf(G)_{0}
. Call the corresponding inclusion functor

\incl:\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)\to\SfResl_{G}(X)

and note that the corresponding quotient functor

\quo:\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)\to\Var_{/K}

factors through
\incl
. As such, we get a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\circ\incl^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\incl^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Consequently we define the pre-equivariant pseudofunctors comprising the simultaneous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor as
(F,\overline{F})
on
X
and
(F\circ\operatorname{incl}^{\op},\overline{F})
on
G\times X
and then taking the equivalence
e
to be the identity transformation on
\overline{F}
.
∎

Remark 6.2.5
.

Reading the proof above we see that it is even unnecessary to start with a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. If we instead simply have a pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
or
\overline{F}:\Top^{\op}\to\fCat
then we get a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
by simply setting
F
to be the pre-composition of
\overline{F}
by
\quo^{\op}
.

Lemma 6.2.6
.

For any pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
F
over
X
, there is a functor

\pi_{2}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X).

Proof.

Since
\pi_{2}:G\times X\to X
is
G
-equivariant, we apply Theorem
4.2.6
to produce
\pi_{2}^{\ast}
as the functor induced by the map
\pi_{2}
.
∎

Constructing the functor
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
is more difficult than constructing
\pi_{2}^{\ast}
. The action morphism
\alpha_{X}:G\times X\to X
is rarely
G
-equivariant; the corresponding diagram takes the form (where
\alpha_{G\times X}
uses the diagonal
G
-action on
G\times X
)

\textstyle{G\times G\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{G\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\id_{G}\times\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{G\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{G\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}}
\textstyle{X}

which commutes if and only if

\alpha_{X}(\alpha_{G\times X}(h,g,x))=hghx=hgx=\alpha_{X}((\id_{G}\times\alpha_{X})(h,g,x))

for every
x\in X
and
g,h\in G
. Because this implies the action on
X
is trivial, we cannot assume this. As such, to construct the pullback of the action
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
we need to use the language of Change of Domain functors in order to overcome this obstruction. We will give two distinct arguments for this proof: one, which is presented as the main proof goes through the exact categorical structure used and illustrates how Theorem
4.2.6
determines the pullback against the action map. The other (more direct) argument given in Remark
6.2.8
shows in a quick-and-dirty fashion how to avoid the categorical arguments and jump straight to the result.

Lemma 6.2.7
.

For any pre-equivariant pseudofunctor there is a functor

\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X).

Proof.

We argue the scheme-theoretic case; the topological case follows similarly. Our strategy is to prove that
F
admits pseudocone translations along
\alpha_{X}
. Begin letting
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
be a morphism in
\Sf(G)
. Then because
f
is
G
-equivariant we can produce a commuting cube:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGammap}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{G\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma}}

Note that the equivariance of
f
is used to deduce the commutativity of the vertically oriented faces of the cube while the top face commutes by construction. This in turn provides the commuting square of quotient varieties:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{(G\times X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGammap}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}}

Let
\underline{{X}}:\Sf(G)\to\SfResl_{G}(X)
and
\underline{{G\times X}}:\Sf(G)\to\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)
be the isomorphisms of Remark
2.1.7
. As the above diagrams of quotient varieties vary naturally in
\Gamma
we induce a
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\incl}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\alpha}_{X}}

where for all
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
,
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\tilde{\alpha}_{X}}:=\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}
. Post-composing by these isomorphisms gives rise to the
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sf(G)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{X}}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{{G\times X}})^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}_{X}^{\op}}

which factors as the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sf(G)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(G\times X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Var_{/K}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{X}}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{{G\times X}})^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\incl^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\id_{-}\times\alpha_{X})^{\op}}

where the empty cell is used as a shorthand for the identity
2
-cell. Post-composing by the pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var^{\op}\to\fCat
then provides us with the induced
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sf(G)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\underline{{X}}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}\circ(\underline{{G\times X}})^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{{\alpha}}_{X}}

where
\underline{{\alpha}}_{X}=\iota_{\overline{F}}\ast\iota_{\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}}\ast(\id_{-}\times\alpha_{X})^{\op}
. Applying Theorem
4.2.6
then gives rise to a functor

\hat{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}:\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\underline{{X}}^{\op}\right)\longrightarrow\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}\circ(\underline{{G\times X}})^{\op}\right)

as induced by
\overline{F}
. However, observe that since
\underline{{X}}
and
\underline{{G\times X}}
are isomorphisms, there are induced equivalences of categories

\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\underline{{X}}^{\op}\right)\simeq\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\right)

and

\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}\circ(\underline{{G\times X}})^{\op}\right)\simeq\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}\right).

We now use Lemma
6.2.4
to write

\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\right)=F_{G}(X),\qquad\PC\left(\overline{F}\circ\quo^{\op}\circ\incl^{\op}\right)=F_{G}(G\times X)

and hence produce our desired functor

\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X).

∎

Remark 6.2.8

(A Second Proof of Lemma
6.2.7
)
.

Proceed as in the first proof above to produce the diagram of varieties

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{(G\times X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGammap}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}}

and now apply the functor
\overline{F}
to produce the pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\XGammap\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times G}{X}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{(G\times X)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\XGamma\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma\times G}{X}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\overline{f\times\id_{X}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

where

\phi=\phi^{-1}_{\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}},\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}\circ\phi_{\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}},\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}}.

It then follows from a verification mutatis mutandis to Theorems
4.1.1
and
4.2.6
that this gives rise to a functor
F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
induced by the assignments, for
A\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
,

\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A=\left\{F(\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}})\left(\AGamma\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0},\AGamma\in A\right\}

on object collecations with the transition isomorphisms given by

T_{\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A}=\left\{F(\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}})(\tau_{f}^{A})\circ\phi^{-1}_{\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}},\overline{f\times\id_{X}}}\circ\phi_{\overline{f\times\id_{G\times X}},\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\alpha_{X}}}\;:\;f\in\Sf(G)_{1}\right\}.

Remark 6.2.9
.

In what follows, we will write

\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}:=\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}:\,_{\Gamma\times G}{X}\to\XGamma

in order to reduce the already significant notational bloat.

Remark 6.2.10
.

In the interest of being explicit, we note that the functor
\pi_{2}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
is given as follows on objects. Let
(A,T_{A})\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
. By Theorem
4.2.6

\pi_{2}^{\ast}A=\left\{F(\,_{\Gamma}{\opi_{2}})\left(\AGamma\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0},\AGamma\in A\right\},

where
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\pi_{2}}
is the projection map
\Gamma\times G\times X\to\Gamma\times X
, and the transition isomorphisms are given as

T_{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}=\left\{\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{\opi_{2}})\tau_{f}^{A}\circ\left(\phi_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{\pi_{2}},\,_{X}{f}}^{\AGammap}\right)^{-1}\circ\phi_{\,{}_{G\times X}{f},\,_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\pi_{2}}}^{\AGammap}\;:\;f\in\Sf(G)_{1}\right\}.

The topological version of the functor is similar save with every
\Gamma
replaced with an
M
.

Remark 6.2.11
.

In order to reduce the (yet even further) notational clutter creeping its way into this section, if
f:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\Sf(G)
and if
h:X\to Y
is a morphism of
G
-varieties, we write

\,{}_{X}{\of}:=\overline{f\times\id_{X}},\,_{Y}{\of}:=\overline{f\times\id_{Y}},\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{h}}:=\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times h},

and
\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\overline{h}}:=\overline{\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times h}
.

In order to produce the isomorphism
\Theta
, we follow a process discovered by Clifton Cunningham during the BIRS focused research group “the Voganish Project” in 2019. The main idea here is to find, for each variety
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
, an
\Sf(G)
-variety
\Gamma_{c}
which induces an isomorphism of quotient varieties

\,{}_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}\cong\,_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}

that is natural over
\Sf(G)
with respect to the actions of
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}
and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\opi_{2}}
so that we can use the transition isomorphisms of an object of
F_{G}(X)
to build an isomorphism with the objects
\pi_{2}^{\ast}A
and
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A
. We present this as the lemma below, which is the main ingredient in cooking up our desired isomorphism
\Theta
.

Lemma 6.2.12
.

For all
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
there exists a variety
\Gamma_{c}\in\Sf(G)_{0}
such that there is an isomorphism of quotient varieties

\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\xrightarrow{\mu_{\Gamma}}\,_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}.

Moreover, if
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
with
f\in\Sf(G)(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime})
there is an
\Sf(G)
-morphism

f_{c}:\Gamma_{c}\to\Gamma^{\prime}_{c}

for which the diagram

\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{G\times X}{\of}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}_{c}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}_{c}}{X}}

commutes. There are also
\Sf(G)
-morphisms
a_{\Gamma},p_{\Gamma}:\Gamma_{c}\to\Gamma
for which the diagrams

\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a}_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma}{\opi_{2}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{p}_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\XGamma}
\textstyle{\XGamma}

commute. Finally, for any
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
the diagrams

\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{a_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{f_{c}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma^{\prime}}

commute as well.

Proof.

We give this proof by doing a sketch using the points of the quotient varieties for readability. Let
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
. Then define
\Gamma_{c}
by setting

\Gamma_{c}:=\Gamma\times G

with action map

\alpha_{\Gamma_{c}}=\alpha_{\Gamma}\times\Ad

where
\Ad
is the adjoint action of
G
on itself. More explicitly,
\Ad:G\times G\to G
is the morphism given on sets by
\Ad(h,g)=hgh^{-1}
and in categorical terms by

\textstyle{G\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta\times\id_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\Ad}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{G\times G\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id_{G}\times\inv\times\id_{G}}
\textstyle{G\times G\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id_{G}\times s}
\textstyle{G\times G\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu\circ(\id_{G}\times\mu)}

where
\inv:G\to G
is the inversion map and
s
is the switching isomorphism of the product. Now because
\Sf(G)
admits products and both
\Gamma
and
G
are
\Sf(G)
-varieties,
\Gamma_{c}
is a pure dimensional smooth variety. That it is a principal
G
-variety follows from the fact that
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and Proposition
6.1.1
.

We define the morphism of quotient varieties
\mu_{\Gamma}:\,_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\to\,_{\Gamma_{c}}{X}
by (in set-theoretic terms)

[\gamma,(g,x)]_{G}\mapsto[(\gamma,g),x]_{G};

that this is an isomorphism is trivial to verify. Define
f_{c}:\Gamma_{c}\to\Gamma^{\prime}_{c}
by
f_{c}:=f\times\id_{G}
. Then we calculate that

\displaystyle(\of_{c}\circ\mu_{\Gamma})[\gamma,(g,x)]_{G}

\displaystyle=\of_{c}[(\gamma,g),x]_{G}=[(f(\gamma),g),x]_{G}=\mu_{\Gamma^{\prime}}[f(\gamma),(g,x)]_{G}

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\circ\,_{G\times X}{\of})[\gamma,(g,x)]_{G},

which shows that the first diagram commutes.

We define the morphisms
a_{\Gamma},p_{\Gamma}:\Gamma_{c}\to\Gamma
. The map
a_{\Gamma}
is defined by the diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{\Gamma\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id_{\Gamma}\times\inv}
\textstyle{\Gamma\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\Gamma}
\textstyle{G\times\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\Gamma}}

(which says in set-theoretic terms that
a_{\Gamma}(\gamma,g)=g^{-1}\gamma
) while we define
p_{\Gamma}

=

\pi_{2}^{\Gamma G}:\Gamma\times G\to G
to be the second projection. Because
\pi_{2}^{\Gamma G}
is trivially verified to be an
\Sf(G)
-morphism, we only need to do this for
a_{\Gamma}
. For
a_{\Gamma}
we note that it is smooth and of constant fibre dimension, so we only need to show that it is
G
-equivariant. We calculate that on one hand

(a_{\Gamma}\circ\alpha_{\Gamma_{c}})(h,\gamma,g)=a_{\Gamma}(h\gamma,hgh^{-1})=(hgh^{-1})^{-1}h\gamma=hg^{-1}hh^{-1}\gamma=hg^{-1}\gamma

while on the other hand

\big(\alpha_{\Gamma}\circ(\id_{G}\times a_{\Gamma})\big)(h,\gamma,g)=(h,g^{-1}\gamma)=hg^{-1}\gamma.

Thus
a_{\Gamma}
is
G
-equivariant and hence an
\Sf(G)
-morphism.

We now verify the commutativity of the triangles. For this note that the triangle involving
p_{\Gamma}
commutes trivially, so we only verify the triangle for
a_{\Gamma}
. In this case we find that

\displaystyle(\overline{a}_{\Gamma}\circ\mu_{\Gamma})[\gamma,(g,x)]_{G}

\displaystyle=\overline{a}_{\Gamma}[(\gamma,g),x]_{G}=[g^{-1}\gamma,x]_{G}=[\gamma,gx]_{G}

\displaystyle=\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}[\gamma,(g,x)]_{G},

as was desired. Finally if
f\in\Sf(G)_{1}
, the last two squares commute trivially in the cases of
p_{\Gamma},p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and exactly because
f
is
G
-equivariant in the cases of
a_{\Gamma},a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.
∎

Because of the set-theoretic nature of the sketch given above we get the following topological version of Cunningham’s naïvification variety.

Lemma 6.2.13
.

For all
M\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{0}
there exists a free
G
-acyclic manifold
M_{c}\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{0}
such that there is an isomorphism of quotient varieties

\,{}_{M}{(G\times X)}\xrightarrow{\mu_{M}}\,_{M_{c}}{X}.

Moreover, if
f\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{1}
with
f\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)(M,N)
there is a
\mathbf{FAMan}(G)
-morphism

f_{c}:M_{c}\to N_{c}

for which the diagram

\textstyle{\,{}_{M}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{G\times X}{\of}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}_{c}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{N}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{N}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{N_{c}}{X}}

commutes. There are also
\mathbf{FAMan}(G)
-morphisms
a_{M},p_{M}:M_{c}\to M
for which the diagrams

\textstyle{\,{}_{M}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{M}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{a}_{M}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M}{(G\times X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\,{}_{M}{\opi_{2}}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M_{c}}{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{p}_{M}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M}{X}}
\textstyle{\,{}_{M}{X}}

commute. Finally, for any
f\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{1}
the diagrams

\textstyle{M_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{c}}
\scriptstyle{a_{\Gamma}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{M}}
\scriptstyle{f_{c}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{N_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{N}}
\textstyle{N}
\textstyle{N_{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{N}}
\textstyle{N}

commute as well.

We now finally have the tools to provide our claimed isomorphism
\Theta:\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A\to\pi_{2}^{\ast}A
for any object of
F_{G}(X)
. The construction of this isomorphism, in view of Lemma
6.2.12
is straightforward, but the proof that it is an
F_{G}(X)
-morphism is quite difficult and involved. The techniques we use are essentially a generalization of an unpublished proof of C. Cunningham and myself for the equivariant derived category, as the proof we gave establishes (after generalizing) the case when
F
is a strict pseudofunctor.

Theorem 6.2.14
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and
X
a left
G
-variety or let
G
be a topological group and
X
a left
G
-space. For any pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
F=(F,\overline{F})
over
X
and any
A\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
, there is an isomorphism

\Theta_{A}:\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A\to\pi_{2}^{\ast}A

such that for any
P:A\to B
in
F_{G}(X)
the diagram

\textstyle{\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{X}^{\ast}P}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{A}}
\textstyle{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}^{\ast}P}
\textstyle{\alpha_{X}^{\ast}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{B}}
\textstyle{\pi_{2}^{\ast}B}

commutes in
F_{G}(G\times X)
.

Proof.

We prove this in the variety situation, as the topological case follows similarly. Begin by fixing
A\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
and defining the isomorphism
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\theta}
as in the diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}})(\AGamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{\opi_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\aGamma\circ\muGamma)(\AGamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\pGamma\circ\muGamma)(\AGamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\muGamma)\left(\overline{F}(\aGamma)(\AGamma)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\muGamma)\,_{\Gamma_{c}}{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\muGamma)\left(\overline{F}(\pGamma)(\AGamma)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\phi_{\muGamma,a_{\Gamma}}^{\AGamma}\right)^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\muGamma)\tau_{a_{\Gamma}}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\muGamma)(\tau_{p_{\Gamma}}^{A})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\muGamma,p_{\Gamma}}}

We now begin our arduous task of showing that the equation

\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\,_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\theta}=\,_{\Gamma}{\theta}\circ\tau_{f}^{\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A}

holds. For this we first find

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\,_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\theta}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\phi_{\muGammap,a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)\circ\big(\overline{F}(\fGX)\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap)\big)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)

\displaystyle\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)^{-1}.

Now using the naturality of the compositor isomorphisms we calculate that

\displaystyle\big(\overline{F}(\fGX)\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap)\big)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi_{\fGX,\muGammap}^{\overline{F}(\pGammap)\AGammap}\right)^{-1}\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap\circ\fGX)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)\circ\phi_{\fGX,\muGammap}^{\overline{F}(\aGammap)\AGammap}.

Using that there is an equality of functors

\overline{F}(\muGammap\circ\fGX)=\overline{F}(\of_{c}\circ\muGamma)

we have

\displaystyle\overline{F}(\muGammap\circ\fGX)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)=\overline{F}(\of_{c}\circ\muGamma)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)

so using the same trick we write

\displaystyle\overline{F}(\of_{c}\circ\muGamma)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\muGamma,\of_{c}}^{\overline{F}(\aGammap)\AGammap}\circ\big(\overline{F}(\muGamma)\circ\overline{F}(\of_{c})\big)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\phi_{\muGamma,\of_{c}}^{\overline{F}(\aGammap)\AGammap}\right)^{-1}

and get that

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\,_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{\theta}

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)\circ\left(\phi_{\muGammap,a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau_{f}^{\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)\circ\big(\overline{F}(\fGX)\circ\overline{F}(\muGammap)\big)\left(\left(\tau_{p_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)^{-1}\circ\tau_{a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{A}\right)

\displaystyle\circ\overline{F}(\fGX)\left(\phi_{\muGammap,a_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}^{\AGammap}\right)^{-1}
