\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{G\backslash P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{p}}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\backslash X}

Since
U
is a trivializing étale open of
\quo_{X}
by construction we have a commuting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{X}^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{GU}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Use these to define the map
\psi_{V}:\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)\to G
via:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{X}^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{GU}}

Because the
\{\quo_{P}^{-1}(V_{i})\to P\;|\;i\in I\}
are a
G
-stable étale cover of
P
and the quotient
P\to G\backslash P
is geometric, we can glue the maps
\psi_{V}
to produce a morphism
\tau:P\to P
defined locally by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)\times\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{V}\times\id}

Because each of these actions are orbit-stable, it follows that
\tau
coequalizes
\alpha_{P}
and
\pi_{2}^{GP}
, i.e., the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{GP}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

commutes. Consequently, by the universal property of the quotient, there is a unique morphism
\overline{\tau}
making the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\tau}}

commute. By intertwining
\overline{\tau}
with the
G
-action we locally induce a morphism
\sigma_{V}:G\times V\to\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)
which makes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V}}

commute. However, since

G\times V\cong(G\times U)\times_{U}V\cong\quo_{X}^{-1}(U)\times_{U}V

the induced diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\times V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}^{-1}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{X}^{-1}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{V}}

also commutes. Thus the
\sigma_{V}
glue to a map

\sigma:X\times_{G\backslash X}(G\backslash P)\to P

fitting into the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{G\backslash X}(G\backslash P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}}

which implies that
\theta
and
\sigma
are necessarily inverse to each other. Furthermore, that these isomorphisms are natural follows from the fact that each one is induced by the universal property of the pullback.
∎

Lemma 7.3.9
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group,
H
a smooth algebraic subgroup of
G
with inclusion
i:H\to G
, and
X
a locally isotrivial
G
-space. Then for any
G
-resolution
P
of
X
, there is a natural isomorphism

P\cong X\times_{H\backslash X}\left(H\backslash P\right).

In particular, there is an invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Resl_{G/H}\left(H\backslash X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Resl_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Resl_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q\mapsto X\times_{H\backslash X}Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\mapsto H\backslash P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

Because
X
is a free
H
-variety by virtue of
G
being a free
H
-variety, the lemma follows by an application of Lemma
7.3.8
once we equip
X\times_{H\backslash X}Q
with the structure of a
G
-resolution of
X
via the first projection.
∎

Proposition 7.3.10
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group,
X
a free left
G
-variety, and
H\leq G
a smooth algebraic normal subgroup with inclusion
i:H\to G
. Then for any pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
there is an equivalence of categories

F_{G}(X)\simeq F_{G/H}\left(H\backslash X\right)

for the induced pre-equivariant pseudofunctors on
X
and
G\times^{H}X
, respectively.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.3.9
we have a natural isomorphism
\id_{\Resl_{G}(X)}\cong X\times_{H\backslash X}(H\backslash(-))
. A straightforward calculation also shows that for any
G/H
-resolution
Q\to H\backslash X
we have that

H\backslash\left(X\times_{H\backslash X}Q\right)\cong H\backslash X\times_{H\backslash X}Q\cong Q

so
\id_{\Resl_{G/H}(H\backslash X)}\cong H\backslash(X\times_{H\backslash X}(-))
. Consequently it follows that we have an equivalence of categories

\Resl_{G}(X)\simeq\Resl_{G/H}(H\backslash X).

From here the proof of the proposition follows mutatis mutandis to that of Proposition
7.3.3
.
∎

7.4.
Averaging Functors

In this short section we describe averaging functors. While we do not use them in any major fashion in this monograph, we expect them to be of use in future work using the pseudocone perspective on equivariant geometry and topology. We can see some evidence of this already in
[
1
]
where the averaging functors are shown to exist for complex algebraic groups
G
, their closed algebraic subgroups
H
, and left
G
-varieties
X
. Our brief discussion follows the basic construction of
[
1
, Section 6.6]
as a point of departure for generalization. As a first step, however, we need to do some setup and discuss the quotient/induction equivalences.

As we proceed, assume we are in either of the cases below:

(1)

Let
G\leq H
be smooth algebraic groups
G
and
H
with subgroup inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
;

(2)

Let
G\leq H
be topological groups with
G
a closed subgroup and inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
.

If
X
is a left
H
-object, it is frequently of interest in representation theory to forget the
H
-action on
X
and regard
X
as a
G
-variety. In doing so, we also want to keep track of what the change of actions on
X
does to the equivariant information by tracking the various Change of Groups functors
\varphi^{\sharp}:F_{H}(X)\to F_{G}(X)
. However, a natural question is whether or not we can return from the category
F_{G}(X)
to
F_{H}(X)
by enriching the
G
-equivariant information recorded by
F_{G}(X)
to the
H
-equivariant information recorded by
F_{H}(X)
in a way compatible with the Change of Groups functors (so in particular we should not enrich our
G
-equivariant information to
H
-equivariant infromation by fundamentally altering the
G
-equivariant information we started with). This leads to the notion of what we call left and right averaging functors
5
5

5

In
[
1
]
these functors are simply called averaging functors and only differentiated by notation. Because the scope of
[
1
]
is focused on the equivariant derived category of a complex variety, this is reasonable as both averaging functors exist; however, because of the more general
2
-categorical nature of what we are doing here it makes sense to make a more explicit distinction between left and right averaging.
(cf.
[
1
, Definition 6.6.2]
).

Definition 7.4.1
.

If

\underline{{F}}=\left((F,\overline{F}),(E,\overline{E}),e\right)

is a
(G,H)
-pre-equivariant pseudofunctor we say that
\underline{{F}}
has a left averaging functor
\LAvg:E_{G}(X)\to F_{H}(X)
if
\LAvg
is a left adjoint of
\varphi^{\sharp}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LAvg}
\dashv

and dually we say that
\underline{{F}}
has a right averaging functor
\RAvg:E_{G}(X)\to F_{H}(X)
if there
\RAvg
is right adjoint to
\varphi^{\ast}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RAvg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\dashv

Example 7.4.2
.

By
[
1
, Theorem 6.6.1]
when
H
is a complex algebraic group and
G
is a closed algebraic subgroup of
H
then for the bounded derived constructible category

\underline{{D}}=\left((D^{b}_{c}(-),D_{c}^{b}(-)),(D_{c}^{b}(-),D_{c}^{b}(-)),\iota_{D_{c}^{b}(-)}\right)

the left and right averaging functors
\LAvg
and
\RAvg
both exist for
\underline{{D}}
. Note that that
\RAvg
is constructed from equivariant pullback and pushforward while
\LAvg
is constructed from exceptional equivariant pullback and pushforward, this example also shows that in general the left averaging functor differs from the right averaging functors.

We now follow the general idea of the proof of
[
1
, Theorem 6.6.1]
to give conditions on when the averaging functors exist and some corollaries which suggest strategies for using the averaging functors. Recall that there is an
H
-equivariant morphism
i:X\to H\times^{G}X
given, in point-set form, by
x\mapsto[1_{H},x]_{G}
. Similarly, the action map
\alpha_{X}:H\times X\to X
induces a morphism

\overline{\alpha}_{X}:H\times^{G}X\to X

which factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\times^{G}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H/G\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[h,x]_{G}\mapsto[hG,hx]_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

with the top row an
H
-equivariant isomorphism (cf.
[
1
, Section 6.6]
). By construction these maps satisfy the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\times^{G}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}}

and hence, for any pseudofunctor
\overline{F}
, induce a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\overline{\alpha}_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i,\overline{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

By applying the Induction Equivalence of Propositions
7.3.4
and
7.3.6
it follows that for any
(G,H)
-pre-equivariant psuedofunctor

\underline{{F}}=\left((F,\overline{F}),(E,\overline{E}),e\right)

we can factor the Change of Groups functor as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}
denotes the pullback functor as induced by Theorem
4.2.6
. Because the functors
F_{H}(H\times^{G}X)\to F_{G}(X)
and
F_{G}(X)\to E_{G}(X)
are equivalences, assuming that we have promoted the Induction equivalence to an adjoint equivalence
6
6

6

Because
e
comes from an adjoint equivalence in a bicategory
\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})
and
\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})
are already adjoint equivalences.
, we always have adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\dashv

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}\left(X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\dashv

where the corresponding functors are both left and right adjoint to each other. Thus in determining if
\varphi^{\sharp}:F_{H}(X)\to E_{G}(X)
has a left or right adjoint we can reduce to asking if the functor
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}:F_{H}(X)\to F_{H}(H\times^{G}X)
admits a left or right adjoint.

Proposition 7.4.3
.

Assume we are in either of the cases below:

(1)

Let
G\leq H
be smooth algebraic groups
G
and
H
with subgroup inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
;

(2)

Let
G\leq H
be topological groups with
G
a closed subgroup and inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
.

If the functor
\overline{\alpha}_{X}:F_{H}(X)\to F_{H}(H\times^{G}X)
induced by Theorem
4.2.6
has a left (respectively right) adjoint
L:F_{H}(H\times^{G}X)\to F_{H}(X)
then
\varphi^{\sharp}:F_{H}(X)\to E_{G}(X)
admits a left (respecitvely right) averaging functor.

Proof.

We show only the left averaging case as the right case is dual. Assume that
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}
has a left adjoint
L
. Because the existence of adjoints is stable under isomorphism, we can replace
\varphi^{\sharp}
with the composite:

F_{H}(X)\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}}F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)\xrightarrow{P}F_{G}(X)\xrightarrow{\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}E_{G}(X)

Writing the adjoint diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})\circ P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda\circ\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\dashv
\dashv

shows that
L\circ\Lambda\circ\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})\dashv\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})\circ P\circ\overline{\alpha}_{X}
and hence that

L\circ\Lambda\circ\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})\dashv\varphi^{\sharp}
. Setting
\LAvg:=L\circ\Lambda\circ\PC(e^{-1}\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})
thus completes the proof.
∎

While the pullback construction above is very useful, Example
7.4.2
shows that the Change of Groups functor
\varphi^{\sharp}
can admit left and right averaging functors where at least one of the averaging functors is not induced by pullback functors given by Theorem
4.2.6
; instead we need to invoke the more general language of psuedocone translations to give rise to the technology which allows us to admit both left and right averaging functors. In essence this discussion simply proceeds mutatis mutandis to the discussion prior to the statmenet of Proposition
7.4.3
. If we have a pseudocone translation functor
\overline{\alpha}^{\square}:F_{H}(X)\to F_{H}(H\times^{G}X)
for which we can factor the Change of Groups functor as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

then we can proceed exactly as before and use adjoints to
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}
to give the existence of Averaging Functors. Namely, the essential ingredients towards this technique for proving the existence of averaging functors comes down to factoring the Change of Groups functor itself by a pseudocone translation against
\overline{\alpha}_{X}
followed by the Induction Equivalence and then using the existence of left/right adjoints to
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}
to give left/right averaging functors.

Proposition 7.4.4
.

Assume we are in either of the cases below:

(1)

Let
G\leq H
be smooth algebraic groups
G
and
H
with subgroup inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
;

(2)

Let
G\leq H
be topological groups with
G
a closed subgroup and inclusion morphism
i:G\to H
.

Let
\underline{{F}}
be a
(G,H)
-pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. Assume that we have pseudocone a translation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{H}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo_{H}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo_{H}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}^{\square}}

for which there is an invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}\left(H\times^{G}X\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PC(e\ast\quo_{G}^{\op})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Then if
\overline{\alpha}_{X}^{\square}
has a right adjoint (respectively a left adjoint),
\underline{{F}}
has a right averaging functor (respectively a left averaging functor).

Proof.

The proof follows mutatis mutandis to the proof of Proposition
7.4.3
.
∎

We close this section with an example illustrating the existence of averaging functors for the
\ell
-adic equivariant derived category.

Example 7.4.5

(
[
1
, Theorem 6.6.1]
)
.

Let
H
be a smooth algebraic group with a closed smooth algebraic subgroup
G
and inclusion morphism
\varphi:G\to H
. Let
X
be a left
H
-variety and consider the
(G,H)
-pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\underline{{D}}
induced by the constructible bounded derived pseudofunctor

D^{b}_{c}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}):\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat.

Then because
\overline{\alpha}^{\ast}\dashv R\overline{\alpha}_{\ast}
, the right averaging functor exists by Proposition
7.4.3
. Similarly, using the exceptional pullbacks and pushforwards of Corollary
4.2.13
also allows us to factor the Change of Groups functor as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{H}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{H}^{b}\left(H\times^{G}X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\alpha}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

This, together with the adjunction
R\overline{\alpha}_{!}\dashv\overline{\alpha}^{!}
gives the existence of the of the left averaging functor
\LAvg
by Proposition
7.4.4
.

Chapter 8
Equivariant Six Functor Formalism and Equivariant Trace

In this chapter we give a culmination of our categorical and geometric/topological techniques by illustrating how they can be used to study both the six functor formalisms on the categories
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
in what amount to essentially purely categorical terms as well as traces for equivariant derived categories.

While the six functor formalism for
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is well-known, our goal here is to illustrate how to use the pseudocone and descent-theoretic techniques can be used to make developing and using the six functor formalism relatively straightforward. For instance, we will show below how the commutativity of the cohomology functors
H_{G}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
{}^{p}H_{G}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
with the forgetful functors
\Forget:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({G};\overline{\Q}_{\ell}){X}\to\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
\Forget:\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are essentially consequences of the pseudocone formalism developed in the first half of this monograph. We will also see that the open/closed distinguished triangles which appear in the six functor formalism for the equivariant derived category
\mathnormal{D}^{\mathnormal{b}}_{{G}}({X})
arise from pseudocone techniques combined with some equivariant topology and geometry we will develop.

Changing gears from studying the various extra properties of the six functor formalism on
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
, we move to establish and study some basic properties of a notion of trace for the equivariant derived category
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. The notion of a trace associated to an object is very important in representation theory, algebraic geometry, and number theory; in fact, Deligne’s first proof of the third Weil conjecture (cf.
[
14
]
or
[
19
]
for an expository textbook account), following a trick of Grothendieck, used the trace of the Frobenius operators acting on the
\ell
-adic cohomology
H^{\bullet}_{\text{{\'{e}}t}}(V;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
of an
\Fbb_{q}
-variety
V
as a crucial step. Extending these traces to objects (as is important in homological algebra, algebraic topology, and the determination of dualizable objects; cf., for instance,
[
43
]
) directly by associating the trace of a canonical endomorphism allows us to use these techniques in a general level. While this has been done in a stack-theoretic way for the equivariant derived category (using, implicitly, an equivalence
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq D^{b}_{c}([G\backslash X];\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
which is either definitional or a theorem) we show that the existence of traces for the equivariant derived category arises, in essence, as a consequence of the pseudocone construction of
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. Additionally we establish various functoriality properties of the trace with an eye towards future applications.

8.1.
A Categorical Approach towards Six Functor Formalisms

We begin this chaptering by proving the existence of open/closed exact triangles in the equivariant derived category
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. For this, however, we will need some basic results regarding the existence these triangles at the
\Gamma
-local level and how they interact with immersions. Consequently, our main topological/geometric technique will be to show that immersions descend through the category of resolutions, i.e., that if we have an (open/closed) immersion
i:U\to V
then the induced map
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\imath}}:\,_{\Gamma}{U}\to\,_{\Gamma}{V}
is an (open/closed) immersion as well. We start this by considering closed immersions. As we proceed through this section, we will also establish the topological analogue of the geometric results as well.

Lemma 8.1.1
.

Let
i:V\to X
be an equivariant closed immersion of varieties. Then for any
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
the morphism

\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}:\,_{\Gamma}{V}\to\XGamma

is a closed immersion.

Proof.

Asking for the map
i
to be equivariant is simply to ensure that the product
\Gamma\times V
is a
G
-variety so
\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}
is defined. Recall that a map
\zeta:Y\to Z
determines a closed immersion if and only if
\lvert\zeta\rvert(\lvert Y\rvert)
is closed in
\lvert Z\rvert
and there is a short exact sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Iscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{\ast}\CalO_{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{\sharp}}

where
\Iscr
is a sheaf of ideals on
Z
. We will first prove that we obtain the desired short exact sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves on
\XGamma
and then show the set

\left\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)

is closed in
\lvert\XGamma\rvert
. Consider the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}}

of varieties. Because the identity map is closed and
i
is closed,
\id_{\Gamma}\times i
is closed and so there is a short exact sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Kscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

on
\Gamma\times X
where
\Kscr
is a sheaf of ideals on
\Gamma\times X
.

We now apply the functor
(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}
and use the fact that since
\quo_{\Gamma\times X}:\Gamma\times X\to\XGamma
is a smooth morphism of varieties (and hence is a smooth separated quasi-compact map between Noetherian schemes), the pushforward gives an exact functor

(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}:\QCoh(\Gamma\times X)\to\QCoh(\XGamma).

As such the sequence of quasi-coherent sheaves

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\Iscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\left((\id_{\Gamma}\times i)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}\right)}

is short exact (and hence the sheaf
(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\Iscr
is a sheaf of ideals on
\XGamma
). Furthermore, because the quotient
\XGamma
is a geometric quotient, the Zariski topology on
\XGamma
coincides with the quotient topology. Thus there is an isomorphism

(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}\cong\CalO_{\XGamma}

so we get that our short exact sequence above is isomorphic to the short exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\Iscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{\XGamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\left((\id_{\Gamma}\times i)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}\right)}

Now from

\displaystyle(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\left((\id_{\Gamma}\times i)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left(\quo_{\Gamma\times X}\circ(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)\right)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\circ\quo_{\Gamma\times V}\right)_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right)_{\ast}\left((\quo_{\Gamma\times V})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times V}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left(\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right)_{\ast}\CalO_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}},

where the last isomorphism follows mutatis mutandis to the isomorphism

(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}\cong\CalO_{\XGamma},

we find that the short exact sequence is isomorphic to the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\Iscr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{\XGamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right)_{\ast}\CalO_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

of quasi-coherent sheaves on
\XGamma
.

We now argue that
\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)
is closed in
\lvert\XGamma\rvert
. For this we recall that since the topology on
\XGamma
is a quotient topology, for any set
W\subseteq\lvert\XGamma\rvert,

W
is closed (open, respectively) if and only if
\lvert\quo_{\Gamma\times X}\rvert^{-1}(W)
is closed (open, respectively) in
\lvert\Gamma\times X\rvert
. As such consider that

\left\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)=\{[\gamma,i(v)]_{G}\;:\;\gamma\in\lvert\Gamma\rvert,v\in\lvert V\rvert\}

and note

\displaystyle\left\lvert\quo_{\Gamma\times X}\right\rvert^{-1}\left(\left\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\right\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{(\gamma,x)\in\lvert\Gamma\times X\rvert\;:\;(\gamma,x)\in\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\gamma,x)\;:\;\exists\,v\in\lvert V\rvert.\,[\gamma,x]_{G}=[\gamma,i(v)]_{G}\right\}=\lvert\Gamma\times V\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\id_{\Gamma}\times i\rvert\left(\lvert\Gamma\times V\rvert\right).

Because
\id_{\Gamma}\times i
is closed, the above preimage is closed as well. This shows that
\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\rvert(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert)
is closed in
\lvert\XGamma\rvert
. Thus
\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}
is a closed immersion.
∎

Because the argument here adapts mutatis mutandis to topological spaces (by simply taking the closed component of the argument), we get the following topological analogue of Lemma
8.1.1
.

Lemma 8.1.2
.

Let
G
be a topological group which admits
n
-acyclic resolutions which are manifolds for all
n\in\N
and let
X
be a left
G
-space. If
i:V\to X
is an equivariant closed inclusion then for any smooth
G
-resolution
\Gamma
, the inclusion

G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times i):G\backslash(\Gamma\times V)\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

is closed.

Lemma 8.1.3
.

Let
j:U\to X
be an equivariant open immersion of varieties and let
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
. The map

\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}:\,_{\Gamma}{U}\to\XGamma

is an open immersion of varieties.

Proof.

That the subset
\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}\rvert(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{U}\rvert)
is open in
\lvert\XGamma\rvert
is dual to the fact that the image
\lvert\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\rvert\left(\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{V}\rvert\right)
in Lemma
8.1.1
is closed and hence is omitted. By
[
24
, Proposition 4.2.2.a]
we only need to show that for any point
[\gamma,u]_{G}\in\lvert\,_{\Gamma}{U}\rvert
there is an isomorphism of local rings

(\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)})^{\ast}_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}:\CalO_{\XGamma,[\gamma,j(u)]_{G}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\CalO_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{U},[\gamma,u]_{G}}.

For this we note that because
\id_{\Gamma}\times j
is itself an open immersion, for each
(\gamma,u)\in\lvert\Gamma\times U\rvert
there is an isomorphism of local rings

(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)^{\ast}_{(\gamma,u)}:\CalO_{\Gamma\times X,(\gamma,j(u))}\xrightarrow{\cong}\CalO_{\Gamma\times U,(\gamma,u)}.

Using the smooth surjectivity of the quotient morphisms, taking stalks, and the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\XGamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}}

we find that the isomorphism
(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)^{\ast}_{(j,u)}
descends to an isomorphism of local rings based induced by the stalks at the point
\quo_{\Gamma\times U}(\gamma,u)=[\gamma,u]_{G}
:

\rho_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}:(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\left(\CalO_{\Gamma\times X,(\gamma,j(u))}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}(\quo_{\Gamma\times U})_{\ast}\left(\CalO_{\Gamma\times U,(\gamma,u)}\right)

which is equal to

\rho_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}:\left((\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}\right)_{[\gamma,j(u)]_{G}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\left((\quo_{\Gamma\times U})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times U}\right)_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}.

Using the isomorphisms
(\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}\cong\CalO_{\XGamma}
and
(\quo_{\Gamma\times U})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times U}\cong\CalO_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}}
we then find
\rho_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}
fits into a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\quo_{\Gamma\times X})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times X}\right)_{[\gamma,j(u)]_{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left((\quo_{\Gamma\times U})_{\ast}\CalO_{\Gamma\times U}\right)_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{\XGamma,[\gamma,j(u)]_{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\CalO_{\,{}_{\Gamma}{U},[\gamma,u]_{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}^{\ast}_{[\gamma,j(u)]_{G}}}

where the bottom map is
\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}^{\ast}
by construction of taking these particular stalks and passing through the various isomorphisms. From a diagram chase it follows that
\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}_{[\gamma,u]_{G}}^{\ast}
is an isomorphism and hence that
\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}
is an open immersion.
∎

Lemma 8.1.4
.

Let
j:U\to X
be an equivariant open immersion of spaces and let
\Gamma
be a smooth
G
-resolution. The map

\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}:\,_{\Gamma}{U}\to\XGamma

is open.

Proof.

In this case the result follows immediately from the fact that quotients by group actions are open maps and the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times j}}

commutes with each of
\id_{\Gamma}\times j,

\quo_{\Gamma\times U}
, and
\quo_{\Gamma\times X}
open morphisms.
∎

Lemma 8.1.5
.

Let
h:W\to X
be an equivariant immersion of varieties and let
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
. Then the map

\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times h)}:\,_{\Gamma}{W}\to\XGamma

is an immersion.

Proof.

If
h=i\circ j
for
i
closed and
j
open, applying Lemmas
8.1.3
and
8.1.1
together with the observation

\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times h)}=\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times(i\circ j))}=\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times i)}\circ\overline{(\id_{\Gamma}\times j)}

gives the result. In the other case, where
h=j\circ i
for
j
open and
i
closed, we simply apply Lemmas
8.1.1
and
8.1.3
in the opposite order of the prior case.
∎

Following the same basic structure as the proof above we get the corresponding topological version.

Lemma 8.1.6
.

Let
h:W\to X
be a locally closed inclusion of topolgoical spaces and let
\Gamma
be a smooth
G
-resolution. Then the map

G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times h):G\backslash(\Gamma\times W)\to G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)

is a locally closed inclusion as well provided that
W
is equipped with a
G
-action for which
h
is
G
-equivariant.

These various immersion lemmas allow us to deduce the existence of the open and closed immersion distinguished triangles in the equivariant derived category. However, because the existence of these triangles holds in principle for a larger class of equivariant categories than just the equivariant derived categories, we introduce some terminology to describe these.

Definition 8.1.7
.

Let
\underline{{F}}=(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor. If
\overline{F}
is a triangulated pseudofunctor, then we say that
\underline{{F}}
is a triangulated pre-equivariant pseudofunctor.

Definition 8.1.8
.

Let
\underline{{F}}=(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor. If
\overline{F}
is a truncated pseudofunctor, then we say that
\underline{{F}}
is a truncated pre-equivariant pseudofunctor.

Proposition 8.1.9
.

Assume we are in either of the cases below:

(1)

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group,
X
a left
G
-variety,
j:U\to X
a
G
-equivariant open immersion, and
i:V\to X
the corresponding (
G
-equivariant) complementary closed immersion;

(2)

Let
G
be a topological group,
X
a left
G
-space,
j:U\to X
a
G
-equivariant open inclusion, and
i:V\to X
the corresponding (
G
-equivariant) complementary closed inclusion.

Assume
\underline{{F}}=(F,\overline{F})
is a triangulated pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
such that:

•

The pullback pseudofunctors
j^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(U)
and
i^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(V)
induced by Theorem
4.2.6
have right adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\ast}}
\dashv

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\ast}}
\dashv

induced by Theorem
4.2.11
.

•

There are pseudocone translations and optranslations which give rise to functors

j^{\square}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(U)\qquad i^{\square}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(V)

and

j_{\square}:F_{G}(U)\to F_{G}(X)\qquad i_{\square}:F_{G}(V)\to F_{G}(X)

which have adjoints

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(U)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\square}}
\dashv

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\square}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\square}}
\dashv

•

For any free
G
-resolution
\Gamma
there is a natural open/closed distinguished triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\,_{\Gamma}{j}_{\square}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{j}^{\square})(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\overline{F}(\XGamma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\,_{\Gamma}{i}_{\ast}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{i}^{\ast})(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{j}_{\square}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{j}^{\square}\right)[1]\right)(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\varepsilon^{j}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\eta^{i}}}

in
\overline{F}(\XGamma)
where
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\varepsilon}^{j}:\,_{\Gamma}{j}_{\square}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{j}^{\square}\Rightarrow\id_{\overline{F}(\XGamma)}
and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\eta}^{i}:\id_{\overline{F}(\XGamma)}\Rightarrow\,_{\Gamma}{i}_{\ast}\circ\,_{\Gamma}{i}^{\ast}
are the counits and units of adjunction, respectively.

Then for every object
A
in
F_{G}(X)
there is an open/closed distinguished triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(j_{\square}\circ j^{\square})(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\ast}\circ i^{\ast})(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\left(j_{\square}\circ j^{\square}\right)(A)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{j}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}^{i}}

Proof.

The assumptions in the statement of the proposition imply by Theorem
5.1.10
that if we can show that
\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}
and
\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}
are complementary open and closed subobjects of
\XGamma
for all
\Gamma
then the corresponding triangle in
F_{G}(X)
is distinguished. However, this is immediate by the fact that
i
and
j
are
G
-equivariant: fix a term
[\gamma,x]_{G}
in
\,{}_{\Gamma}{X}
. Note that because
i
and
j
are equivariant,
[\gamma,x]_{G}
is in the space
\,{}_{\Gamma}{V}\cap\,_{\Gamma}{U}
if and only if
x\in U\cap V
, which is impossible because
U\cap V=\varnothing
.
∎

We now can use the proposition above to prove the existence of open/closed distinguished triangles for the equivariant derived categories on varieties and on spaces.

Corollary 8.1.10
.

Let
j:U\to X
and be a
G
-equivariant open immersion and let
i:V\to X
be the corresponding closed immersion. Then for any object
A\in\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
there is a distinguished triangle of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{\ast}\left(i^{\ast}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}A\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}^{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A}^{i}}

where
\varepsilon^{j}:Rj_{!}\circ j^{!}\Rightarrow\id_{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
is the counit of adjunction and
\eta^{i}:\id_{\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}\Rightarrow Ri_{\ast}\circ i^{\ast}
is the unit of adjunction.

Proof.

Because the derived categories
D_{c}^{b}(Z)
and
D_{c}^{b}(Z;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
have open/closed distinguished triangles with respect to the pushforward/pullback and exceptional pullback/exceptional pushforward adjunctions, the result follows by applying Proposition
8.1.9
.
∎

Corollary 8.1.11
.

Let
G
be a topological group for which
G
admits
n
-acylic resolutions which are manifolds for all
n\in\N
and let
X
be a left
G
-space. Let
j:U\to X
be a
G
-equivariant open inclusion and let
i:V\to X
be the corresponding (
G
-equivariant) closed inclusion. Then for any object
A
of
D^{b}_{G}(X)
there are distinguished triangles of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{\ast}\left(i^{\ast}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}A\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{j}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{i}_{A}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{!}\left(i^{!}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{\ast}\left(j^{\ast}A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Ri_{!}\left(i^{!}A\right)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{i}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{j}_{A}}

where
\varepsilon^{j}:Rj_{!}\circ j^{!}\Rightarrow\id_{D_{G}^{b}(X)}
and
\varepsilon^{i}:Ri_{!}\circ i^{!}\Rightarrow\id_{D_{G}^{b}(X)}
are the counits of adjunction while
\eta^{i}:\id_{D_{G}^{b}({X})}\Rightarrow Ri_{\ast}\circ i^{\ast}
and
\eta^{j}:\id_{D^{b}_{G}(X)}\Rightarrow Rj_{\ast}\circ j^{\ast}
are the units of adjunction.

Proof.

The basic structure of the argument follows mutatis mutandis to the proof of Corollary
8.1.10
in the case of the first (family of) distinguished triangle(s). From here the existence of the second (family of) distinguished triangle(s) follows from the fact that there are distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(R(\,_{M}{\overline{\imath}})_{!}\circ\,_{M}{\overline{\imath}}^{!}\right)(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{D^{b}(\,_{M}{X})}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(R(\,_{M}{\overline{\jmath}})_{\ast}\circ\,_{M}{\overline{\jmath}}^{\ast}\right)(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\left(R(\,_{M}{\overline{\imath}})_{!}\circ\,_{M}{\overline{\imath}}^{!}\right)(-)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{M}{\varepsilon^{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{M}{\eta^{j}}}

for each
M\in\mathbf{FAMan}(G)_{0}
and then proceeding as in the proof of Proposition
8.1.9
.
∎

We now prove that the forgetful functor of Proposition
6.1.17
(and also Proposition
7.1.19
), applied to the equivariant derived category pre-equvariant pseudofunctors and the standard and perverse cohomology functors
H^{0}_{G}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
,
{}^{p}H_{G}^{0}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
, commute up to isomorphism for smooth algebraic groups
G
and left
G
-varieties
X
and in both the variety-theoretic and topological cases for the equivariant derived categories.

For what follows we will need to recall a technical result from algebraic geometry. Additionally, we will also need to define a special notation to denote the conventional perspective on equivariant sheaves and perverse sheaves which is distinct from the pseudocone language we have been using so far. This leads us to the following lemma and definition of convenience.

Lemma 8.1.12

(
[
56
,
Tag 0F7L
]
)
.

Consider a pullback diagram of quasi-compact and quasi-separated schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where
f
is a separated morphism of finite type. Then there is a natural isomorphism

h^{\ast}\circ Rf_{!}\cong Rk_{!}\circ g^{\ast}.

Definition 8.1.13
.

Let
G
be either a smooth algebraic group or a topological space which admits
n
-acylcic resolutions which are manifolds for all
n\in\N
and let
X
be a left
G
-variety or a left
G
-space, respectively. We then write the following when
G
is an algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Shv\left(G\backslash(-)\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X)

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant sheaves}

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Shv\left(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant}\,\ell\text{-adic sheaves}

\displaystyle\Per_{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Per\left(G\backslash(-)\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Per_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X)

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant perverse sheaves}

\displaystyle\Per_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Per\left(G\backslash(-);\overline{\Q}_{\ell}\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Per_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant}\,\ell\text{-adic perverse sheaves}

and

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Shv\left(G\backslash(-)\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X)

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant sheaves}

\displaystyle\Per_{G}(X)

\displaystyle:=\PC\left(\Per\left(G\backslash(-)\right):\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}\to\fCat\right)

\displaystyle\Per_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X)

\displaystyle:=\text{traditional definition of equivariant perverse sheaves}

when
G
is a topological group and
X
is a left
G
-space.

Observe that because of the equivalences of Corollaries
5.1.27
and Corollary
5.1.28
we have invertible
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{\Spec K}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{\Spec K}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({1_{G}^{\sharp}};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\Spec K}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{D}^{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(1_{G}^{\sharp})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Shv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({1_{G}^{\sharp}};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
is the Change of Groups functor along the identity inclusion
1_{G}:\Spec K\to G
against the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
(and similarly for
\Shv(1_{G}^{\sharp})
; cf. Theorem
7.1.13
) and where
\overline{D}^{b}
and
\overline{\Shv}
are the equivalence witnesses for

D^{b}_{\Spec K}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and

\Shv_{\Spec K}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

furnished by Proposition
2.3.13
. Note that the inner squares form invertible
2
-cells via a routine use of Proposition
7.1.16
. Pasting all the cells together we get the invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Adding the invertible
2
-cell induced via the equivalence of categories

\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\simeq\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

of Corollary
5.1.27
fitting into the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

produces the total diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Note that the existence of the invertible
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per_{G}^{\textrm{na{\"{i}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

follows mutatis mutandis to the diagram above, save from using Corollary
5.1.28
instead of Corollary
5.1.27
. Putting these together gives the proposition below.

Proposition 8.1.14
.

For any smooth algebraic group
G
and any left
G
-variety
X
there are invertible
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

That is, the forgetful functors on the equivariant derived category restrict to the forgetful functors on equivariant perverse sheaves and equivarant
\ell
-adic sheaves. Moreover, we also have invertible
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}({X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{c}^{b}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv_{G}^{\operatorname{na\ddot{\imath}ve}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Shv(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}^{b}({X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{c}^{b}({X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Per(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H_{G}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{p}H^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

whenever
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety or whenever
G
is a topological group which admits free
n
-acyclic
G
-spaces which are manifolds for all
n\in\N
and
X
is a left
G
-space.

We now show that the each of the
\ell
-adic equivariant six functors
h^{\ast},

Rh_{\ast},

Rh_{!},

h^{!},

(-)\otimes(-),
and
R[-,-]
commute up to isomorphism with the forgetful functors when
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
X
is a left
G
-variety. Because the proof we give below is essentially entirely formal in nature and mainly organizes various coherence results and commutativity witnesses together, we will also be able to adapt it to the topological and non-
\ell
-adic cases more-or-less freely.

Proposition 8.1.15
.

Let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism for a smooth algebraic group
G
and left
G
-varieties
X
and
Y
. Then each of the six functors of Corollaries
4.1.21
,
4.2.7
,
4.2.12
, and
4.2.13
commute with the forgetful functors up to isomorphism for both the
\ell
-adic equivariant derived category and standard equivariant derived category.

Proof.

We begin by writing the non-equivariant isomorphisms
\psi_{X}:X\to\,_{G}{X}
and
\psi_{Y}:Y\to\,_{G}{Y}
of Proposition
6.1.17
. Note that these are natural in the sense that the diagram of varieties

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}}

commutes. We also write
\Forget_{X}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
for the corresponding forgetful functors of Proposition
6.1.17
associated to the equivariant derived category
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and similarly for
\Forget_{Y}:\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
.

Let us now show that the natural isomorphism
\Forget_{X}\circ h^{\ast}\cong h^{\ast}\circ\Forget_{Y}

exists. We calculate that on one hand, for any
A\in\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
,

\displaystyle\Forget_{X}\left(h^{\ast}A\right)

\displaystyle=\Forget_{X}\left(\left\{\hGamma^{\ast}\left(\AGamma\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0},\AGamma\in A\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)=\left(\psi_{X}^{\ast}\circ\,_{G}{\overline{h}}^{\ast}\right)\,_{G}{A}.

On the other hand

h^{\ast}\left(\Forget_{Y}(A)\right)=h^{\ast}\left(\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)=\left(h^{\ast}\circ\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right);

using the natural isomorphism of functors
h^{\ast}\circ\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\cong\psi_{X}^{\ast}\circ\,_{G}{\overline{h}}^{\ast}
thus gives the desired invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We now show the existence of the natural isomorphism

\Forget_{X}\circ h^{!}\cong h^{!}\circ\Forget_{Y}.

Because the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}}

is a pullback diagram with both morphisms
\psi_{X}
and
\psi_{Y}
smooth (by virtue of being isomorphisms — isomorphisms are étale and hence smooth of relative dimension zero) we have a natural isomorphism of functors
h^{!}\circ\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\cong\psi_{X}^{\ast}\circ\,_{G}{\overline{h}}^{!}
. Using this we calculate for any
A\in\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}

\displaystyle\Forget_{X}\left(h^{!}A\right)

\displaystyle=\Forget_{X}\left(\left\{\hGamma^{!}\left(\AGamma\right)\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0},\AGamma\in A\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}^{!}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)=\left(\psi_{X}^{\ast}\circ\,_{G}{\overline{h}}^{!}\right)\,_{G}{A}

\displaystyle\cong\left(h^{!}\circ\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\right)\,_{G}{A}=h^{!}\left(\psi_{Y}^{\ast}(\,_{G}{A})\right)

\displaystyle=h^{!}\left(\Forget_{Y}\left\{\AGamma\;|\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}\right\}\right)=h^{!}\left(\Forget_{Y}(A)\right)

This establishes the invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We now prove the existence of the natural isomorphism

Rh_{\ast}\circ\Forget_{X}\cong\Forget_{Y}\circ Rh_{\ast}.

As in the case above, we use that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}}

is a pullback diagram of varieties with both
\psi_{X}
and
\psi_{Y}
smooth morphisms. Thus we get isomorphisms of functors
Rh_{\ast}\circ\psi_{X}^{\ast}\cong\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\circ R(\,_{G}{\overline{h}})_{\ast}
by the Smooth Base Change Theorem. Proceeding as in the two prior cases we derive that for any
A\in\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
,

\displaystyle(Rh_{\ast}\circ\Forget_{X})A

\displaystyle=\left(Rh_{\ast}\circ\psi_{X}^{\ast}\right)\,_{G}{A}\cong\left(\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\circ R(\,_{G}{\overline{h}})_{\ast}\right)\,_{G}{A}

\displaystyle=(\Forget_{Y}\circ Rh_{\ast})A

which gives the invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

In fact, using that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\overline{h}}}

satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
8.1.12
(because every morphism and scheme in sight is finite type and separated) we get that
Rh_{!}\circ\psi_{X}^{\ast}\cong\psi_{Y}^{\ast}\circ R(\,_{G}{\overline{h}})_{!}
. Proceeding mutatis mutandis to the pusforward case gives the invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({Y};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rh_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We now show that the tensor products commute with the forgetful functor, i.e., there is a natural isomorphism

\Forget_{X}\left(A\overset{L}{\otimes}B\right)\cong\Forget_{X}(A)\overset{L}{\otimes}\Forget_{X}(B)

for any
A,B\in\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. For this we calculate that on one hand

\displaystyle\Forget_{X}\left(A\overset{L}{\otimes}B\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\underset{\XGamma}{\overset{L}{\otimes}}\,_{G}{B}\right)

while on the other hand

\Forget_{X}(A)\overset{L}{\otimes}\Forget_{X}(B)=\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right).

Because
\psi:X\to\,_{G}{X}
is smooth, there is a a natural isomorphism

\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\underset{\XGamma}{\overset{L}{\otimes}}\,_{G}{B}\right)\cong\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\overset{L}{\otimes}\psi_{X}^{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right)

which gives an invertible
2
-cell:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\times\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\times\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{L}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}\times\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{L}{\otimes}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Following this argument mutatis mutandis for the functors
R[-,-]
gives an invertible
2
-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\op}\times\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{G}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\op}\times\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({X};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[-,-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}^{\op}\times\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Forget_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R[-,-]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and completes the proof of the proposition for the
\ell
-adic case. Proceeding mutatis mutandis also establishes the same results for the equivariant derived categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(Y)
.
∎

Following the same proof mutatis mutandis for the topological case gives the following topological results.

Proposition 8.1.16
.

Let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism for a topology group
G
which admits free
n
-acyclic
G
-spaces which are manifolds and left
G
-varieties
X
and
Y
. Then each of the six functors of Corollaries
4.1.22
,
4.2.8
,
4.2.14
, and
4.2.15
between
D^{b}_{G}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(Y)
commute with the forgetful functors up to isomorphism.

Putting everything together in this section gives the theorems below which state that in both the topological and geometric cases the equivariant derived categories are good homes for equivariant cohomology. The good news, however, is that we have already done most of the work required to show this and now just have to collect the various corollaries, propositions, and such to record this explicitly. In particular, this tells us quite practically that the existence and structure of the equivariant six functor formalism follows from the properties of the
\PC(-)
construction and how it interacts with the corresponding equivariant geometry and equivariant topology.

Theorem 8.1.17
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism of left
G
-varieties. Then the following hold for both pseudofunctors
D_{c}^{b}(-;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}):\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
and
D_{c}^{b}(-):\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
.

(1)

The categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D^{b}_{G}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are symmetric monoidal closed categories.

(2)

The categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are triangulated categories which have standard and perverse
t
-structures for which

D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{stand}}}\simeq\Shv_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\Per_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

and similarly for
D_{G}^{b}(X)
.

(3)

There are equivariant pullback and equivariant exceptional pullback functors
h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
h^{!}:D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
as well as equivariant pushforward and equivariant proper pushforward functors
Rh_{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
Rh_{!}:D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})\to D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. The same also holds for
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(Y)
.

(4)

There are adjunctions
h^{\ast}\dashv Rh_{\ast}
and
Rh_{!}\dashv h^{!}
.

(5)

For any
G
-equivariant open immersion
j:U\to X
and corresponding
G
-equivariant closed immersion
i:V\to X
there is an open/closed distinguished triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{\ast}\left(i^{\ast}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}(-)\right)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{j}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{-}^{i}}

in
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and the same is true of
D_{G}^{b}(X)
.

(6)

The standard and perverse
t
-structure cohomology functors on
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
commute up to isomorphism with the forgetful functors and the same is true of the standard and perverse
t
-structures on
D_{G}^{b}(X)
.

(7)

The six functors
(-)\otimes(-),

R[-,-]
,
h^{\ast}
,
Rh_{\ast}
,
Rh_{!}
, and
h^{!}
all commute up to isomorphism with the corresponding forgetful functors.

(8)

There are natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle Rh_{!}(A)\underset{Y}{\otimes}B

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{!}\left(A\underset{X}{\otimes}h^{\ast}(B)\right),

\displaystyle\left[Rh_{!}(A),B\right]_{Y}

\displaystyle\cong Rh_{\ast}\left(\left[A,h^{!}(B)\right]_{X}\right),

\displaystyle h^{!}\left([C,B]_{Y}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\left[h^{\ast}(C),h^{!}(B)\right]_{X}

where
A\in D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
and
B,C\in D_{G}^{b}(Y;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})_{0}
. The same also holds for
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(Y)
.

Proof.

We prove the different aspects of the theorem in the order listed in the statement of the theorem.

(1)

The fact that both categories
D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
are symmetric monoidal closed is Corollary
4.1.21
.

(2)

That both
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and
D_{G}^{b}(X)
are triangulated follows from Corollary
5.1.12
while the existence of the standard and perverse
t
-structures follow from Corollaries
5.1.27
and
5.1.28
.

(3)

The existence of the functors listed follows from Corollaries
4.2.7
,
4.2.12
, and
4.2.13
.

(4)

The adjunctions listed are given in Corollaries
4.2.14
and
4.2.13
.

(5)

The existence of the open/closed distinguished triangle is Corollary
8.1.10
.

(6)

The commutativity of the cohomology functors with the forgetful functors, up to isomorphism, is the content of Proposition
8.1.14
.

(7)

The commutativity of the six functors with the forgetful functors is Proposition
8.1.15
.

(8)

The given natural isomorphisms follow from Corollary
5.2.2
.

∎

Theorem 8.1.18
.

Let
G
be a topological group which admits
n
-acyclic free
G
-spaces which are manifolds and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism of left
G
-spaces. Then the following hold for the pseudofunctor
D_{c}^{b}(-):\Top^{\op}\to\fCat
.

(1)

The category
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is symmetric monoidal closed.

(2)

The category
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is triangulated and has standard and perverse
t
-structures for which

D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{stand}}}\simeq\Shv_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),

and

D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})^{\heartsuit_{\operatorname{per}}}\simeq\Per_{G}^{\operatorname{na{\ddot{\imath}}ve}}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell}).

(3)

There are equivariant pullback and equivariant exceptional pullback functors
h^{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
and
h^{!}:D_{G}^{b}(Y)\to D_{G}^{b}(X)
as well as equivariant pushforward and equivariant proper pushforward functors
Rh_{\ast}:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(Y)
and
Rh_{!}:D_{G}^{b}(X)\to D_{G}^{b}(Y)
.

(4)

There are adjunctions
h^{\ast}\dashv Rh_{\ast}
and
Rh_{!}\dashv h^{!}
.

(5)

For any
G
-equivariant open inclusion
j:U\to X
and corresponding
G
-equivariant closed inclusion
i:V\to X
there are open/closed distinguished triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{\ast}\left(i^{\ast}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Rj_{!}\left(j^{!}(-)\right)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{j}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{-}^{i}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ri_{!}\left(i^{!}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{D^{b}_{G}(X)}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Rj_{\ast}\left(j^{\ast}(-)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Ri_{!}\left(i^{!}(-)\right)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon^{i}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{j}_{-}}

in
D_{G}^{b}(X)
.

(6)

The standard and perverse
t
-structure cohomology functors on
D_{G}^{b}(X)
commute up to isomorphism with the forgetful functors.

(7)

The six functors
(-)\otimes(-),

R[-,-]
,
h^{\ast}
,
Rh_{\ast}
,
Rh_{!}
, and
h^{!}
all commute up to isomorphism with the corresponding forgetful functors.

Proof.

As before, we prove the different aspects of the theorem in the order listed in the statement of the theorem.

(1)

This is Corollary
4.1.22
.

(2)

The triangulation of
D_{G}^{b}(X)
is Corollary
5.1.13
. The standard
t
-structure is given in

, Proposition 2.5.3]
and the perverse
t
-structure is given similarly to Corollary

.

(3)

4.2.8
,
4.2.14
, and
4.2.15
.

(4)

The adjucntions listed are given in Corollaries

and
4.2.15
.

(5)

The existence of the open/closed distinguished triangle is Corollary
8.1.11
.

(6)

The commutativity of the cohomology functors with the forgetful functors, up to isomorphism, is described in Proposition

.

(7)
