.

∎

8.2.
Equivariant Stalks and Trace

We now change gears to the final topic of the monograph: that of equivariant traces and stalks. While we have discussed some of the general motivation for having a theory of traces and stalks already, let us say some words regarding motivation from the Langlands Programme for
p
-adic groups.

In
[
11
]
the authors studied
p
-adic groups using microlocal geometry and the equivariant derived category of
\ell
-adic sheaves by extending the microlocal analytic approach of
[
2
]
(which was used in the Archimedian setting) to
p
-adic groups. In doing so they developed and gave a systematic study of what they called ABV packets and how those packets gave a geometric approach towards studying the representations (and in particular the Arthur packets) of a
p
-adic group
G
.

Recall that a
p
-adic group
G
is an algebraic group
G
defined over a
p
-adic field
F
, i.e.,
G
is a group object in
\Var_{/F}
for
F/\Q_{p}
a finite degree (algebraic) extension of some field
\Q_{p}
of
p
-adic numbers where
p
is an integer prime. Fix an integer prime
\ell
coprime to
p
. In
[
11
, Section 8.3, Conjecture 1]
the authors give a conjecture which relates the geometry of either a special orthogonal
p
-adic group
G
or a quasisiplit symplectic
p
-adic group to the representation theoretic aspects of
G
(in particular by comparing the pure Arthur packets to the ABV packets) in a precise and careful way. This conjecture, following
[
11
, Equation 8.8]
, is equivalent to verifying the “fixed point formula” for every perverse sheaf
\Pcal\in\Per_{H_{\lambda}}(V_{\lambda};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})

\langle\eta_{\psi,s},\Pcal\rangle=(-1)^{\dim C_{\psi}}\trace_{a_{s}}\left(\mathsf{NEvs}_{\psi}\Pcal\right)

where the pairing on the left takes place in the Grothendieck group
\mathsf{K}\Per_{H_{\lambda}}(V_{\lambda};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and the trace operations are calculated in the category
D^{b}_{H_{\lambda}}(V_{\lambda};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
. Note that we have left the meaning of
\lambda,

\psi
,
H_{\lambda}
,
V_{\lambda}
,
s
,
a_{s}
,
C_{\psi}
,
\eta_{\psi,s}
, and
\mathsf{NEvs}
purposely vague; the important thing here is that these representation-theoretic conjectures ultimately reduce to performing a calculation in the equivariant derived category
D_{H_{\lambda}}^{b}(V_{\lambda};\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
involving traces and functorial constructions. Consequently, in order to study this conjecture in detail from a categorical geometric perspective requires a more in depth understanding of trace operators in the categories
D_{G}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
and, more generally, in the pseudocone categories
\PC(F)
for a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
(F,\overline{F})
on
X
.

We begin our stalk and trace journey by discussing how to define equivariant stalks. While our first focus will be on extending the definition of stalks of geometric points to the equivariant situation, we will at least sketch how to define the stalks of topological points to the equivariant situation as this will be relevant for both the geometric and topological cases. First, however, let us recall the definition of a geometric point of a
K
-variety
X
.

Definition 8.2.1
.

Let
K
be a field with a fixed separable closure
K^{\operatorname{sep}}
and let
X
be a
K
-variety. A geometric point of
X
is a morphism
x:\Spec K^{\operatorname{sep}}\to X
.

Definition 8.2.2
.

Let
K
be a field and let
X
be a
K
-variety with a geometric point
x:\Spec K^{\operatorname{sep}}\to X
. An étale neighbourhood of
x
is a pair
(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}X,u)
where
\varphi
is an étale morphism and
u
is a geometric point
u:\Spec K^{\operatorname{sep}}\to U
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec K^{\operatorname{sep}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commute.

Remark 8.2.3
.

By abuse of notation we will often write an étale neighbourhood of a geometric point
x
as
(U,u)
instead of
(U\xrightarrow{\varphi}X,u)
.

Lemma 8.2.4
.

Let
x:\Spec K^{\operatorname{sep}}\to X
be a geometric point of
X
with an étale neighbourhood
(U,u)
of
x
. Then if
G
is a smooth algebraic group and
U
and
X
are left
G
-varieties for which
\varphi
is
G
-equivariant then for any pseudofunctor
\overline{F}:\Var_{/K}^{\op}\to\fCat
there is a pseudocone translation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(X)^{\op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fCat}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SfResl_{G}(U)^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo_{G\backslash(-)}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\circ\quo_{G\backslash(-)}^{\op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\square}}

where
\gamma
is the isomorphism of Remark
2.1.7
.

Proof.

The assumptions on
U
being equivariant mean that the quotient varieties
G\backslash(\Gamma\times U)=\quo_{G\backslash(\Gamma\times U)}
, and hence the quotient functor, are defined for every
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and make the cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma^{\prime}\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\times\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(f\times\id_{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(f\times\id_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}

commute. The result now follows from a routine application of the proof of Theorem
4.2.6
.
∎

Before we proceed to discuss the stalks of at geometric points we need to verify that if
\varphi:U\to X
is étale then so is
G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi):\,_{\Gamma}{U}\to\,_{\Gamma}{X}
for every
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
, as otherwise the evaluation
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}(\,_{\Gamma}{U})
we anticipate need not be valid.

Lemma 8.2.5
.

if
\varphi:U\to X
is étale and a
G
-equivariant morphism then
G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi):\,_{\Gamma}{U}\to\,_{\Gamma}{X}
is étale for every
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
.

Proof.

Recall that by construction we have a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\Gamma\times X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quo_{\Gamma\times X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi)}

for every
\Gamma
in
\Sf(G)
with
\quo_{\Gamma\times U}
smooth and surjective and
\quo_{\Gamma\times X}\circ(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi)
smooth. It then follows by Lemma
6.1.2
. Since
G\backslash(\id_{\Gamma}\times\varphi)
is smooth, it suffices to verify that it is of relative dimension zero to conclude that it is étale. However, this follows from the fact that
\Gamma
is an étale locally trivializable
G
-fibration, the identity is zero-dimensional, and the fact that
\varphi
is of relative dimension zero.
∎

These pseudonatural transformations may then be constructed for every morphism between étale neighbourhoods of a geometric point. This allows us us to consider a filtered colimit of the form

\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of the geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}(\,_{\Gamma}{U})

for every
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
and every object
A
in some equivariant category
F_{G}(X)
provided that the object
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
can be “evaluated” at the scheme
\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}
and the colimit displayed exists; this makes sense, for instance, if
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
is an étale sheaf or a (derived) complex of étale sheaves or even a (derived) complex of (pre)sheaves on
\Sch_{/\XGamma}
, if
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
is a pseudofunctor, or some other such similar situation; cf. Definitions
8.2.7
and
8.2.8
for a more precise notion of what we mean by evaluating at an (étale) open and Remark
8.2.9
for a discussion of some inherent imprecision in this notion.

This consideration highlights the two main issues which arise in our search for stalks of an equivariant object:

(1)

Whatever categories the objects
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
arise in, we at least need to ensure that the colimits

\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}(\,_{\Gamma}{U})

make sense to discuss in order to hope to have a notion of a stalk at
x
.

(2)

We have a large amount of choice as to
which
smooth free
G
-fibration
\Gamma
we use when considering the stalks; is there one fibration
\Gamma
we
should
choose over the others?

The first issue is a technical issue. Stalks only exist, even in the classical sheaf-theoretic sense, when the underlying sheaf category considers sheaves with values in a categroy
\Ascr
which admits certain types of colimits
1
1

1

We leave this purposefully vague; the type of colimits we ask
\Ascr
to satisfy changes based on site-theoretic considerations on the domain of the sheaves. For instance, if we are considering sheaves on a topological space it suffices to ask for
\Ascr
to admit filtered colimits in order to have a notion of stalks.
. We can axiomatize the properties required of
\Ascr
in order to have a notion of stalks which is sufficient for our site-theoretic needs; in this case, in terms of the étale topology or, later on, in terms of the open topology.

The second issue is more subtle and requires some choices. There are a few different ways we can handle the issue of having to choose the
G
-fibration
\Gamma
which is “most natural” for defining and considering stalks:

(1)

Avoid the choice altogether and work with the family of stalks

A_{x}=\left\{\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}\right)\;:\;\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}\right\}

all at the same time.

(2)

Wrap up the choices into one object by taking a colimit

A_{x}=\colim_{\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}}\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}\right)\right).

(3)

Work with a specific
\Gamma_{0}
chosen for a reason and define the stalk to be given as

A_{x}=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{\Gamma_{0}}{A}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma_{0}}{U}\right).

Each of these approaches has its merits and downsides. The first is an approach is consistent with our approach towards equivariance in the sense that it gives us a stalk for each resolution
\Gamma\times U
and then collects the stalks together. As a downside, however, the first approach makes it difficult to produce just one object and forces us to still choose a way of producing a stalk at the end. The second approach is a good way of producing a single object because it gives a canonical “one stalk” out of all potential stalks and smooths out the geometry of the resolutions and how the object
A
records said geometry. However, the way the second definition relates to conventional stalks and the forgetful functor is unclear and depending on the categories in which the objects
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
reside, it is unclear that the colimit indexed by
\Sf(G)_{0}
would always exist. The third approach has the benefit that it compares readily with the forgetful functor and also does not ask much formally in order to be defined. However, there had better be a good reason or justification in choosing just one variety
\Gamma_{0}
in order to define
the
stalk at
x
to be given as just the
\,{}_{\Gamma_{0}}{A}
stalk at
x
.

We choose the third approach for defining equivariant stalks and leave examination and study of the two remaining approaches for future work. Our justification comes from the following desideratum:

Desideratum 8.2.6
.

Let
F
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
and let
x
be a geometric point of
X
. Any equivariant stalk of an object
A
in
F_{G}(X)
, if it exists, should satisfy

\left(\Forget(A)\right)_{x}\cong\Forget(A_{x}).

Consequently we are obliged to choose
\Gamma_{0}:=G
as our variety and then define what we mean by evaluating the objects of
F
at étale opens before we can define the equivariant stalk at
x
. We also must do the same for topological points (both in the geometric and topological cases), but this is deferred to Definitions
8.2.14
,
8.2.15
, and
8.2.17
.

Definition 8.2.7
.

Let
X
,
G
, and
F
be given as in either of the two cases below:

(1)

G
is a topological group,
X
is a left
G
-space, and
(F,\overline{F})=F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
;

(2)

G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
is a left
G
-variety, and
(F,\overline{F})=F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
.

Then in either case we say that the objects of
F
can be evaluated at open subspaces of
X
if for every object
A
of
F(\,_{G}{X})
and for every open subobject
U
of
\,{}_{G}{X}
, there is an assignment
A(U)
taking values (pseudo)functorially in
\Open(\,_{G}{X})^{\op}
in some category
\Cscr
.

Definition 8.2.8
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group,
X
a left
G
-variety, and
(F,\overline{F})=F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. Then we say that the objects of
F
can be evaluated at étale opens of
X
if for every object
A
of
F(\,_{G}{X})
and for every scheme
U
of
\operatorname{\acute{E}t}(\,_{G}{X})
, there is an assignment
A(U)
taking values functorially in
\operatorname{\acute{E}t}(X)^{\op}
in some category
\Cscr
.

Remark 8.2.9
.

We have been somewhat imprecise in our definition of what it means to evaluate an object at an open of
X
. The definitions given are sufficiently general that we can capture the primary examples of interest to us: sheaves on a space/scheme
X
in the open topology, quasi-coherent sheaves on a scheme, sheaves on a scheme in the étale topology,
\ell
-adic sheaves on a scheme, and each of the corresponding chain complex and derived categories. However, because these categories need not be a sheaf category, presheaf category, or even a derived category of a (pre)sheaf category
2
2

2

Most distressingly this is seen with both the categories of
\ell
-adic sheaves and the
\ell
-adic derived category; infamously
D_{c}^{b}(X;\overline{\Q}_{\ell})
arises as a pseudolimit of a construction which itself is based on the Ore localization of a subcategory of
[\Nbb^{\op},\mathbf{TorShv}(X)]
; cf.
[
27
, Exposé V, VI]
or
[
19
]
for explicit details.
we have left the definition of what it means to evaluate at this level so as to apply to at least the examples listed above.

Remark 8.2.10
.

Perhaps a better name for the structure described in Definitions
8.2.7
and
8.2.8
would be to say that
F
may be globally evaluated at (étale) opens of
X
and instead leave the definition of evaluation at (étale) opens without a modifier to mean that for all
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
(or for all
\Gamma\in\Resl_{G}(X)_{0}
in the topological space case) that the evaluations
A(U)
are defined for all objects
A
of
F(\XGamma)
and for all opens (respectively étale opens)
U
of
\XGamma
(or for
G\backslash\Gamma
in the topological case) of the relevant type. In either case, both situations can be readily adapted to other Grothendieck topologies over each object
\XGamma
, but for the scope of this work it suffices to focus on both the open and étale topologies as well as the case where the objects
A
of
F(\,_{G}{X})
may be evaluated at the opens (in whichever relevant topology) of
\,{}_{G}{X}
.

Definition 8.2.11
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety with a geometric point
x
. If
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and for which each object
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
may be evaluated at étale schemes over
\,{}_{G}{X}
. We then define the equivariant stalk of an object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
at
x
by

A_{x}:=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right).

We now give two short sanity check results. The first shows that our choice to index the colimit defining the stalk by (étale) opens of
X
does not pose a problem while the second that this definition indeed satisfies Desideratum
8.2.6
.

Proposition 8.2.12
.

For any smooth algebraic group
G
and any left
G
-variety
X
there is an equivalence of categories
\operatorname{\acute{E}t}(X)\simeq\operatorname{\acute{E}t}(\,_{G}{X})
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the isomorphism of varieties
X\cong\,_{G}{X}
.
∎

Lemma 8.2.13
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety with a geometric point
x
. If
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
F(\XGamma)
admits filtered colimits for all objects
\Gamma\times X
in
\SfResl_{G}(X)
and for which each object
\,{}_{\Gamma}{A}
may be evaluated at étale schemes over
\XGamma
. Then for any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
,
A
satisfies Desideratum
8.2.6
:

\Forget(A_{x})\cong\left(\Forget(A)\right)_{x}.

Proof.

Recall that
\psi:\,_{G}{X}\to X
is the non-equivariant isomorphism of varieties furnishing the existence of the forgetful functor
\Forget:F_{G}(X)\to\overline{F}(X)
. Then we calculate on one hand

\Forget(A)_{X}=\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)_{x}=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)(U)

while on the other hand

\displaystyle\Forget(A_{x})

\displaystyle=\Forget\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right)

\displaystyle=\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right)

Now observe that since
\psi
is an isomorphism,
\overline{F}(\psi)
is an equivalence of categories and hence is cocontinuous. Consequnetly there is an isomorphism

\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right)\cong\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right).

Note that there are non-equivariant isomorphisms
\psi_{U}:\,_{G}{U}\to U
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{X}}

commutes for every étale map
\varphi:U\to X
. This, combined with the pseudofunctoriality of
\overline{F}
, ensures that there is a natural isomorphism

\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right)\cong\left(\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)(\right)U)

in
\text{{\'{E}}t}(X)
and hence gives rise to an isomorphism

\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}(\,_{G}{U})\right)\cong\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(U,u)\,\text{{\'{e}}tale neighbourhood}\\
\text{of geometric point}\,x\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{F}(\psi)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)(U).

Putting these together gives the isomorphism

\Forget(A_{x})\cong\Forget(A)_{x}

and hence proves the lemma.
∎

The benefit of this perspective is that we can give the definition of a topological stalk of
X
mutatis mutandis and also derive that this notion of an equivariant topological stalk also commutes with the forgetful functor. We record both these below after recalling the definitions of topological points both for topological spaces and for schemes.

Definition 8.2.14
.

Let
X
be a topological space. A (topological) point of
X
is a map
x:\{\ast\}\to X
.

Definition 8.2.15
.

Let
X
be a scheme. A topological point of
X
is a map
x:\Spec k\to X
where
k\cong\kappa(x)=\Ocal_{X,x}/\mfrak_{X,x}
for some
x\in\lvert X\rvert
.

Remark 8.2.16
.

While Definition
8.2.14
seems like it would be best phrased in terms of saying a point is an element
x\in X
, we want to think of points in terms of maps. Because the category of topological spaces is reasonably well-behaved the underlying set of a space
X
may be recovered by
X\cong\Top(\{\ast\},X)
, i.e., the terminal object of
X
co-represents the identity functor on
\Top
.

Alternatively, writing the elements
x\in\lvert X\rvert
when
X
is a scheme cannot be done directly. As such, we need to exploit the fact that schemes
X
are locally ringed spaces. Because each local ring
\Ocal_{X,x}
of
X
admits a residue field
\kappa(x):=\Ocal_{X,x}/\mfrak_{X,x}
we get that there is a scheme map
\Spec\kappa(x)\to X
given by
x\mapsto x
locally and defining the sheaf map
\Ocal_{X}\to i^{\ast}\Ocal_{\kappa(x)}
by noting for every open
U\subseteq\lvert X\rvert
, if
x\in U
then there is a canonical map given by the composition

\Ocal_{X}(U)\to\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq X\,\text{open}\\
x\in U\end{subarray}}\Ocal_{X}(U)\to\kappa(x)

and if
x\notin U
then
i_{\ast}\Ocal_{\kappa(x)}(U)=\Ocal_{\kappa(x)}(i^{-1}(U))=0
so we simply use the zero map in this case.

Definition 8.2.17
.

Let
G
be a topological group and let
X
be a topological space. If
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor for which each category
\overline{F}(G\backslash(G\times X))
admits filtered colimits objects of
F(G\backslash(G\times X))
may be evaluated on opens of
X
, then if
x\in X
is any point of
X
we define the equivariant stalk of an object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
to be given by

A_{x}:=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq X\,\text{open}\\
x\in U\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right).

Definition 8.2.18
.

Let
G
be a smooth algebraic group and let
X
be a left
G
-variety with a topological point
x
. If
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
F(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and objects can be evaluated at topological points of
X
then we define the equivariant stalk of an object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
at
x
by

A_{x}:=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq X\,\text{open}\\
x\in U\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right).

Remark 8.2.19
.

In both cases above note that the definitions type because
\,{}_{G}{U}
is open by Lemmas
8.1.3
and
8.1.4
.

Lemma 8.2.20
.

Let
G,X,

x
, and
F
be either of the following:

(1)

G
is a topological group,
X
is a left
G
-space,
x:\{\ast\}\to X
is a point, and
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor such that the category
\overline{F}(G\backslash(G\times X))
admits filtered colimits and for which each object
A
of
\overline{F}(G\backslash(G\times X))
can be evaluated at opens of
G\backslash(G\times X)
.

(2)

G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
is a left
G
-variety,
x
is a topological point of
X
, and
F
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor such that the category
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and for which each object
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
can be evaluated at the opens
\,{}_{\Gamma}{U}
for
U
open in
X
.

Then in either case for any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
,
A
satisfies Desideratum
8.2.6
:

\Forget(A_{x})\cong\left(\Forget(A)\right)_{x}.

Proof.

This follows mutatis mutandis to the proof of Lemma
8.2.13
and uses the fact that the non-equivariant isomorphisms
\psi
are isomorphisms in the exact same way.
∎

We now introduce some terminology to describe the pre-equivariant pseudofunctors for which we can take equivariant stalks as well as those which give rise to stalks which behave functorially over the category of
G
-objects.

Definition 8.2.21
.

Let
G
,
X
, and
(F,\overline{F})
satisfy any of the cases below:

(1)

G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
is a left
G
-variety, and
(F,\overline{F})
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
such that the category
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and each object
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
can be evaluated on étale schemes
V\to\,_{G}{X}
.

(2)

G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
is a left
G
-variety, and
(F,\overline{F})
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
such that the category
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and each object
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
can be evaluated on open subschemes
U\to\,_{G}{X}
.

(3)

G
is a topological group,
X
is a left
G
-space, and
(F,\overline{F})
is a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
such that the category
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
admits filtered colimits and each object
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
can be evaluated on open subspaces
U\subseteq\,_{G}{X}
.

Then in Case (1) we say that
(F,\overline{F})
is an étale weakly herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor and in Cases (2) and (3)
(F,\overline{F})
is a weakly topological herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor.

Finally, if
h:X\to Y
is any
G
-equivariant morphism, if
(F,\overline{F})
is a weakly herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor, and if
(\,_{Y}{F},\overline{F})
is the induced pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
Y
with
h^{\ast}:F_{G}(Y)\to F_{G}(X)
the pullback functor induced by Theorem
4.2.6
satisfies the fact that for all relevant points
x
of
X
there is an isomorphism

\left(h^{\ast}A\right)_{x}\cong A_{h(x)},

we say that
(F,\overline{F})
is an herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
3
3

3

The notation
h(x)
means, in the geometric point case, the map
\Spec k^{\operatorname{sep}}\xrightarrow{x}X\xrightarrow{h}Y
.
.

Remark 8.2.22
.

The conditions given for a weakly (étale or topological) herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor mean that for every object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
and for any relevant type of point
x
of
X
, the stalk
A_{x}
is well-defined. The additional criterion required for
(F,\overline{F})
to be a herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor asks that the stalks vary continuously in the varieties
X
and
Y
.

Example 8.2.23
.

Some standard examples of étale herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctors are the equivariant derived
\ell
-adic category pre-equivariant pseudofunctor, the étale sheaf pre-equivariant pseudofunctor, and the étale
D
-module pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
. Some topological herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctors may be defined similarly save for making sure that we take topological sheaves instead of étale sheaves in each case.

Now that we have a class of pre-equivariant pseudofunctors with which we can take stalks, we are led to ask what of the equivariant trace? This requires us to tackle understanding two distinct but related aspects of what goes into defining our desired trace object

\trace_{g}(A,x)

for an element
g\in G
, some specific point
x\in X
, and an object
A\in F_{G}(X)
.

To this end we will first explore the aspect of defining the trace which can be done for
any
herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor. Recall that Theorem
6.2.14
gives the existence of an equivariance (cf. Definition
6.2.21
)

\Theta:\alpha_{X}^{\ast}\xRightarrow{\cong}\pi_{2}^{\ast}

where
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}
and
\pi_{2}^{\ast}
are the pullbacks against the action map and the projection given by an application of Theorem
4.2.6
in the case of
\pi_{2}
and Lemma
6.2.7
in the case of
\alpha_{X}
. To each object
A
we can thus associate the equivariance
\Theta^{A}
which, we will see, forms the backbone of our trace formalism. First, however, in order to have a well-behaved notion of trace we need to understand the interaction between stalks and pullback functors.

Proposition 8.2.24
.

Let
(F,\overline{F})
be a weakly herbaceous pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
\overline{F}
is strict. Then for any point
g
of
G
and any point
x
of
X
we have that

\left((\id_{G}\times h)^{\ast}\Theta^{A}_{Y}\right)_{(g,x)}=\left(\Theta_{X}^{h^{\ast}A}\right)_{(g,x)}.

Proof.

Since
\overline{F}
is a strict pseudofunctor, we have an equality

(\id_{G}\times h)^{\ast}\Theta_{Y}^{A}=\Theta_{X}^{h^{\ast}A}

for every object
A
of
F_{G}(Y)
by Corollary
6.2.23
.
∎

With this above technical detail established we can now define and describe the equivariant trace. We simply need to define the kinds of pre-equivariant pseudofunctors for which we can even hope to define equivariant trace before establishing the basic properties which it satisfies. To do this carefully we simply recall what it means to be fully dualizable in a symmetric monoidal category before defining trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctors.

Definition 8.2.25
.

Let
(\Cscr,\otimes,I,\lambda,\rho,\alpha,\gamma)
be a symmetric monoidal category. We say that an object
X
in
\Cscr
is dualizable if
X
admits a right adjoint
X^{\ast}
in the delooping bicategory
\underline{B}\Cscr
. More explicitly,
X
is dualizable if there is an object
X^{\ast}
and morphisms
\eta_{X}:I\to X^{\ast}\otimes X
and
\varepsilon_{X}:X\otimes X^{\ast}\to I
for which the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes(X^{\ast}\otimes X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\otimes X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes X^{\ast})\otimes X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X}\otimes\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{X}\circ\rho_{X}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X,X^{\ast},X}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\id_{X}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\otimes X^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\ast}\otimes X)\otimes X^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\ast}\otimes I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\ast}\otimes(X\otimes X^{\ast})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X}\otimes\id_{X^{\ast}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X^{\ast}}\circ\lambda_{X^{\ast}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X^{\ast},X,X^{\ast}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X^{\ast}}\circ\varepsilon_{X}}

commute. The subcategory of
\Cscr
generated by the dualizable objects is denoted
\Cscr_{\operatorname{f.d.}}
.

There is, of course, a well-established and well-studied notion of fully dualizable objects which is important in representation theory and the theory of TQFTs. We will not use this more general higher-categorical notion in this monograph but instead refer the reader to
[
17
, Appendix A]
and
[
37
]
. The connection to the
1
-categorical notion used in this monograph lies in the fact that every
1
-morphism of a symmetric monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathsf{C}
is dualizable in the sense that every
1
-morphism in the homotopy bicategory
h_{2}^{(0)}\mathsf{C}
admits a left/right adjoint and a
1
-morphism in the
homotopy bicategory
h_{2}^{(-1)}\mathsf{C}
has left/right adjoints if and only if the corresponding object in
h\mathsf{C}
is dualizable in the sense of Definition
8.2.25
.

Definition 8.2.26
.

Let
F=(F,\overline{F})
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on a left
G
-variety
X
. We say that
F
is a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor if:

•

F
is herbaceous;

•

The categories
\overline{F}(X)
and
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
have objects which may be evaluated on opens of
X
for which each object
A
takes values in a category
\Cscr_{\operatorname{f.d.}}
, where
\Cscr_{\operatorname{f.d.}}
is the subcategory of fully dualizable objects in a symmetric monoidal category
\Cscr
.

Example 8.2.27
.

The pre-equivariant pseudofunctors
\Shv(-;\Z_{\ell}\mathbf{PrMod}_{\operatorname{f.g.}})
of finitely generated projective
\ell
-adic sheaves and
\mathnormal{D}_{\mathnormal{c}}^{\mathnormal{b}}({-};\overline{\Q}_{\ell}),
the bounded derived category of
\overline{\Q}_{\ell}
-sheaves are trace-class. Additional examples can be found in the categories
\Shv(-;\mathbf{Vect}_{\operatorname{f.d.}})
of sheaves of finite-dimensional vector spaces and bounded derived complexes of perfect chain complexes
D_{c}^{b}(-)_{\operatorname{perf}}
. A more silly example can be found by taking the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor
F
induced by taking
\overline{F}
to be the constant pseudofunctor on the terminal category
\One
.

Example 8.2.28
.

For a non-example of a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor let
F
be the pre-equivariant pseudofunctor induced by the pseudofunctor
\overline{F}
defined by object assignments
\overline{F}(Z):=\KVect
for a field
K
and taking translation functors
\overline{F}(f)
to be given by the identity functor on
\KVect
. Then
F
is not trace-class because the category of fully dualizable objects in
\KVect
,
\KVect_{\operatorname{f.d}}
, is the category of finite dimensional vectors

Proposition 8.2.29
.

If
F
is a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor then for any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
, any endomorphism
f:A\to A
, and any point
x
of
X
the stalks
A_{x}
and
f_{x}
admit traces.

Proof.

This is immediate from the definitions with
\trace(A_{x})
being given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ibb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}^{\ast}\otimes A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}\otimes A_{x}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ibb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A_{x}^{\ast},A_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A_{x}}}

and
\trace(f_{x})
given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ibb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}^{\ast}\otimes A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}^{\ast}\otimes A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}\otimes A_{x}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ibb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{A_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A_{x}^{\ast}}\otimes f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{A_{x}^{\ast},A_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A_{x}}}

where
\Ibb
is the monoidal unit of
\overline{F}(X)
and
\gamma_{B,C}:B\otimes C\to C\otimes B
is the braiding map.
∎

We now are in position to define the trace of an equivariant category. Let
F
be a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor and let
g
be a point of
G
and
x
be a point of
X
for which
x
is fixed by
g
, i.e.,
gx=x
. Then on one hand by virtue of
F
being herbaceous there is an isomorphism of stalks

\pi_{2}^{\ast}(A)_{(g,x)}\cong A_{\pi_{2}(g,x)}=A_{x}

while on the other hand

\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(A)_{(g,x)}\cong A_{\alpha_{X}(g,x)}=A_{gx}=A_{x}.

Consequently the map

\,{}_{G}{\theta}_{(g,x)}^{A}:A_{x}\to A_{x}

gives an automorphism of
A_{x}
which we use to define the equivariant trace of
A
at the point
(g,x)
.

Definition 8.2.30
.

Let
F=(F,\overline{F})
be a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
and let
g
be a point of
G
and
x
a point of
X
fixed by
g
. Then if
A\in F_{G}(X)_{0}
we define the trace at
g
and
x
of
A
to be given by

\trace_{g}(A,x):=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta_{(g,x)}^{A}}\right).

Our first goal is to prove that when
F
is a triangulated trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor with right exact translation functors, trace is additive on distinguished triangles in the sense that if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

is a distinguished triangle in
F_{G}(X)
then

\trace_{g}(B,x)=\trace_{g}(A,x)+\trace_{g}(C,x).

To go about this proof we will need one technical lemma that allows us to see, in the presence of a regular epimorphism
e:B\to C
, the equivariance
\Theta^{C}
is functionally determined by the “quotient” of
\Theta^{B}
by
e
. We will also collect some folkloric facts about triangulated categories which are well-known to experts solely for the sake of completeness.

Lemma 8.2.31
.

Let
F
be a pre-equivariant pseudofunctor for which the functor
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
is right exact. Then for any regular epimorphism
e:B\to C
in
F_{G}(X)
, the equivariance
\Theta^{C}
is a quotient of
\Theta^{B}
in the sense that it is induced by
\Theta^{B}
and the universal property of an epimorphism.

Proof.

Because we have that
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}e
is a regular epimorphism by assumption and because the equivariances
\Theta
are natural in
F_{G}(X)
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}e}

commutes with
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}e
a regular epimorphism. Then it follows that
\Theta^{C}
is the unique map making

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}e\circ\Theta^{B}}

commute, which in turn proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 8.2.32

(
[
57
]
)
.

Let
\Cscr
be a triangulated category (cf. Definition
5.1.7
). Then
\Cscr
is a semisimple category, i.e., every epic and monic split in
\Cscr
.

Proof.

We present the argument for monics, as the example for epics follows dually. Fix a monic
\mu:A\to B
and complete it to a distinguished triangle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Rotating the triangle to the left gives a distinguished triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C[-1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi[-1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

which, because adjacent morphisms in a triangle compose to zero give that
\mu\circ\psi[-1]=0=\mu\circ 0
and hence show that
\psi=0[1]=0
. Thus it follows that the triangle is isomorphic to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\oplus C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

by
[
48
, Corollary 1.2.7]
, giving that
\mu
splits.
∎

Lemma 8.2.33
.

If
\Cscr
and
\Dscr
are triangulated categories then any additive functor
F:\Cscr\to\Dscr
is exact.

Proof.

Because additive functors of additive categories being exact is equivalent to preserving monics and epics, and because every epic and monic in
\Cscr
splits by Lemma
8.2.32
, the result follows because every functor preserves split epics and split monics.
∎

Theorem 8.2.34
.

Let
F
be a triangulated trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
and
G\times X
for which:

•

The category
\Cscr
of values the objects
A
of
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{X})
,
\overline{F}(X)
,
\overline{F}(\,_{G}{(G\times X)})
and
\overline{F}(G\times X)
take is triangulated and arises as the “derived category” of a “homotopy category” in the sense of
[
43
, Section 5]
.

•

The operation of taking stalks gives triangulated exact functors
(-)_{(g,x)}:F_{G}(G\times X)\to\Cscr
and
(-)_{x}:F_{G}(X)\to\Cscr
.

•

Each functor

\overline{F}\left(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}\right):\overline{F}(\XGamma)\to\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\right)

(cf. the proof of Lemma
6.2.7
) is additive.

Then equivariant trace is additive on distinguished triangles in the sense that if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

is a distinguished triangle for objects
A,B,C
in
F_{G}(X)
then

\trace_{g}(B,x)=\trace_{g}(A,x)+\trace_{g}(C,x).

Proof.

Note that the assumption that each functor
\overline{F}\left(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}}\right)
is exact implies, because
\overline{F}(\XGamma)
and
\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)})
are triangulated, that each corresponding functor is exact by Lemma
8.2.33
and hence so is
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
by Corollary
4.2.10
. The functor
\pi_{2}^{\ast}:F_{G}(X)\to F_{G}(G\times X)
and the functors
\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}:\overline{F}(\XGamma)\to\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{\Gamma}{(G\times X)}\right)
are exact because
F
is a triangulated pre-equivariant pseudofunctor and hence are additive and exact for similar reasons.

Assume that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

is a distinguished triangle in
F_{G}(X)
. Applying the functors
\pi_{2}^{\ast}
and
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}
gives us that the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(A)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}\Psi}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(A)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}\Psi}

are distinguished in
F_{G}(G\times X)
. The naturality of
\Theta
proved in Theorem
6.2.14
gives rise to a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(A)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(A)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{A[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}\Psi}

in
F_{G}(G\times X)
. Taking the
(G\times X)
-component of the diagram above and using the definition of being distinguished in
F_{G}(G\times X)
(cf. Theorem
5.1.10
) produces the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{C}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{C}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\rho}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\rho}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\varphi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\theta^{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\varphi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\theta^{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\psi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\psi}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A[1]}}}

for which both columns are distinguished triangles. Now because
\overline{F}
is triangulated, the equivalence of categories
\overline{F}(\psi_{G\times X}):\overline{F}(\,_{G}{(G\times X)})\to\overline{F}(G\times X)
is triangulated and hence sends distinguished triangles to distinguished triangles. As such, applying the functor
\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)
gives the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{B}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{C}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{C}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\rho}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\rho}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\varphi}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{B}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\varphi}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{C}}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\alpha}_{X}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\psi}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\pi}_{2}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\psi}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\psi}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A[1]}}\right)}

with each column a distinguished triangle. Taking stalks of the diagram at the pair
(g,x)
where
g
is a point of
G
and
x
is a
g
-fixed point of
X
then gives a commuting diagram which is equal to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(A)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(B)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(C)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(A)[1]_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(A)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(B)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(C)_{(g,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(A)[1]_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(P)_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(\Phi)_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}^{\ast}(\Psi)_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(P)_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(\Phi)_{(g,x)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\ast}(\Psi)_{(g,x)}}

where each row is a distinguished triangle. Now by virtue of
F
being herbaceous we can rewrite this to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{x}}

in which each row is a distinguished triangle. Using now that
\Cscr
is the category of fully dualizable objects in a triangulated symmetric monoidal category allow us to invoke
[
43
, Theorem 1.9]
and produce a map
\omega
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A[1]_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{(g,x)}^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{x}}

commutes and the additivity relation

\trace\left(\Theta^{B}_{(g,x)}\right)=\trace\left(\Theta^{A}_{(g,x)}\right)+\trace(\omega)

holds. However, a reading of the proof of
[
43
, Theorem 1.9]
(explicitly
[
43
, Section 8]
) shows that
\omega
is given in essence as a homotopy class of quotients. Lemma
8.2.31
shows that the equivariances
\Theta^{-}
respect these quotient constructions and hence allows us to choose
\omega=\Theta^{C}_{(g,x)}
. In this way we get that

\displaystyle\trace_{g}(B,x)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\Theta_{(g,x)}^{B}\right)=\trace\left(\Theta_{(g,x)}^{A}\right)+\trace\left(\Theta_{(g,x)}^{C}\right)

\displaystyle=\trace_{g}(A,x)+\trace_{g}(C,x),

as was desired.
∎

Corollary 8.2.35
.

Equivariant trace is additive in any of the cases below:

•

G
is a smooth algebraic group,
X
is a left
G
-variety, and
F
is either the equivariant derived pre-equivariant pseudofunctor or the
\ell
-adic equivariant derived pre-equviariant pseudofunctor;

•

G
is a topological group which admits
n
-acyclic free
G
-spaces which are manifolds,
X
is a left
G
-space, and
F
is the equivariant derived category pre-equivariant pseudofunctor.

Proof.

The proof ultimately comes down to the fact that, in any of the cases listed, because pullback functors are right exact
4
4

4

As pullback functors are adjoints
f^{\ast}\dashv f_{\ast}
, giving that
f^{\ast}
preserves colimits because it is a left adjoint.
, each functor
(\overline{\id_{\Gamma}\times\alpha_{X}})^{\ast}
is right exact; consequently
\alpha_{X}^{\ast}
is right exact by Corollary
4.2.10
and so is exact by Lemma
8.2.33
. From here the corollary follows by a direct application of Theorem
8.2.34
.
∎

We now conclude the monograph by showing that there are two “continuity” properties of equivariant trace which apply to strict pre-equivariant pseudofunctors: first, if
h:X\to Y
is an equivariant map and if
g
is a point of
G
with
x
a point of
X
fixed by
g
, then for any object
A
of
F_{G}(Y)
,

\trace_{g}\left(h^{\ast}A,x\right)=\trace_{g}\left(A,h(x)\right).

Second, we show that if
i:X\to Y
is a
G
-equivariant immersion and if
F
admits a pushforward functor along
i
(right adjoint to pullback against
i
) with evaluation on opens calculated by

h_{\ast}A(\,_{G}{U})=A\left(X\times_{Y}\,{}_{G}{U}\right)

then for any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
there is an equality of traces

\trace_{g}(A,x)=\trace_{g}\left(i_{\ast}A,i(x)\right).

The assumptions regarding the strictness are not too harsh in practice. Many practical models of the equivariant derived category which appear in the literature (cf. those used in
[
1
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
38
]
,
[
46
]
,
[
63
, Chapters 6 – 9]
,
[
64
]
) are those induced from the corresponding strict derived category pseudofunctor (or its
\ell
-adic counterpart).

Proposition 8.2.36
.

Let
F=(F,\overline{F})
be a strict trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
and let
h:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant morphism. Then for any object
A
of
F_{G}(Y)
and any point
g
of
G
with
x
a point of
X
fixed by
g
, we have that

\trace_{g}(h^{\ast}A,x)=\trace_{g}\left(A,h(x)\right)

where
h^{\ast}
is induced from
\overline{F}
by Theorem
4.2.6
.

Proof.

Recall from Corollary
6.2.23
we have that

\Theta_{X}^{h^{\ast}A}=(\id_{G}\times h)^{\ast}\Theta_{Y}^{A}

so it follows that in this case we have that

\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta_{X}^{h^{\ast}A}}\right)_{(g,x)}=\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta_{Y}^{A}}\right)\right)_{(g,x)}.

Now, writing
(g,x)\to U
for opens
U
to mean that
U
is an open neighbourhood in the relevant (Grothendieck) topology around the point
(g,x)
, we find that

\displaystyle\trace_{g}(h^{\ast}A,x)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta_{(g,x)}^{h^{\ast}A}}\right)=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{(h^{\ast}\theta^{A}_{Y})_{(g,x)}}\right)=\trace\left(\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}^{\ast}(\,_{G}{\theta^{A}_{Y}})\right)_{(g,x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(g,x)\to W\\
W\to G\times X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)\right)(\,_{G}{W})\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,x\to W^{\prime}\\
V\to G,W^{\prime}\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{(V\times W^{\prime})}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,x\to W^{\prime}\\
V\to G,W^{\prime}\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}\right)(\,_{G}{(V\times W^{\prime})})\to\,_{G}{U^{\prime\prime}}\\
U^{\prime\prime}\to G\times Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)(\,_{G}{U^{\prime\prime}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,x\to W^{\prime}\\
V\to G,W^{\prime}\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{F}\left(\,{}_{G\times G}{\overline{h}}\right)(\,_{G}{(V\times W^{\prime})})\to\,_{G}{(V\times U^{\prime})}\\
U^{\prime}\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)(\,_{G}{(V\times U^{\prime})})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,h(x)\to U^{\prime}\\
V\to G,U^{\prime}\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}\right)(\,_{G}{(V\times U^{\prime})})\right)

\displaystyle=\trace\left(\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(g,h(x))\to U\\
U\to G\times X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}}(\,_{G}{U})\right)=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{(g,h(x))}}\right)=\trace_{g}(A,h(x))

which was what was desired.
∎

Our goal now is to show that trace preserves base points for immersions
i:X\to Y
in the sense that

\trace_{g}(A,x)=\trace_{g}\left(i_{\ast}A,i(x)\right)

in mild conditions. To go about this, we now assume that there is a right adjoint

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\ast}}
\dashv

induced by adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,{}_{\Gamma}{\overline{\imath}_{\ast}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}(\,_{\Gamma}{\overline{\imath}})}
\dashv

for all
\Gamma\in\Sf(G)_{0}
. We also assume that evaluation of the objects of
i_{\ast}A
at opens of
Y
takes the form

(i_{\ast}A)(U)=A(U\times_{Y}X).

As a first sanity check we show that for immersions
i:X\to Y
, stalks are stable under pushforward.
In this case we first note that if
A
is an object of
F_{G}(X)
then for any point
x
of
X
,

(i_{\ast}A)_{i(x)}=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(x)\to U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\imath}_{\ast}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right)=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(x)\to U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}\,{}_{G}{U}\right).

Now asking that we have a map
i(x)\to U
is equivalent to asking for a map
x\to U\times_{Y}X
, so we can rewrite the colimit as

\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(x)\to U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}\,{}_{G}{U}\right)=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to X\times_{Y}U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}\,{}_{G}{U}\right)

However, since the system of opens
\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}\,{}_{G}{U}
is cofinal in the class of opens of
\,{}_{G}{X}
because
i
is an immersion, we can further rewrite the colimit as

\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to X\times_{Y}U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}\,{}_{G}{U}\right)=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to V\\
V\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{V}\right)=A_{x}.

Putting this all together gives that

\displaystyle(i_{\ast}A)_{i(x)}

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}i(x)\to U\\
U\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\imath}_{\ast}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right)

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\to V\\
V\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{V}\right)=A_{x}

as well as our next proposition.

Proposition 8.2.37
.

Let
i:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant immersion and let
F=(F,\overline{F})
be a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
\overline{F}
is strict and for which pushforwards
i_{\ast}A
are evaluated by the equation

\left(\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\imath}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)\left(U\right)=\,_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}U\right).

Then for any point
x
of
X
and any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
,

(i_{\ast}A)_{i(x)}=A_{x}.

We can now use the techniques that appear in this derivation to establish that immersions preserve traces.

Proposition 8.2.38
.

Let
i:X\to Y
be a
G
-equivariant immersion and let
F=(F,\overline{F})
be a trace-class pre-equivariant pseudofunctor on
X
for which
\overline{F}
is strict and for which pushforwards
i_{\ast}A
are evaluated by the equation

\left(\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\imath}}\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{A}\right)\right)\left(U\right)=\,_{G}{A}\left(\,{}_{G}{X}\times_{\,{}_{G}{Y}}U\right).

Then for any point
g
of
G
and any
g
-fixed point
x
of
X
, for any object
A
of
F_{G}(X)
we have that

\,{}_{G}{\theta^{i_{\ast}A}_{(g,i(x))}}=\,_{G}{\theta^{A}_{(g,x)}}

and

\trace_{g}(i_{\ast}A,i(x))=\trace_{g}(A,x).

Proof.

Note that if we can establish the first claim the second follows immediately because

\trace_{g}(i_{\ast}A,i(x))=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{i_{\ast}A}_{(g,i(x))}}\right)=\trace\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{(g,x)}}\right)=\trace_{g}(A,x).

To establish the first claim we make the following calculation:

\,{}_{G}{\theta^{i_{\ast}A}_{(g,i(x))}}

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(g,i(x))\to U\\
U\to G\times Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta_{Y}^{i_{\ast}A}}\left(\,{}_{G}{U}\right)=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,i(x)\to W\\
V\to G,W\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta_{Y}^{i_{\ast}A}}\left(\,{}_{G}{(V\times W)}\right)

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,i(x)\to W\\
V\to G,W\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\left(\left(\,{}_{G}{\overline{\imath}}\right)_{\ast}\left(\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{X}}\right)\right)\left(\,{}_{G}{(V\times W)}\right)

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,i(x)\to W\\
V\to G,W\to Y\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{X}}\left(\,{}_{G}{\left(V\times\left(X\times_{Y}W\right)\right)}\right)

\displaystyle=\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\to V,x\to W^{\prime}\\
V\to G,W^{\prime}\to X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{X}}\left(\,{}_{G}{\left(V\times W^{\prime}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\colim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(g,x)\to U^{\prime}\\
U^{\prime}\to G\times X\,\text{open}\end{subarray}}\,{}_{G}{\theta^{A}_{X}}\left(\,{}_{G}{U^{\prime}}\right)=\,_{G}{\theta_{(g,x)}^{A}},

which proves the first claim and hence the proposition.
∎
